Kessa rotated both images until the truss bolts aligned. The wounds moved with the hidden structure, not the cage. Their spacing had widened as the layers grew. One scar passed beneath a replacement support installed eighty years after the cut that formed it.

Anu stood with both hands on the image table. “That excludes maintenance residue deposited after the support.”

“It excludes that support,” Kessa said. “Test every station before you make it carry more.”

“You have taught caution enough for one week.”

“It still has work.”

Threadbare remained under Charter evidence hold, but evidence custody had weakened after Irian placed convoy clocks and drones on the same public stream. Examiner Vaust now occupied the cockpit with a recorder of her own. She had not removed the seal from propulsion. She had stopped objecting when Kessa opened archives the warrant did not mention.

The convoy's six-hour inspection had become forty-one hours of continuous mapping because the second route closure left nobody with a useful reason to stop. Kindly Argument had shut down its damaged spin bearing after completing patient transfers. The rear group remained stranded, alive on margins Irian redistributed twice a day.

Sena called up Resonator Two. “Same positions?”

Pell aligned a fifty-two-year-old burnoff report with the drone pass. Two scars crossed the visible layers beneath a service rail. A third disappeared into the dark center, then reappeared on the far side after the lensing model corrected the image.

“Same,” he said.

At Resonator Seven, the oldest available picture showed no layers because the camera had exposed for ceramic. Anu recovered their edges from bent background stars. The lines formed a shallow fold around every cutter site.

Kessa marked each one.

A diagram assembled beside the images: nine resonators, hundreds of maintenance operations, and a wound field that repeated according to cage position. Thermal clearances cut broad patches. Cold-shear work left narrow paired lines. Emergency holds produced compressed bands around deeper structures no camera could resolve.

If the material had accumulated like frost, new growth should have buried old damage. Instead, the scars enlarged with it.

Vaust watched from the hatch. “Your sample closed a probe wound in seconds.”

“Small wounds,” Anu said. “These reach through multiple layers. Repair seals the boundary but does not restore original organization.”

“Like scar tissue.”

“Analogous to it. We do not know the chemistry well enough to claim identity.”

Kessa glanced at Anu. “Caution still has work.”

The ecologist ignored her with care.

Sena moved the station-age slider backward. “Show me the whole cage without overlays.”

Nine images arranged themselves around the dark center. Most showed frost, hardware, and failed light. Kessa had spent her working life treating such pictures as access problems. Where could a cutter fit? Which brace could take load? What material had to leave before a seized mount turned?

Now the clear layers connected across the gaps. Scars formed nine repeated clusters around something the camera never captured whole.

“Put the lens correction on,” Sena said.

Pell applied the best model from the convoy drones. Starlight straightened around the cage, then bent across the deeper absence in a broad moving contour. The visible layers narrowed toward one end and spread near the other. No reliable edge appeared, but the field suggested length, curvature, and a raised central form.

A pulse traveled through it.

Stars lifted along the curve and settled beyond it. For an instant the shape resembled a back passing beneath a dark veil.

Sena leaned closer. “Veilback.”

Anu frowned. “Do not name it from an optical artifact.”

“I named what the picture does to my eyes.”

“You named an adult body form without evidence of adulthood.”

“I didn't say adult.”

“You implied a complete animal.” Anu pointed toward the smaller bundles visible near the inspection ring. “We have tissue analogues and coordinated response from a compact developmental structure. The visible structure at Nacre remains a juvenile. A larger life stage may have a different morphology entirely.”

Sena folded her arms. “Then the juvenile belongs to something that may become a veilback.”

“You are attempting taxonomic blackmail.”

“It has been effective on pilots.”

Kessa turned the word against the image in her mind. A useful term needed enough shape to travel without claiming knowledge they lacked. Hollow had hidden the structure inside an engineering category for one hundred and sixty-eight years. Animal carried too many assumptions. Veilback described an observed effect and left anatomy unresolved.

“Log it as a field term,” she said. “Veilback for the unknown larger form. Juvenile or hollow for Nacre until evidence changes.”

Anu looked offended by the compromise, which suggested it divided the available errors fairly.

Pell added the term to their local notes.

Kessa returned to the wound map. “Nacre alone proves long-lived structure under repeated damage. It does not prove every anchor holds the same thing.”

“The cage design repeats,” Vaust said.

“So does bad procurement. We need other ages.”

Route maintenance archives held the answer behind contracts, local formats, and professional embarrassment. Kessa sent a narrow request through the Routekeepers' Union: exterior cage images, any anchor, any year, raw if possible. She did not mention biology. She asked whether their old burnoff scars moved between inspections.

The first reply came from Brine eighteen minutes later. A routekeeper named Orra Senn sent nine images and a note: Thought our registration had slipped for six years. Charter calibration says nominal.

Tern supplied a service scan from before its last refit. Pell aligned it with a recent public navigation survey. Three ridges had migrated outward through hardware while retaining their spacing.

By the third hour, images arrived from eleven anchors. Old cages showed fewer scars and wider clear layers. Newer cages displayed tight bands where resonators pressed closest. Anchor age changed the amount of visible injury, not its arrangement. Every scar corresponded to a standard maintenance or hold position.

Orra Senn opened a live union channel from Mica Hand while Brine's archive continued uploading behind her. She wore a cutter harness with one shoulder unfastened and kept glancing toward a local cage plot outside the image.

“Your request stirred up half the Reach,” Orra said. “Nobody likes discovering an old calibration problem might have been accurate.”

“Show me the earliest clean image you trust,” Kessa said.

Orra projected Brine's commissioning inspection. The cage had operated for only eleven months. Pale fibers crossed Resonator Three in a loose fan without visible ridges. A second image from year nine showed the first burnoff line. By year thirty, the cut had widened into a dark seam whose ends turned away from the resonator face.

Anu traced the changed growth. “Repair diverted around the injured area. It did not restore the original path.”

“We called that preferential deposition,” Orra said.

“Because the manual did,” Kessa replied.

“Because it let us cut the new path without asking why the old one stopped.”

The next union caller worked at Hallowglass, one of the youngest anchors in the network. Its visible layers carried only two narrow scars, both created during mandatory acceptance tests before the corridor opened. No freight had yet passed when human crews made the first wound.

At Pilgrim's Rest, age one hundred and sixty-two, the inner photographs showed so many overlapping ridges that Pell could not separate individual cuts. He used work orders to assign likely dates. Scar thickness increased after every high-strength hold, creating compressed shelves across the underlying layers.

“Maintenance damage and hold damage differ,” Kessa said. “Cuts leave directional seams. Holds crush whole bands against the cage.”

Anu added colored marks to the diagram. “Both persist. The tissue grows around them, not through them.”

A routekeeper from Pellucid-2 joined without video. “Our anchor closed after it produced the Nacre sequence. Charter control has ordered full hold until a cruiser arrives.”

“How strong?” Kessa asked.

“One hundred and twelve percent of service limit.”

“Your coolant bus will not carry that for long.”

“We said so.”

“What did they answer?”

“That continuity authority accepts material risk.”

Kessa looked at the scars gathered around Nacre's wheel. Material risk meant hardware in Charter language. The phrase had no place for tissue because the category excluded it before anyone measured harm.

“Save your local clocks outside the station network,” she said. “Do not cut any layer while the hold remains active. If they order a burn, demand a named engineer under equivalent exposure.”

“You think it can happen here?”

“I think I don't know why both juveniles moved within days. That uncertainty argues against adding force.”

The caller disconnected to copy logs.

More images arrived. Some routekeepers apologized in their covering notes, as if Kessa held authority to forgive work she had also performed. Others defended each cut with the conditions visible at the time. A fouled radiator could cook a station. A blocked service rail could prevent rescue. The wound map did not erase those dangers. It showed how an institution had solved them repeatedly by injuring the same hidden body and renaming every reaction as obstruction.

Sena sorted the notes into technical categories instead of confession. “If guilt gets its own folder, nobody will open the load data.”

“Put operator statements beside the relevant work orders,” Kessa said. “No separate absolution.”

Anu glanced between them. “You both assume the union will accept the biological finding.”

“They will accept scars that match their tools,” Sena said. “The name can arrive later.”

A diagram from an abandoned training cage showed the standard cut zones as nine red wedges. Pell laid those wedges over Nacre, Brine, Tern, Hallowglass, and Pilgrim's Rest. The pattern covered nearly every mapped ridge. What remained clustered at hold boundaries.

Kessa had wanted a comparison strong enough to survive hostile review. The result carried something harsher than a single altered report. No secret committee needed to coordinate a wound repeated across a century. Manuals had done the work. Shift training carried it. License exams rewarded the cleanest injury.

She found her own marks inside that history.

Kessa had performed some of those operations herself.

She found her work at Merrow-2 in a seven-year inspection series. Her cutter marks appeared as two clean lines near Resonator Eight. She remembered the job: a stubborn translucent obstruction wrapped around a coolant branch, the material flexing away from her first cut and fouling the tool. She had changed angle, increased field pressure, and finished ahead of schedule.

The later image showed a thick ridge grown around both cuts.

Sena saw her initials in the work record. “Kess.”

“Mark operator verified.”

“You don't have to make yourself evidence.”

“I already did the work.”

Kessa logged the cut angle, pressure, and her own certainty at the time that she had removed inert accretion. The admission did not restore anything. It prevented grief from turning the map into a story where harm belonged only to strangers.

Anu placed Merrow beside Nacre. “Different juvenile, same cage injury.”

“Likely.”

“Your caution has become decorative.”

“It is holding the word likely in place.”

Another archive packet arrived without a sender. Its wrapper used a Routekeepers' Union relay, but the internal format belonged to the Charter's old fault system. Twelve anchors. Thirty-four hold events. In each, resonator strain propagated around the cage in three measured loads before control flattened it into a single excursion.

Pell isolated the oldest event. “Lark-3.”

The name cleared space around Kessa.

She touched the torque key clipped beside the image table. The raw Nacre spool remained inside its obsolete cavity. Tomis had also stored local calibrations there, old fragments Pell had ignored while extracting the recent data. If his Lark tools had synchronized with the fault board, the key might retain more than factory tables.

“Open the cavity image,” she said.

Pell brought up the directory. Most files carried unreadable names encoded by an obsolete diagnostic suite. Kessa recognized Tomis's habits inside the disorder. He named working files after torque settings so supervisors browsing a tool would see routine numbers. A cluster labeled 42N appeared on the day Lark-3 failed.

Sena put one hand against the table. “You have used that setting since.”

“The key never overwrote old fault buffers. It added calibration blocks.”

“How much is in there?”

“Twelve years of reasons to maintain old tools,” Pell said.

Kessa opened the first block.

A routekeeper board appeared in fragments. Local time. Resonator outputs. Tomis Rho's operator credential. The public finding had summarized these minutes as escalating compensation error during an unexpected fluctuation. Kessa had read that sentence until its words lost edges.

The raw trace began with three loads.

Resonator Three sampled first. Four followed, then Five. The pattern crossed the cage according to position, just as Nacre's pulse had crossed Threadbare. Tomis reduced hold by eight percent. The structure relaxed. Automatic corridor control restored the pressure without his approval.

A text entry flashed beside his credential: UNCLASSIFIED INTERNAL MOTION. REQUEST TRAFFIC STOP.

Kessa's scarred palm flattened on the table.

The next block showed Charter control rejecting the stop because traffic occupancy exceeded the local operator threshold. Tomis tried again under evacuation authority. His message included a projected service-ring shear and ordered three work crews out.

The public finding claimed he increased compensation.

The raw command showed him reducing it a second time.

Automatic hold overrode him again.

No one in the gallery moved. The repair systems hummed behind the walls. Somewhere aft, a crew member dropped a tool and cursed, unaware that twelve years had changed shape in the forward compartment.

Pell read the checksum twice. “This came from his key at Lark. Local signatures match the station format.”

Vaust stepped into the gallery. “Let me verify.”

Kessa blocked the table with one arm. “On an independent machine.”

“You need Charter authentication.”

“I need a copy you cannot replace.”

Vaust met her gaze. “Then make three before I touch it.”

The examiner had earned that much inside Resonator Six. Kessa let Pell prepare the copies.

Anu aligned Lark's first three loads with Nacre's recorded pulse. Timing matched within the tolerance expected from different cage dimensions. The smaller tremble inside each beat appeared in both. At Lark, Tomis had labeled it moving strain source. At Nacre, the juvenile's filament reproduced it under the field forks.

His fault signature did not show operator error. It showed an animal structure responding to a distant load while automatic systems forced it to remain fixed.

Kessa read his evacuation request again.

Sena stood beside her, close enough for shoulders to touch but did not offer contact. “He saw it.”

“He saw a load path.”

“He tried to clear the crews.”

“Yes.”

The repair words held. Barely.

Vaust completed her authentication. “The command record conflicts with the official finding.”

“Say his name,” Kessa said.

The examiner looked at the credential. “Tomis Rho issued the reduction and evacuation request. Automatic control reversed him.”

Anu saved the statement with Vaust's seal.

Kessa turned away before anyone mistook stillness for permission to comfort her. The wound map covered the wall: Nacre, Brine, Tern, Merrow, Lark, and seven other anchors. Different juveniles carried the same marks. Different workers had made them under one procedure. The system had called every response drift until drift became fatal.

Irian's request indicator waited on the communication slate. The commodore needed an operational answer, not a classification. Kessa could not yet provide a safe way through Nacre. She could provide the cost of forcing it.

“Build a release packet,” she told Pell. “Raw images first. Registration method. Scar map. Sample controls. Lark command blocks. Vaust's authentication uncut.”

Vaust stiffened. “Charter evidence cannot enter a public packet during active review.”

“Then remove your seal and leave your spoken verification.”

“That does not cure disclosure.”

“It cures the part where we pretend time will wait for review.”

Examiner Vaust looked toward Nacre-9's live field display. Resonator loads had risen six percent since morning. “Send the technical packet to Commodore Mott under emergency navigation privilege. The union receives maintenance comparisons without my sealed authentication.”

Kessa shook her head. “The union receives Lark too. Routekeepers need to know automatic hold can reverse a worker stop.”

“That release may cost your license.”

“The license already instructed me to cut living material.”

Anu corrected quietly. “Probable living tissue.”

“Put probable in the packet.”

Vaust understood that Kessa would transmit with or without her. She added a narrow evidence note stating that the original files remained under dispute but checksums matched available Charter formats. It protected the examiner less than silence and the truth more than Kessa expected.

They sent Irian the complete case first. The mass carrier acknowledged receipt with convoy clock and redistributed the packet to six independent stores. Kessa watched the copies spread beyond Nacre's ability to seal them.

The Routekeepers' Union received the same evidence twenty seconds later. Brine confirmed. Tern confirmed. Then local crews across the Reach began requesting their own raw hold logs.

Nacre control entered the channel. “Routebreaker Rho, cease unauthorized transmission and surrender ship communication.”

“Communication carries convoy safety data.”

“You have distributed unverified biological claims.”

“I distributed photographs of our cuts.”

The distinction left control silent for four seconds.

A warning rose from every cage monitor at once.

Kessa turned to the wall. Resonator One exceeded its normal load band. Two followed before control could respond. Then Three, Four, and Five climbed together. The juvenile's hidden contour sharpened beneath the star field.

“Pulse?” Sena asked.

Anu checked the gradient trace. “Not yet. This originates locally.”

Six rose. Seven and Eight took load hard enough to trip coolant alarms. At Nine, the inner support moved eleven centimetres toward the dark center.

Control applied more hold.

“Stop tightening it,” Kessa said.

No one answered.

Across the image wall, every old wound line drew taut. The structure beneath Nacre's cage expanded against its restraints, not toward one gap or one damaged station. It pushed against the full wheel.

All nine resonators began to move.

Chapter 06 — Charterman's Law

Halvek Enro watched three relief routes close while Continuity crossed into Nacre.

The closures appeared on the command wall as amber rings. Nacre-9. Pellucid-2. A local anchor near Brine that had reported no structural fault until routekeepers received Kessa Rho's packet and inspected its inner cage. Traffic projections spread around each ring in red branches, every diversion adding mass to another corridor.

“Recompute with priority cargo separated,” Halvek said.

His navigation chief stripped passenger and bulk freight from the model. Medical loads remained. Food substrate remained. Coolant cultures for Sable's orbital ring remained. Even that narrow traffic exceeded available capacity at two receiving anchors within six days.

Continuity held steady under fusion braking. The cruiser had crossed from Orison Gap with nine cutters distributed behind it, a formation designed to place Charter authority within one rapid route of any threatened system. Now fixed routes disappeared faster than the force could arrive.

“Population under direct continuity risk?” Halvek asked.

“Eleven point eight million across Nacre, Brine, and Sable,” Analyst Orem said. “That excludes the emergency convoy. Commodore Mott carries four hundred ten thousand in-system, divided by the Nacre closure.”

“Do not exclude them.”

Orem corrected the field. The number grew.

A second analyst displayed Kessa's evidence beside the traffic map. Pale filament. Repaired puncture. Scar patterns from multiple anchors. The raw Lark record carried an examiner's limited authentication and enough independent copies to make seizure a matter of control, not erasure.

Halvek disliked that distinction. Law functioned poorly when evidence outran custody, but reality often did the same.

“Begin with the strongest claim,” he said.

Ecology specialist Venn Cor answered. “The translucent anchor material shows directed repair and coordinated response to gradient change. It persists across the lifetime of a cage. Repeated wounds alter later growth. Those observations support a living structure.”

“Support or prove?”

“Support strongly. We lack a complete body boundary, energy budget, reproductive observation, or comparative specimen.”

“The term juvenile?”

“Inference. Developmental growth patterns make it plausible.”

“Veilback?”

“Field term coined from lensing. No evidentiary value.”

Halvek moved to the Lark record. “Operator action?”

A fault auditor enlarged the command sequence. “Tomis Rho reduced hold twice and requested evacuation. Automatic continuity control reversed both reductions. The public finding attributed escalation to operator compensation. That attribution conflicts with the raw commands.”

“Can the raw record have been altered inside the torque key?”

“Not without breaking station signatures we have verified.”

“Then correct the operator finding.”

Several people around the command table looked up.

Halvek did not mistake institutional liability for factual uncertainty. “Issue a provisional correction under my seal. Tomis Rho did not increase the hold. The automated system did. Notify his surviving family before public posting.”

“His surviving family released the file,” Orem said.

“Notify her anyway.”

The correction entered the legal queue. It would anger the station authority that wrote the first report and weaken Charter defense if any death claims reopened. Neither cost changed the command trace.

Halvek placed both hands behind his back. “Now show me a safe route state.”

No analyst answered.

Venn Cor selected a model of Nacre-9. “Rho proposes mapping all nine resonators before further cutting. Her evidence indicates holds injure the structure.”

“That does not answer the question.”

“We cannot yet model release.”

“Can the corridor pass traffic under present hold?”

“Mechanically, perhaps. Biologically, continued operation may cause harm.”

“May the structure release under traffic?”

“Yes.”

“Consequences?”

“Unknown. The observed motion displaced local objects and distorted service geometry. Greater release could collapse the corridor or produce local shear.”

Halvek looked at the traffic wall. Food convoys waited beyond two systems. Sable had restored one elevator after unrest near its ring and depended on route imports for bearing composite. Brine exported substrate to habitats that kept only nine days in reserve. Nacre fabricated pressure skins for four smaller colonies.

A living inference did not make those dependencies imaginary.

“Give me probabilities,” he said.

Venn Cor shook her head. “The sample size does not support them.”

“Then bounds.”

“Worst case under forced release includes loss of nearby ships, resonator stations, and local orbital structures. Worst case under continued restraint remains unbounded because we do not know the life cycle.”

“Best case?”

“Present motion settles. The corridor remains usable while study continues.”

The answer described hope without giving it a number. Halvek had commanded too many relief schedules to confuse an available best case with a plan.

A signal officer reported that six routekeeper crews had refused thermal maintenance until local tissue inspections finished. Two cited Kessa's packet. Four cited worker exposure. The Routekeepers' Union had not declared a general stop, but local decisions were producing one by increments.

“Open the shortage board,” Halvek said.

Cargo classes filled the wall. Brine substrate to Nacre: delayed. Nacre pressure skins to Sable: delayed. Antineoplastic cultures for three habitats: delayed. Reactor salt for Oriel staging: diverted through a route with one-third the freight throat. Every line attached to inventories, consumption rates, and the date when inconvenience became mortality.

One cell showed famine relief contingency.

Halvek selected it before anyone could hide it in a broader category.

The command room around him remained aboard Continuity. For an instant his hands remembered another rail, thirty-one years earlier, on a relief carrier called Tamar's Mercy. The route to Caud had closed after a resonator fire. Command expected repairs within twelve hours. Halvek, then a logistics lieutenant, had approved holding the grain fleet in place rather than burning fuel toward a slower corridor.

Twelve hours became four days.

Caud's receiving inventories had looked adequate at system scale. They were not distributed. Two orbital farms held reserves behind failed transfer spines. Surface towns received allocations through one elevator that could not carry food down quickly enough. The numbers died in different columns before people did.

He remembered fixing a throughput formula while a field medic read settlement names over the channel. He remembered choosing which ships carried children already sick enough to survive acceleration. He remembered a cargo master asking whether emptying seed grain counted as rescue or delayed murder.

When the route reopened, the final toll reached 830,000.

The inquiry blamed resonator maintenance, local hoarding, late evacuation, and inadequate reserves. Every finding held truth. None reduced the dead.

Halvek had learned one lesson that survived every argument over responsibility: a closed route killed outside the frame of the closure.

“Marshal?” Orem asked.

He returned his attention to Nacre. “Show reserve distribution, not system totals.”

The shortage board divided into habitats and districts. Brine's six million did not starve together. The outer agricultural cylinders could feed themselves. Dense processing habitats depended on daily substrate transfers. Nacre's fabrication stations held food but lacked long-duration waste capacity. Sable's surface had storage; its orbital workers did not.

Consequences acquired locations.

“Assume all three closures last thirty days,” Halvek said. “Prioritize transfers that prevent local reserve cliffs. Suspend luxury cargo. Offer civilian carriers full Charter indemnity for relief diversions.”

“Funding authority?”

“Continuity emergency article.”

“That article has never covered biological closure.”

“It covers interruption. The cause can wait for lawyers.”

Orders moved outward. They bought days. Days mattered, but they did not restore the network.

Captain Jalen Vorr of Cutter Two joined the command table by projection. Two other cutter captains appeared beside him. Their ships held station at Pellucid and Brine, where routekeepers refused stronger holds without signed authority.

Vorr spoke first. “Local crews ask whether we will compel operation.”

“Not until we establish a controlled procedure,” Halvek said.

“Pellucid's hold runs at one hundred and twelve percent. The routekeeper chief says coolant will breach within eighteen hours.”

“Reduce to rated maximum. No thermal cuts.”

“The juvenile may move.”

“So may a failed resonator after coolant loss. Keep the hardware inside known limits.”

Captain Esch at Brine asked, “Do we recognize Rho's life classification?”

“We recognize evidence of a potentially living structure. That does not settle route authority.”

“Workers hear those as incompatible positions.”

“They are inconvenient positions. Incompatibility requires proof.”

Esch looked unconvinced. Halvek allowed it. Command did not require every officer to feel certainty. It required them to know which uncertainty governed action.

He brought up Tern Junction. Three anchor cages occupied an uninhabited system with a sparse service spindle and no planetary population. Traffic could divert before entry. One cage, Tern-3, had already been decommissioned for throat instability and retained complete resonator hardware.

“We will conduct a controlled experiment at Tern,” Halvek said. “Cold-shear procedure under instrument saturation. No civilian traffic. Remote crews where possible. Rho performs the cut because she has direct experience with the material and raised the hazard.”

Venn Cor objected. “Her current evidence argues for observation before cutting.”

“We need an operational result. A controlled cut can test injury response and corridor release.”

“Only if the conditions match her proposed method.”

“She has not proposed a complete method.”

“She requested mapping without automatic hold.”

Halvek enlarged Tern's cage. “We cannot permit an unbounded release while crews occupy the service spindle. A safety hold remains available.”

The ecologist's face tightened. “A hold may contaminate every observation.”

“Then instruments will record the contamination.”

Captain Vorr nodded once. “My cutter can project local hold from outside the debris plane.”

“Prepare to support. Do not activate without direct command.”

Halvek assigned two additional cutters to traffic exclusion and rescue. He ordered uncrewed tugs positioned at likely drift vectors. Medical pods would stand outside the cage plane. The plan accumulated redundancies until every known failure had an answer.

Rescue Chief Damel put the sequence through a live geometry simulation. In the first branch, the cut released stored lens stress symmetrically and Tern-3's corridor throat collapsed into a harmless dark point. In the second, one side retained topology after the opposite resonators dropped. The resulting gradient threw the service spindle fourteen kilometres before local tugs could answer. A third branch refused to converge because the model treated the juvenile as static mass.

“Remove that assumption,” Halvek said.

Damel spread both hands. “Replace it with what behavior?”

“Bounded motion from Nacre.”

Venn Cor objected from the ecology station. “Nacre moved while constrained inside an active cage. A release response may exceed that range.”

“Then set three bounds. Observed motion. Ten times observed. Structural limit of the rescue formation.”

The simulation ran again. Cutter placement survived the first two cases. At the highest bound, one medical pod crossed the projected debris cone before its pilot could burn clear.

“Move rescue outside the cone,” Halvek said.

“That adds eleven minutes to casualty retrieval,” Damel replied.

“It prevents rescuers from becoming casualties before retrieval begins.”

They shifted the pods. One uncrewed tug took the nearer position with a pressure shelter and automated tow line. Halvek then required every worker on the service spindle to carry independent propulsion. Tern's contractor protested the delay and suit inventory. He overruled them.

Traffic created the harder compromise. Closing all three cages for the experiment would cut a relief diversion feeding Oriel staging. Keeping Tern-1 active placed occupied ships within the outer uncertainty band if Tern-3 released asymmetrically.

“Schedule a clean window,” Halvek said.

Navigation displayed the queue. “A complete window delays two reactor-salt carriers by nineteen hours.”

“Oriel reserve?”

“Twenty-six hours at current draw, less if the stranded rear group takes local power.”

Halvek moved the carriers ahead of the test and accepted a four-hour delay to setup. No occupied ship would enter either adjacent corridor after cold-shear equipment armed. The last carrier might still remain inside the receiving corridor when mapping began, but it would clear before the first cut under every certified transit estimate.

Venn Cor studied the timing. “You are protecting the schedule from an unknown animal with a clock derived from known machinery.”

“I am protecting people from both sides of the unknown.”

“Until one side runs late.”

“Then direct command decides. Automatic hold does not.”

Unknown failures remained. They always did.

Legal counsel cleared her throat. “Rho's vessel remains impounded. Her routebreaker license permits refusal under active worker stop.”

“Her stop applies at Nacre.”

“She may argue the hazard class carries to Tern.”

“Then we give her access to design the procedure and document every objection. If she refuses under conditions she controls, I will assign a Charter routebreaker.”

Venn Cor said, “You want her expertise or her legitimacy?”

“Both, if both survive contact with the work.”

He ordered a channel opened to Threadbare.

Kessa Rho appeared in a cramped cockpit under red evidence lights. Sena Ibo sat at manual controls behind her though the tug's drives remained sealed. Anu Sel occupied the edge of the image with a data slate. None looked rested.

Halvek introduced himself. “Routebreaker Rho, I have reviewed your evidence.”

“Did you review the cuts or only the closures?”

“Both. The provisional Lark finding will state that Tomis Rho ordered hold reduction and evacuation. Automatic control reversed him.”

The routebreaker's face changed by less than he expected. One hand closed around something below the frame, perhaps the old torque key listed in the seizure inventory.

“You found twelve years quickly,” she said.

“I found a valid command record. Delay would not improve it.”

“And the juvenile?”

“Evidence supports possible living structure.”

“Probable.” Anu spoke from the edge.

Halvek inclined his head. “Your report says probable. My authority must account for the probability and for the proven dependence of eleven point eight million people on the affected routes.”

Kessa looked toward someone outside the image, likely a monitor carrying convoy shortages. “Keeping a corridor open by tightening the cage increases the load. We watched Nacre push all nine resonators.”

“Can you release it safely?”

“Not yet.”

“Can you certify continued restraint will cause immediate failure?”

“No.”

“Then we have two unresolved hazards and active shortages.”

“You have one hazard built around another.”

“That may be true. It does not produce an alternative route.”

Halvek sent the Tern plan. Kessa read it without speaking. Her eyes moved from cold-shear sequence to remote tug positions, then stopped at the safety hold.

“Remove the hold,” she said.

“I will not expose crews to uncontrolled geometry without a stabilizing option.”

“The cut tells the structure to release. The hold forces it not to. Running both means you test which one breaks first.”

“Then specify a threshold below which standby hold cannot interfere.”

“No active beam. No automatic trigger. If your cruiser keeps a remote system cold, we can discuss it after I map Tern's layers.”

Vorr sent Halvek a private note: Traffic may occupy adjacent corridor during setup. Full cold status limits rescue.

Halvek considered the request. Removing automatic hold would place the decision entirely in human timing. Human operators had failed before. Automatic control had also failed Tomis Rho. Tern existed to separate those facts under measured conditions.

“The beam remains cold during initial mapping,” he said. “Activation requires my direct order. You will perform a sequential cut under Charter control.”

Kessa kept reading. “And if mapping shows no safe cut?”

“You will submit the finding before refusing.”

“That isn't what I asked.”

“No. If you refuse after full access, your license will be suspended pending continuity review. Threadbare will remain impounded and may be requisitioned for emergency route work.”

Sena leaned into view. “You offer her a choice between cutting and giving you the cutter.”

“I offer the worker who raised the hazard authority to shape the test.”

“Under a beam she says makes it unsafe.”

“Under a beam that begins cold.”

Anu lifted her slate. “A biological response may begin before your instruments classify release. Threshold control assumes the cage knows the event better than the tissue does.”

Halvek looked at her data. “Then define a biological indicator my officers can use.”

“We need the experiment to discover one.”

Every answer returned to the same absence: no safe route state, no release threshold, no time estimate for learning. Meanwhile food, medicine, and waste capacity moved toward known cliffs.

Kessa touched the tool below frame and looked directly at him. “I will go to Tern. I will map before cutting. I will put every objection in the work log. If you activate hold while my shear runs, I stop.”

“Agreed, provided stopping does not endanger occupied traffic.”

“There should be no occupied traffic.”

“Tern has three cages. We can close the test corridor. The others may carry diverted relief.”

Her expression hardened. “Then your controlled site already has a load you value more than the test.”

“I value both.”

“That does not make them compatible.”

“No. It makes compatibility the work.”

Halvek released Threadbare from static impound for escorted transit to Tern. Evidence seals remained on external communication, with a Charter relay carrying approved technical traffic. Kessa objected. He allowed an independent union recorder under Examiner Vaust's custody. Control required witnesses who distrusted one another.

The channel closed without agreement on the larger question.

Outside Continuity's forward ports, Nacre's convoy occupied half the star field. Habitat drums turned. Barges waited on vectors that consumed fuel even while appearing still. Beyond them, the anchor cage held a darkness whose injured layers might belong to a vast animal life cycle.

Halvek accepted that possibility.

Acceptance did not feed Brine or move a hospital cylinder.

He returned to the command table after his analysts left. Only Orem remained, closing shortage files one by one. The young analyst paused at Kessa's packet.

“Classification?” Orem asked.

“Restricted pending Tern.”

“The union already has most of it.”

“Most is not the same as an authorized conclusion.”

Orem waited. Analysts learned which pauses invited honesty and which invited dismissal.

Halvek opened a private directive. Its recipients included Charter evidence control, route communications, and every local station administrator in the threatened chain.

“Findings that support safe operation may be released with methods and limits,” he dictated. “Findings that require route closure must include an immediately executable alternative for essential traffic. Until such an alternative exists, restrict distribution to command review.”

Orem's hands stopped above the table. “That standard could withhold a true hazard.”

“It withholds an incomplete instruction.”

“People may read the absence as safety.”

“Then local notices state investigation continues.”

The analyst looked toward the population field. “And if Tern confirms the structures live but gives no route?”

Halvek remembered Caud's settlement names arriving after every useful choice had expired. “Truth without movement will not keep them alive.”

He sealed the directive under continuity authority.

At the bottom, one operative word remained visible after encryption closed over the rest.

SUPPRESS.

Chapter 07 — Cut at Tern

Kessa set the cold-shear cutter against Tern's outermost feed and felt two commands fighting through the housing.

One came from her tool. Its ceramic jaws drew heat away from the resonator conduit in measured steps, asking the field to diminish along a path the hardware could survive. The other came through the anchor cage as a continuous tightening. Charter control called that pressure a safety hold. In Kessa's gloves it felt like somebody hauling a door shut while she removed the hinges.

The beam had begun cold, as Halvek promised. After her first three withdrawal steps, the last passenger spindle reported a delayed exit from the adjacent corridor. Halvek activated minimum containment without taking Kessa's cutter out of the feed.

She lifted the cutter before either side won.

Tern Junction filled her visor with machinery and almost nothing else. Three anchor cages hung around the service spindle, their resonators joined by pale trusses. Sparse dust caught the work lights in thin amber sheets. No inhabited world waited below. No habitat rotated behind the spindle. Halvek had chosen Tern because a failure here could supposedly spend itself on empty space.

His crews had still brought people into it.

More than a hundred route workers occupied the service spindle and its three exterior galleries. Charter security teams held positions in two armored cutters and the control blister. More personnel waited farther out aboard Continuity. Kessa had counted every suit transponder twice before leaving Threadbare.

Sena held the tug six kilometres above the cage plane, close enough to retrieve them and far enough to survive an ordinary dump. Anu watched field response from the repair gallery. Jori and Vasken worked beside Kessa on separate tethers, their boots clamped to Resonator Tern-Beta Four.

Nothing about the load under Kessa's hands felt ordinary.

“Cutter paused,” she said. “The cage is adding counterforce during each withdrawal step.”

Halvek answered from the control blister. “The hold remains at minimum containment.”

“Minimum containment still opposes the cut.”

“Our instruments show both systems inside tolerance.”

Kessa looked along the conduit. Frost had formed a straight white band before she began. Now it curled around the housing in hooked ridges, following stress no fabrication plan included. “Your instruments average nine stations. My hands are on one.”

A Charter engineer named Rusk opened a private diagnostic pane between them. His face appeared in the corner of Kessa's visor, flattened by helmet light. “Local phase error is point zero two.”

“Show the unaveraged gradient.”

He hesitated.

“Engineer Rusk.”

The raw trace arrived. Two lines crossed and separated with every cold-shear step. Her cutter reduced tension across the outer feed. The hold system restored it from the inner ring before the material could settle. Each cycle left a small excess beneath both measurements.

Jori whistled softly. “We're winding it.”

“Correct,” Kessa said. “Tools off.”

Vasken pulled his isolation fork from the adjacent conduit. Jori secured the thermal braid. Across the cage, other route workers stopped in sequence as Kessa's order passed through their foremen.

Halvek entered the common channel. “Routebreaker Rho, explain the stoppage.”

“The cut and your hold form a ratchet. We remove local stress. You restore global geometry before the release completes. The excess has nowhere to go except into the hollow.”

“You agreed to a controlled experiment.”

“I agreed to perform a sequential cold-shear cut. Nobody told me you would pull against it.”

“The containment condition accompanied the authorization.”

“It accompanied the transport order. That does not make the systems compatible.”

Beyond Beta Four, Tern's dark center deformed the service spindle into two overlapping crescents. Kessa had spent three days studying every available trace from Nacre, Lark-3, and the other cages Halvek allowed them to see. The juvenile remained invisible except when frost, debris, or stars revealed the topology around it. Here the absence seemed compressed. Its bent-light outline pressed close to the resonator wheel, then drew inward in a slow contraction.

Three beats traveled through the feed beneath Kessa's boots.

Not loud. Not violent. The first two arrived close together. The third followed after a pause long enough for the cage to respond. Around the wheel, output rose by four percent.

Anu spoke from Threadbare. “That matches the distant component at Nacre. Longer interval because the source is farther along the chain.”

“Do not call it a source until you can establish one,” Halvek said.

“I called it a component.”

Kessa kept her palm on the conduit. The load did not repeat. A smaller trembling continued beneath it, like a cable carrying motion from somewhere beyond sight.

“Remove the hold,” she said.

Halvek's reply took four seconds. Tern itself imposed almost no communication delay. The time belonged to people consulting consequences.

“A convoy segment entered the far corridor nine minutes ago,” he said. “Four freight carriers, one passenger spindle, two tugs. They have not cleared the arrival throat.”

Kessa turned toward the third cage. Tern-Gamma's corridor mouth showed as a narrow field of doubled stars beyond the service spindle. “Why were they sent through during the cut?”

“They were committed before your stoppage.”

“You brought live mass inside linked geometry.”

“Their originating system has six hours of reserve power. Delay would strand them at a failing port.”

Sena broke in. “And now you want their arrival corridor held open through an incompatible severance.”

“I want Routebreaker Rho to resume the agreed procedure.”

Kessa studied the raw lines. The excess had accumulated across only three preliminary steps, yet the two traces no longer returned to baseline. If she continued, every cut would store more geometry. If she stopped while the hold remained, the juvenile would keep carrying what they had already put into it.

“How long until the passenger spindle clears?” she asked.

“Eleven minutes at present translation.”

“Then remove my crews from the wheel.”

“The experiment cannot be rescheduled. The Nacre corridor remains closed, and two more route failures are under review.”

“We have learned what the experiment can tell us under a hold. It tells us to stop.”

A security authorization spread across her visor. It shifted cutter control from Threadbare to the blister. The tool in Kessa's hands armed without her touch.

She ripped its physical bus free.

The cutter went dead. Ceramic jaws floated open against the conduit.

Vasken stared at the loose cable. “That voids the tool bond.”

“Send the invoice to Charter safety.”

Halvek's voice lost none of its control. “Reconnect the bus.”

“No.”

“This procedure supports routes serving eleven point eight million people.”

“These workers support it with their bodies. I will not let your remote system move my hands.”

For several breaths nobody spoke. Kessa could hear suit pumps, the small scrape of Jori's tether ring, and the deeper vibration coming through Beta Four. Halvek's arithmetic had followed them from Nacre. Closed routes meant hospitals without compounds, habitats without food substrate, stations without machine parts. Kessa did not doubt those consequences. She doubted the claim that they excused adding a second unknown to a dangerous cut.

Rusk sent another private trace. The hold had increased by two percent while Halvek called it unchanged.

Kessa marked the rise and pushed it to every worker channel. “All exterior teams, disengage tools. Move toward spindle shelters by tether line. Do not use powered winches.”

A foreman on Alpha protested. “Our orders come through Charter control.”

“Then ask Charter control whether its hold rose after the stop.”

Rusk's face vanished from her visor. The diagnostic pane remained.

Kessa copied the raw trace into every suit and forced it onto the blister's main board. A red margin appeared around the two climbing lines. The system labeled that margin advisory because the averaged cage still reported tolerance.

“Advisory does not move a body,” she said. “Beta teams, state your withdrawal path.”

Jori answered for their station. “Rail to Junction Twelve. Handline across the service gap. Spindle shelter Beta.”

“If Junction Twelve bends out of reach?”

“Cross to the inspection cradle.”

“Cradle has room for four. You carry eleven.”

Jori looked along the rail as if a second path might appear when challenged. Kessa made him find one. The thermal-braid locker could take two suited workers if they stripped its spools. A maintenance sled parked below the truss could move six without powered guidance. The remaining three would need separate tether pairs along the feed itself.

Alpha's foreman reported a shelter route through the central spindle. Kessa asked him to count pressure doors. He reached three before discovering that one door's local bottle had failed inspection two months earlier. Charter control had deferred replacement because Tern lacked permanent staff.

“Put a portable bottle there now,” Kessa said.

“Security has the nearest one.”

“Security can breathe from suits.”

The foreman requested it. A lieutenant refused until Kessa put the denial on the shared channel with the raw gradient. Twelve seconds later, two security suits crossed the truss carrying the bottle between them.

Halvek said, “You are consuming the experiment window.”

“I am discovering your empty junction has people in it.”

She called Gamma next. The arrival-side crews had planned to retreat toward the corridor mouth, exactly where the delayed passenger spindle would emerge. Kessa reversed them toward the outer radiator frame and assigned one route tug to hold beside it with engines warm. The tug pilot objected that Charter traffic control owned his vector.

“Who owns your throttle?” Kessa asked.

“I do.”

“Keep that answer.”

Anu added live pulse timing to each withdrawal display. No line promised safety. The overlays showed where a worker could move without crossing a loaded feed, where changing lens geometry might duplicate visual handholds, and which route relied on a powered winch the crews could not trust after a field excursion.

Kessa made every foreman point a gloved hand toward the first physical rail. Speech could survive as theory. A hand aimed at metal.

On Beta, Jori reached for the maintenance sled and found its mechanical release wired shut beneath a Charter tamper seal. “Do I preserve evidence?”

“Preserve breathing,” Kessa said.

He cut the wire. Vasken kicked the sled clear of its cradle and checked the cold-gas bottle. Half pressure. Enough for one crossing if nobody asked it to steer twice.

Rusk returned to the common channel without his image. “I have posted the unaveraged gradient to the blister board.”

Halvek asked, “Under whose authority?”

“Mine as station engineer.”

For the first time, the safety hold indicator showed its actual two-percent rise beside Halvek's command. Workers who had waited for supervisors began unclipping tools.

Across Tern, route workers began to move. Some obeyed Kessa at once. Others waited until their supervisors copied the raw trace. Security personnel left the cutters in powered suits, not to help with withdrawal but to guard the control nodes.

Anu said, “Kessa, the filament response is changing.”

“Direction?”

“Outward. All measured branches.”

The juvenile answered again.

Three loads struck the cage hard enough to flex the feed under Kessa's boots. The first drove her knees against the housing. The second snapped a frost sheet from the conduit and sent it spinning. Before the third arrived, every resonator fired its hold correction.

Stored geometry released through the system in the wrong order.

Beta Four moved sideways.

The station itself shifted less than a metre, but its surrounding lens did not follow as one body. Kessa saw the work lights bend into white hooks. The service spindle seemed to kink at its center. Jori, two metres away, stretched into a narrow reflection and vanished behind a fold of dark.

“Lines loose!” Kessa shouted.

Vasken cut Jori's tether before the changing angle could pull him into the conduit. Kessa fired her suit jets once, aiming for the place Jori had occupied. Her display showed three versions of his transponder. Two were instrument ghosts. The third accelerated toward the spindle with no physical thrust.

Sena moved Threadbare before Kessa called. The tug descended across the cage plane, grapple drums already opening.

Halvek ordered the hold increased.

“Don't!” Kessa drove her isolation fork into the local feed. “Every increment comes back uneven.”

His command reached the stations anyway.

Tern-Beta Two shed its outer truss. A hundred metres of braced ceramic tore away and struck the service spindle. The impact traveled without sound through Kessa's boots, a blunt kick that folded three exterior galleries. Atmosphere burst from the control blister in a silver cloud.

Emergency channels filled at once.

Kessa heard route workers calling suit failures. Security demanded vectors nobody could provide. A cutter pilot reported two engines firing against each other because the star field no longer agreed with inertial reference. Under all of it, the cage carried a long physical groan that her suit rendered through contact audio.

“Threadbare, give me manual grapple authority.”

“Drum one is yours,” Sena said. “Drum two is holding the tug together.”

A targeting line appeared along Kessa's left wrist. She caught Jori's real transponder by watching debris around him. Frost chips curved toward one image and passed through the others. She fired the grapple past his tumbling suit, opened its three teeth, and reeled until one hooked his tether ring.

The line came tight.

Jori spun toward her with his right arm hanging wrong. Kessa clipped him to Beta Four's surviving rail. “Seal status?”

“Green,” he gasped. “Arm isn't.”

“Vasken, take him inward.”

“The spindle shelters are gone.”

“Then use Threadbare.”

Another shift swept the cage. This one rolled Kessa across the conduit and pulled the grapple line toward the hidden center. Jori cried out as his broken arm struck the rail. Vasken locked both arms around him while Kessa cut their boot clamps loose.

Sena aligned the tug's forward lock along their new fall. It looked impossibly far away, then close enough to touch, then far again as the lens changed. Kessa waited for the work lights to straighten before pushing Jori and Vasken free. They crossed six kilometres in twenty seconds according to her suit clock. Sena caught them with drum two and a burst from the tug's port jets.

Kessa remained on Beta Four because someone still had to stop the local feed.

She reached the manual break and found its ceramic lever fused under hold current. The cold-shear cutter floated beside her on its wrist tether, bus severed but thermal pack intact. She wedged one jaw around the lever housing and triggered the pack mechanically.

Cold crawled across the ceramic. A crack formed.

“Rho,” Halvek said. “Leave the station.”

“You kept my crews here.”

“The hold has lost synchronization. We are evacuating control.”

“Release it completely.”

“We cannot while the corridor remains occupied.”

The far cage flashed. Tern-Gamma's passenger spindle had begun to emerge, eleven kilometres of rotating structure entering geometry that no longer held a stable throat. Kessa saw one end in ordinary space. The other remained stretched across the corridor. Four freight carriers followed as clusters of duplicated lights.

A Charter cutter drifted between Beta Four and the spindle with its engines dark. Its pilot called for tow assistance. Twelve transponders showed aboard. One radiator folded toward the hull under a gradient that treated metal like soft foil.

Kessa broke the manual lever.

Beta Four's output dropped out of the hold network. Stored load rushed toward the remaining eight stations. The cage center expanded against them, visible through a sudden sweep of stars. She had not freed the juvenile. She had only removed one hand from a body still pulled by eight others.

The security cutter started to fall inward.

Sena said, “I can take you or them on this pass.”

Kessa looked at the cutter's twelve transponders. “Take them.”

“You have ninety seconds of suit thrust.”

“I have a truss.”

“That truss has ambitions.”

“Learned them at Nacre.”

Threadbare rolled beneath the disabled cutter. Pell fired drum one across its bow and missed as the lens shifted. He retracted without complaint, corrected by the way loose hull flakes fell, and shot again. The grapple teeth caught a radiator root. Sena burned laterally. For a moment the tug and cutter moved on separate visible trajectories while the line between them curved through darkness.

Then both ships agreed on a direction.

The cutter cleared the juvenile's expanding topology by thirty metres. Its crew came across the emergency channel one by one, reporting injuries and pressure. Twelve living signals stabilized beside Threadbare.

Kessa climbed the damaged truss toward the service spindle. She passed an empty pair of boots still clamped to the rail. A torn suit glove circled a work light. Transponders winked out faster than the rescue board could classify them.

Alpha's foreman called from beneath collapsed ceramic. “We have nine in pressure. Six outside. Our lock won't cycle.”

“Cut the inner hinge and push the whole door,” Kessa said.

“No cutter power.”

“Use suit packs against the panel corners. Opposed pairs.”

She talked them through the load while crossing a gap on her tether. Four workers got the door moving. Two lost contact when the spindle bent again. Kessa did not ask their names over the open channel. Names would come when somebody could answer with certainty.

The door opened against the crushed gallery and jammed at the width of a shoulder pack. Pressure remained on both sides, but a broken cable tray crossed the passage like a sprung rib. Nine workers waited behind it. Kessa could hear one of them counting suit oxygen in minutes.

“Opposed packs will not clear that tray,” the foreman said.

Kessa studied debris moving past the spindle. Every loose fragment followed a curve that changed when Beta Four flexed. The inspection cradle drifted forty metres above the gallery, still carrying the mechanical pry bars from the evacuation route she had forced Jori to identify.

“Jori, can you release the cradle tools?”

His injured breath came from Threadbare. “Panel is under the rear bench.”

Vasken reached it while holding Jori's splint with one knee. A control packet crossed to Kessa's suit. She rejected the cradle's visual location and found its transponder among two ghosts. One short grapple shot caught the tool cage. The line drew a curve around the broken spindle and delivered the cage against her boots hard enough to crack its lid.

She passed a pry bar through the narrow door. “Set the hook behind the tray root, not the middle. Two of you pull. Everyone else stays off the door.”

Metal scraped through suit contact. The tray shifted a handspan and tried to return. A worker wedged a second bar beneath it. Another found an emergency hinge pin on the door and drove it into the gap. They built a ratchet from whatever the station had left.

The smallest worker came through first, dragging a pressure bag with three suit bottles. She clipped each bottle to a waiting chest before moving toward the tether line. Five more followed. The seventh had a cracked visor layer and crossed backward so the foreman could watch the seal. Eight cleared.

The foreman remained inside with the last worker, whose left boot had fused to a floor clamp under the field surge.

“Cut the boot,” Kessa said.

“Clamp wraps the ankle.”

“Cut the suit joint above it and cap the leg.”

The worker heard her. “That takes the leg.”

“The gallery takes everything in thirty seconds.”

No comforting word belonged in the channel. The foreman used a rescue cuff above the knee, flooded it with sealant, and set a utility blade against the joint. Kessa turned the open channel to medical so the worker heard dosage and pressure instructions while metal parted.

They came through together. The foreman carried the capped suit against his chest. The worker screamed once, then spent the rest of his breath reporting a green torso seal.

Kessa pushed both toward the handline. A route tug at the radiator frame reeled them away without waiting for Charter vector authority. The gallery folded inward seconds after their transponders cleared.

Tern-Gamma's passenger spindle finally cleared the throat. Its rotation had slowed, and three habitation sectors reported structural alarms, but it remained one piece. The freight carriers emerged behind it on diverging vectors. Their tugs burned hard to keep them from crossing.

“Corridor empty,” Rusk said from somewhere outside the blister. His breathing came ragged through a damaged suit channel. “Marshal, corridor reads empty.”

Halvek dropped the hold.

Eight resonators shut down within a fraction of a second.

Kessa felt every stored increment find motion.

The juvenile unfolded.

No body appeared in the human sense. Darkness widened beneath the spindle and carried a surface made from bent stars. The topology rose through the cage, passing between stations that had seemed to enclose it. Trusses tore outward. Dust vanished along curved paths. Kessa's suit clock lost nine seconds, recovered seven, then flooded her visor with disagreement warnings.

A final three-beat load passed through Tern. The first beat broke the service spindle at its middle. The second threw Kessa free. The third opened a black corridor where no fixed route remained.

She tumbled without a tether, suit jets refusing automatic control because every reference looked false. Ahead, Threadbare appeared in four places. Kessa ignored the stars and watched the tug's hot radiator. Heat followed matter more honestly than light in the lens. She fired toward it in three short bursts.

Sena met her halfway with a grapple.

The teeth closed around Kessa's thigh clamp. Drum one hauled her toward the forward lock while debris crossed paths that should have collided and did not. She struck the outer hatch hard enough to empty her lungs. Vasken pulled her inside before pressure finished equalizing.

Jori lay strapped to the deck with his arm splinted. The rescued cutter crew crowded the repair gallery. Anu worked at a console bolted beside the specimen bench, reading the retreating gradient.

“Where did it go?” Kessa asked.

Anu pointed to the forward display. “Open space. Away from all three corridor axes.”

The bent-light back moved beyond Tern's broken wheel. It crossed the dust sheet without impact, drawing particles into a pale wake. No cage beam followed. No resonator remained aligned enough to catch it.

Behind the juvenile, Tern's arrival corridor narrowed to a line and disappeared.

Pell began the casualty count from suit records, lock logs, and the blister roster. He spoke each category once. Seventeen route workers had vanished around Alpha and Beta. Twenty-four more signals had ended across the spindle galleries. Charter's partial roster showed four dead from the broken spindle, while eighteen others remained missing in the blister and second cutter zones.

More reports arrived from the far galleries. The numbers worsened as damaged suits stopped repeating old beacons. By the time Continuity established an independent sweep, the total had settled at 63 dead: forty-one route workers and twenty-two Charter or security crew. Survivors remained in every group, but nobody found another living signal beyond the mapped rescue pockets.

Kessa stared at the board until the figures stopped trying to become a problem she could repair. Jori's blood moved in small spheres inside his splint bag. Vasken held the injured arm steady with both hands. Across the gallery, one rescued security pilot pressed his helmet to his knees.

Halvek called from Continuity. “Routebreaker Rho, report whether the cut caused release.”

She opened the common channel so every surviving worker could hear her answer.

“The cut and the hold opposed each other. You delayed removal of the hold with occupied traffic inside linked geometry. Every correction stored more load. When you let go, Tern spent all of it.”

A pause followed, shorter than his delay before the accident and far longer than truth required.

“I asked whether your cut caused release.”

Kessa watched the juvenile diminish into unanchored dark. “The animal left because the cage failed. We do not yet know how to free the next one safely.”

Tern's last service lights went out around the severed spindle. With no corridor to bend them, the stars drew back into single points.

Between those ordinary stars, the juvenile vanished. On the casualty board, Record 63 finished writing coordinates beside a suit transponder that no longer answered. Rescue clocks continued counting in the volume where the route had been.

Chapter 08 — The First Breach

Irian watched seven versions of Kindly Argument drift across the logistics pit.

Only one represented the hospital cylinder. The others came from route solutions that had not admitted Tern's corridor no longer existed. Each model carried the same hull number, the same thirty-two thousand patients and dependents, and a different future position. Two placed the cylinder safely beside Accord of Dust. Three sent it through the wrecked service spindle. One left it inside a corridor throat that had collapsed ten hours earlier. The last drew a graceful curve toward Tern's dark center and labeled the outcome STABLE ARRIVAL.

“Remove every automatic plot,” Irian said.

The bridge crew stared at her across tiers of workstations. Accord's command deck had been built to manage mass, not uncertainty. Navigation filled one wall. Habitat loads filled another. Between them, six hundred and forty attached modules hung from the carrier's eleven-kilometre truss as boxes of light, each carrying water, seed, pressure, machinery, or people. None could be allowed to become an icon without consequence.

Navigation chief Ko Demir looked from his board to the central plot. “We need a reference solution to remove one.”

“We have fixed stars outside the lens, ship clocks, and our own thrust records.”

“Those disagree by four hundred kilometres.”

“Then print the disagreement.”

Irian keyed the convoy channel. “All captains, disable route-assisted navigation. Preserve your local instrument buffers. You will receive manual navigation slates by tug or tight beam. Do not follow a corridor marker unless two independent ships confirm it by direct sighting.”

A dozen objections reached the queue before she finished. The loudest came from captains whose vessels had never flown without an anchor solution. Irian understood the fear. Fixed routes had reduced interstellar navigation to alignment, authorization, and thrust. Tern had taken away the first two while leaving every captain responsible for the third.

She expanded the actual convoy geometry.

Accord of Dust and forty-seven attached habitats had cleared Tern-Gamma before the breach. Four freight carriers and their tugs drifted near the broken spindle. Sixty-three vessels remained in Nacre's system behind a closed departure throat, including food barges that supplied half the convoy's mobile kitchens. Another twenty-nine had diverted before entry and now occupied ordinary space between the two route control volumes. They had neither arrived nor stayed behind. Their last corridor impulse had thrown them outside any charted approach cone.

The convoy existed in three pieces. Irian could speak to two with seconds of delay. The Nacre segment answered through a relay path that took eleven minutes each way and could fail whenever Nacre's cage shifted.

A casualty report opened on a side panel. Tern had confirmed 63 dead. Forty-one route workers. Twenty-two Charter or security crew. Irian read the categories once, then minimized them without dismissing the file. She could not command rescue by staring at a number already settled.

“Plot weak habitats,” she said.

Load control painted twenty-six modules amber. Their drive collars could correct spin but could not sustain translation without tow. Eight had tug contracts. Eighteen depended on Accord's truss or route arrival machinery.

“Pair every unpowered habitat with a tug.”

“We have eleven free tugs,” Demir said.

“Then stronger habitat clusters lend theirs. Pair by life-support margin, not ownership.”

A freight captain cut into the command queue. “Commodore, my tugs belong to paid cargo.”

“Your paid cargo has forty-one days of passive thermal tolerance. Cylinder D-19 loses air processing in nine hours if its spin collar drifts. Choose which invoice you want read at the inquest.”

The captain muted himself.

Marl Quade stood at the delegate rail below the bridge stations, one hand hooked through a restraint strap. He had come up from the passenger drums when the first route models began contradicting one another. His rigger's shoulders filled a civilian coat that had never been tailored for bridge harnesses.

“You're moving contracted tugs without a passenger vote,” he said.

“I'm preventing immediate pressure loss.”

“Say that into the order.”

Irian looked at him. “Why?”

“So the loan ends with the emergency. Otherwise the cargo captains will claim you reassigned their equipment for the rest of the voyage, and the habitat delegates will spend the next week fighting over whether to give it back.”

The distinction cost six words and might save six days. Irian amended the order. “Emergency tow assignment until independent pressure and spin are secured.”

Marl nodded without thanks. They had no room for gratitude performed as ceremony.

The first weak habitat to receive a borrowed tug carried nine hundred glassworkers from Pellin Arc. Its captain had flown spin corrections for twenty-six years and had never commanded free translation. She called Irian with a manual slate held close to the camera, the paper display trembling in her hand.

“I have the tug on our south collar,” Captain Aves said. “Its pilot wants a vector. My board gives five.”

“Cover your board.”

Aves looked offended. “Commodore?”

“Put a maintenance sheet over every route solution. Keep the slate visible. Which fixed star sits above your spin axis?”

The captain turned toward an off-camera port. “Kered. I think.”

“Do not think. Ask your tug.”

The tug pilot confirmed Kered and named two dimmer references. Demir marked their triangle on Accord's plot. Irian had Aves draw the same triangle by hand, then place her habitat where its rim crossed the lower star. The mark looked crude enough to belong on a child's lesson card. It also came from a thing the captain could see.

“Now have the tug burn until Kered moves one finger-width from your axis,” Irian said. “Stop. Wait thirty seconds. Mark where the other two stars go.”

“That does not give me a route.”

“It gives you a measured change. Routes have failed us. Changes have not.”

Aves relayed the instruction. Her borrowed tug fired for four seconds. The habitat's spin axis shifted with no promise of elegance, but its pressure collar moved away from the debris sheet. After thirty seconds the captain added a second pencil mark.

“I see it,” she said.

“Good. Teach the next habitat.”

Irian transferred her channel to Cylinder D-19, where air processors had fallen to eight hours of margin. The glassworkers' captain began the same questions before the connection finished. One manual slate passed knowledge faster than a central solution could pass authority.

Along the convoy, stronger clusters released their tugs reluctantly. Latches opened. Tow craft moved between drifting habitats by direct sighting, each small vessel carrying a navigator who could say what the stars had done rather than what route software expected them to do. Passenger channels filled with rumors of total engine failure, Charter attack, and collision. Marl sent delegates the plain order and its limit: emergency tow assignment, immediate pressure and spin only.

By the time the first eleven pairings stabilized, no habitat had gained a path out of Tern. Eighteen had gained another ship able to see where it actually moved.

A warning turned Kindly Argument red.

The hospital cylinder had begun to rotate around a point no longer occupied by an anchor. Its local tugs reported thrust in the correct direction. The main plot showed their exhaust bending away from the carrier, robbed of useful acceleration by the lens remnant.

“Direct sighting?” Irian asked.

A sensor officer stripped overlays from the cupola feed. Tern's broken spindle appeared against sparse stars. Beyond it, Kindly Argument crossed the field as a long rotating bar, white hospital markings flashing every twenty-eight seconds. Its path curved toward a patch of doubled light where the collapsed corridor had left a closing lens.

Demir measured the drift from three fixed stars. “Closest approach to the remnant in fifty-four minutes. Margin unknown.”

“Cylinder thrust?”

“Spin units only. Tow tugs are at seventy percent and losing leverage.”

The hospital commander's voice arrived strained but precise. “Accord, our surgical drum cannot take another gravity discontinuity. We have six procedures in progress. Recommend you detach the outer recovery wards and concentrate tow on the core.”

The outer wards held eight thousand people.

Irian opened their module list. Postoperative beds, radiation shelter, prosthetic fabrication, three water tanks, and the convoy's largest sterile pharmacy. Calling them outer wards made separation sound architectural. Detaching them here would put eight thousand people into units with no independent translation.

“Negative,” she said. “Maintain full cylinder integrity.”

A new voice entered under Charter encryption. Halvek Enro appeared at the edge of the plot from Continuity, his cruiser holding beyond the debris field. “Commodore, the hospital's trajectory intersects active lens shear. Separate nonessential mass and recover the medical core.”

Irian disliked how quickly his language made murder resemble sorting.

“Define nonessential.”

“Machine shops, long-recovery wards, dependent berths, reserve water beyond survival allocation.”

“Those dependent berths contain people.”

“They contain people whose survival probability decreases if the entire cylinder is lost.”

“Then send your cutters to tow it.”

“My cutters are recovering Tern personnel and securing the failed cage.”

“The cage is gone.”

“The remaining hardware may endanger every vessel in this volume.”

Irian brought the hospital roster beside his face. Names ran past faster than either of them could read. “I will not jettison slow civilian modules as nonessential mass to preserve your security perimeter.”

“You may lose all of them.”

“That risk belongs in the rescue plan, not in a label.”

She closed his channel before he could place an order through naval precedence. Accord no longer served in the route navy. Irian had spent fourteen years learning that civilian command required more explanation and a cleaner refusal.

“Available carrier thrust,” she said.

Engineering answered from three decks aft. “Twelve point four percent reserve if we keep scheduled Brine transfer and spin support. Seventeen if we cancel both.”

“Tow geometry for Kindly Argument.”

Demir built it manually. Accord could not approach the cylinder as a ship approached another ship. Seventy point four million tonnes of convoy infrastructure stretched along her truss and barges. A turn begun now would take hours to settle. Instead, she could burn crosswise and use the carrier's mass to shift the hospital's relative fall, then send every available tug down the new line.

The maneuver would consume fuel intended for later route corrections. No later route yet existed.

“Prepare a lateral burn at point three standard,” Irian said. “Duration six hundred seconds.”

Engineering came back immediately. “That spends five point one percent reserve.”

“I heard the estimate.”

“Thermal recovery leaves us unable to repeat it for nineteen hours.”

“Then make this one accurate.”

Marl leaned toward the central plot. “What happens to the attached habitats?”

“Every drum feels a lateral load below design comfort and above design politeness. Tell delegates to secure loose mass and stop water transfers.”

“People will think we're turning.”

“We are turning one degree so a hospital does not fall into a hole.”

“Say that too.”

Irian added it to the convoy announcement. Across the truss, habitat controllers shut down pumps and locked agricultural carts. Command crew tightened harnesses. Barges reported latches. One cylinder refused the burn because its neonatal ward could not tolerate the projected vibration.

Irian called its captain herself. “You have six minutes to float every crib on shock webbing.”

“We need twenty.”

“You have six before thrust and fifty before the hospital reaches the lens. Use corridor mattresses under the web anchors.”

“Those are not certified.”

“Neither is the dark.”

The captain accepted.

Manual navigation slates began leaving Accord through courier tubes and tight-beam packets. Each contained the same blank field, three fixed-star references, local clock offsets, and instructions for marking another ship's position by direct sighting. No central route line appeared. Demir hated the absence.

“Captains will draw different maps,” he said.

“They already have different maps. Now they can show us where.”

“Some of them cannot solve a three-body drift without automation.”

“Pair them with crews who can. Put a navigator on every habitat-tug channel.”

The first slate came back from a mining tender. Its hand-marked hospital track differed from Accord's by ninety kilometres but agreed on the direction of drift. A routekeeper cutter contributed a second trace. Three cargo ships added rougher marks. Demir layered them without averaging away the disagreement.

A corridor of probable motion emerged.

Irian pointed to its outer edge. “Place tugs there, not on the nominal center. If the hospital falls faster than predicted, they meet it. If it falls slower, they can burn inward.”

Eleven tugs detached from stronger habitats. Four belonged to freight firms that had objected. Two hauled water bladders and left them drifting under passive beacons. One came from Accord's own docking spine with half its maintenance crew still fastening panels.

Halvek reopened his channel. “Your burn could destabilize the remaining Tern hardware.”

“Your hold already completed that work.”

“This is not the time for accusation.”

“No. It is time for thrust. Clear my projected plume.”

Continuity moved two cutters aside. The cruiser itself held position.

At burn minus thirty seconds, Kindly Argument reported a failure in its near tow collar. One of the original tugs had pulled against a false star reference until the collar bearings seized. The tug remained attached but could not alter its angle.

“Tell them to cut loose,” Demir said.

Irian watched the hospital rotate. A drifting tug near the cylinder could strike an outer ward during the carrier burn. Keeping it attached would twist the failed collar.

“Can they lock their gimbal?”

“Not remotely.”

“Crew outside?”

“Two suited riggers on the collar.”

The hospital commander spoke over him. “We can abandon the tug with both crew aboard. Their drive remains functional.”

Irian counted time. Twenty-one seconds.

“Riggers return to tug. Blow collar bolts on my mark. Tug burns radial north and joins the outer tow line.”
