A rigger answered with heavy breathing. “One bolt won't arm.”

“Manual striker?”

“On the cylinder side.”

Nine seconds.

“Leave it,” Irian said. “Everyone secure for thrust.”

The failed tug would remain attached. They would have to carry its bad angle through the burn.

Accord of Dust fired lateral engines.

Acceleration entered the bridge as a slow pressure against Irian's harness. The logistics pit creaked around its reinforced joints. On the mass board, six hundred and forty module icons shifted from green to amber as their mounts took load. Water moved inside bladders despite locked valves. One agricultural cylinder lost spin synchronization and recovered under local control.

Outside, the carrier's eleven-kilometre truss bowed by a measurable fraction.

The hospital did not move toward them at first. Its apparent track worsened as Accord changed the reference field. Three navigation programs generated collision warnings based on the discarded route solution. Demir killed them one by one.

“Use the slates,” Irian said.

Direct-sighting marks arrived from the tug line. Each reported Kindly Argument crossing a slightly different star pair. Together they showed the cylinder leaving its original curve.

The carrier's mass had changed the meeting geometry. Eleven tugs burned from the outside edge of the predicted corridor. Their plumes made a ragged fan. The hospital rotated into it, carrying the failed tug like a splint tied at the wrong angle.

“Tow contact in ninety seconds,” Demir said.

The closing lens brightened.

It had no surface, only a region where starlight doubled and slipped. Tern debris approaching that region accelerated along curves the manual plot could not anticipate. A section of ceramic truss vanished from one side and reappeared as fragments thirty kilometres away.

Kindly Argument passed within the uncertain margin.

Its outer wards stretched on the visual feed, an effect of bent light rather than metal, yet pressure alarms rose across three decks. The surgical drum lost half a rotation. One tow tug crossed behind the cylinder and nearly entered its radiator wash. Another burned dry correcting the line.

Irian held Accord's thrust until engineering called the reserve at seven point six percent spent.

“Commodore, planned duration complete.”

“Ten more seconds.”

“That takes us below Brine margin.”

“Ten.”

The engines continued. Direct sighting showed the hospital clear the remnant by eighteen kilometres, a number with an uncertainty larger than itself. What mattered lay in the trend. Every new mark placed Kindly Argument farther from the closing lens.

“Cut thrust.”

Pressure left the bridge. The truss rebounded with a shudder felt through deck plates and seats. Engineering began listing overheated units before the burn ended.

Tow tugs brought the hospital alongside over the next three hours. No route solution guided them. Navigators called star pairs and clock marks across open channels. Captains corrected by fractions, then waited to see which geometry held. The failed tug remained attached until a rigger reached the manual striker and broke the last bolt with a steel bar.

When Kindly Argument finally matched Accord's drift, its commander reported six patients lost during spin interruption and thirty-one serious injuries. Eight thousand people still occupied the outer wards Halvek had called dispensable. The sterile pharmacy retained pressure. Every reserve water tank remained attached.

Irian entered the losses without converting them into victory.

Fuel control gave her the new reserve. The rescue had spent enough reaction mass to erase Halvek's offered timetable for Brine and whatever destination followed it. Accord could still turn. She could not do so quickly, and no second lateral burn remained available before the engines cooled.

A tight-beam packet arrived from the Nacre segment. Their route throat had sealed while they waited. Sixty-three convoy vessels, carrying one hundred thirty-six thousand people, now lacked any open fixed corridor to Tern. The twenty-nine ships between control volumes had found stable ordinary-space drift, but the nearest charted approach lay months away at fusion speed.

Demir placed those groups on the manual plot. “One third,” he said.

Irian checked the population and vessel counts. Depending on whether the between-volume ships could rendezvous, the fraction varied by two points. The useful truth did not.

One third of her convoy had fallen outside every fixed corridor.

Halvek requested command coordination. Irian left the request unanswered while she reviewed air, water, and tug assignments for the stranded ships. The manual slates spread across her bridge in dozens of incompatible hands. Each carried a piece of where the convoy had gone.

She ordered blank slates copied to every remaining helm.

On the central plot, Demir removed the last automatic route line. What remained looked less like a convoy than scattered work marks across empty space. Irian fastened her harness tighter and began pairing every mark with another ship that could see it.

Chapter 09 — The Wake Map

Tomis's torque key opened along a seam Kessa had never noticed.

She sat on Threadbare's repair-gallery deck with the brass shaft clamped between her boots. Charter seals covered the outer lock. A security grapple held the tug against Nacre's evidence berth. Every legal tool aboard had been tagged, indexed, and disabled through its network bond. The key remained in her hands because an inspector had classified it as personal memorial hardware.

Tomis would have objected to the word memorial. He had used the key to open compressor housings, strike frozen latches, and cheat old diagnostics into accepting manual calibration. Its edges carried more machine oil than reverence.

Anu knelt opposite Kessa with a probe wire threaded through the hollow shaft. “Rotate left by one tooth.”

“There are no teeth.”

“Then by whatever amount makes your hand stop arguing.”

Kessa turned the collar. A sliver of brass lifted beneath her thumb.

Inside lay a second diagnostic cavity, narrower than the one Pell had used to hide Nacre's raw sensor spool on Day 1. Its contacts had oxidized black. Anu touched the probe to them and brought up a memory map on the specimen bench.

Two archives appeared.

The first held Nacre-9's rejected gradients and station timing. The second carried an unabridged Lark-3 service header dated twelve years earlier. Tomis Rho's apprentice identification occupied the author field.

Kessa stopped turning the key.

Neither woman spoke. Ventilation pushed citrus solvent through the gallery, failing to cover the smell of burned insulation from Tern. The tug's patched drum remained still. Charter impound had shut down spin to prevent crew movement, leaving everyone in berth gravity supplied through the lock.

“How much?” Kessa asked.

Anu counted sectors. “Forty-eight minutes of cage telemetry. Maintenance channel. Local load traces. One complete command log.”

“The copy we released had nine minutes.”

“Tomis kept the rest here.”

The answer cut cleanly because Anu did not soften it.

Kessa connected the cavity to an isolated slate. Lark-3 opened as rows of obsolete diagnostics. The command extract already in Charter evidence had established Tomis's hold reductions and evacuation order. Halvek's provisional correction removed operator error from the finding. This larger archive carried something the correction did not: physical load before any command.

Three beats crossed the outer resonators seven minutes before the official fault time.

Each carried the same rise, tremble, and release Kessa had felt through Nacre's grapple line. The interval between pulses differed. Lark's beats arrived closer together, compressed by a cage already drawing more power than its public log admitted.

Anu laid Tern's surviving trace beside it. “Do not align by station clock. Align by the first outward filament response.”

“Tern lost nine seconds during release.”

“Which clock?”

“All of them.”

“Then use physical sequence.”

Kessa marked the first frost displacement on Tern-Beta Four, the first load at Nacre's fairlead, and Lark's first unclassified gradient. The traces settled into three columns. What had looked like separate failures shared one internal shape.

Tomis's log included a maintenance voice channel.

Kessa muted it before sound emerged.

Anu waited. “We need the timing.”

“We have timing.”

“We have station response. The full maintenance channel may identify what the crew saw before the command extract begins.”

“I heard the extract.”

“It begins after the first load.”

Kessa's left palm pressed against the old scar. She had accepted the report because it converted Tomis's death into work she understood. An apprentice misread a fluctuation. A senior routebreaker failed to teach him. A cage corrected. The dead could be assigned to causes, and causes could be prevented with better hands.

She restored the channel.

Static filled the bench speaker. Tomis sounded younger than her memory had allowed.

“Lark control, the center is pushing across the outer feeds. This isn't thermal.”

A supervisor told him to verify compensation.

“I did. The load came before our correction. Evacuate the wheel.”

Another voice called the order premature. Then three impacts struck the recording, spaced at the same interval as the raw gradient.

Tomis said, “Something answered from route north. Get them off the wheel.”

An automatic system announced HOLD COMMAND ACCEPTED.

The cage response climbed. Metal failed. The channel ended under noise.

Kessa shut off the speaker without touching the file.

Anu looked at the twelve-year-old trace. “He read it correctly.”

“He ordered evacuation.”

“Yes.”

“The finding says he applied a local correction before authority transferred.”

“The command log shows an automatic hold overriding him.”

Kessa removed her hand from the key. Brass had marked her palm with a line of old oil. “Save the voice separately. We map first.”

Working spared no one, but it gave grief edges.

They needed more than three failures. Nacre, Tern, and Lark-3 could form a pattern because two frightened people wanted one. Kessa opened the archive seizure copies Charter had placed aboard while inspectors searched the tug. Halvek's people had gathered telemetry from every cage that showed an unexplained excursion, intending to prove equipment commonality. They had stored the files on Threadbare's own bench and trusted the impound bond to keep Kessa out.

The specimen bench had never respected network bonds.

Anu routed its microscopy controller through the old diagnostic cavity. The archive opened one station at a time.

They searched all 73 anchors for the three-beat load. Public logs contained none. Raw maintenance buffers retained fragments at twelve anchors because local instruments had classified them as frost shift, cable recoil, clock error, or operator contact. The oldest event had occurred at Sable's inner cage. The newest preceded Tern by less than an hour.

Kessa placed the twelve anchors on a route chart.

Nacre and Tern sat adjacent. Lark-3 lay two nodes behind Nacre on a corridor the Charter had sealed after its failure. Brine, Orison Gap, and Sable curved away along different commercial routes. On the trade map they formed no useful line. Cargo algorithms crossed the Reach by demand, not by physical proximity outside corridors.

“Remove human routes,” Anu said.

Kessa blanked trade connections, transit times, and corridor ownership. Twelve anchor positions remained against the Reach's actual stars.

The marks formed a broad curve.

Not a perfect one. Space offered no drafting table. Yet each three-beat event followed the next by position along that curve, while event timing ignored the short cuts humans had built between cages.

“A failure cascade should follow shared control links,” Kessa said.

“Or hardware age. Or common maintenance.”

“These do none of them.”

Anu expanded the event times. “Treat each juvenile response as a receiving point.”

“Receiving what?”

“A changing gravitational field. Do not promote it to communication.”

Kessa built a propagation model using the physical distance between anchors. The first attempt scattered the events across six days. The second required impossible speed changes. On the third, Anu corrected for each cage's hold strength. Stronger holds delayed visible response. Weaker cages moved earlier and left cleaner traces.

The twelve points aligned.

A pulse had traveled along the curve. The juveniles had not failed in sequence. They had answered the same migration condition at different times under different restraint.

Kessa stared at the chart. “A wake map.”

Anu frowned at the phrase.

“Not a message. A path left by the load.”

“That I can tolerate.”

She labeled the file WAKE MAP, MODEL 1.

One clean curve did not satisfy Kessa. She broke the model on purpose.

First she removed Lark-3. The remaining points still followed the physical arc, but the timing window widened by nine hours. Next she removed Tern. The model predicted its response within eleven minutes of the recorded first load. Nacre mattered less to the fit than she expected. It mattered more to them because they had touched it first.

“Swap the hold values between Sable and Brine,” she said.

Anu did. The sequence failed. Two juvenile responses landed in reverse order, and the model demanded a pulse that accelerated between anchors without a corresponding change in field strength.

“Use public values only.”

“That failed before.”

“I want to know how.”

Published hold records spread the twelve events into a false cascade. The earliest apparent failure occurred at the oldest cage. Later events followed maintenance dates closely enough to support Halvek's equipment theory. Kessa opened the underlying power draws. Every station had reported lower restraint than it actually applied. The difference increased whenever local controllers received a three-beat anomaly.

“They hid the response by tightening,” she said.

“Possibly without knowing what they hid. Automatic correction would do this.”

“Someone wrote the automatic correction.”

“Intent does not change our timing.”

Kessa accepted the correction and returned to the work. They replaced official power with measured draw wherever raw buffers survived. Six anchors had complete records. Four retained partial feeds. Two offered only thermal rejection, forcing Anu to infer resonator load from waste heat.

The wake curve tightened again.

A second test used direction. If the events came from linked control failure, command traffic should lead physical response. At eight anchors, local cable strain preceded the first control packet. At three, station clocks lacked enough resolution. Lark-3 provided the deciding trace: Tomis had marked outward load thirty-seven seconds before Charter synchronization reached the cage.

Kessa listened to that segment without playing his voice. A maintenance stylus scratched against metal. Suit fabric moved. Then the first sensor tone arrived. Those small sounds fixed his body in a place the report had erased.

“Physical before network,” she said.

Anu entered the result beneath the chart. “Compatible with one external migration pulse crossing juvenile positions. Still not proof of an adult.”

“No. It proves the failure-cascade model cannot explain order without inventing a second clock.”

“Halvek may prefer the second clock.”

“Then we make him name it.”

They saved every test, including the ones that weakened their claim. Kessa added error bars large enough to offend anyone selling certainty. Even with those margins, the twelve anchors remained a chain. Whatever traveled along it did not care which human corridor made cargo fastest.

A knock sounded through the locked repair-gallery hatch. Sena floated on the far side of its narrow window, one shoulder hooked under a berth restraint.

“Security inspection in fourteen minutes,” she said. “They have decided the burned insulation might conceal moral intent.”

“We need the forward cupola.”

“You need a ship that can power its sensors.”

Kessa held up the torque key. “Can you give me one?”

Sena's eyes narrowed. “Lawful, quiet, or effective?”

“Effective for twenty minutes.”

“That excludes lawful.”

She pushed away from the window.

Anu opened the Tern release trace. Its final movement extended beyond the twelve anchors, a gradient tail caught by Threadbare as the juvenile vanished into open space. The tail did not point along any fixed corridor. It curved toward the next point on their map.

“If the juvenile follows the pulse path, what made the path?” Kessa asked.

“Something with a larger effective lens body.”

“How much larger?”

“We cannot derive mass from one wake.”

“Use cage responses.”

Anu objected for six minutes. Then she did it.

They treated every hold correction as a crude measuring instrument. Each cage had resisted the distant field before its juvenile moved. The effect weakened by position, returned in periodic troughs, and grew across the most recent events. Kessa supplied resonator loads. Anu supplied biological limits derived from the Nacre filament. Neither trusted the other's assumptions, which improved the model.

A body capable of generating the measured field would extend its lens topology across tens of thousands of kilometres.

Kessa reduced the scale twice. The data pushed it back.

“Adult stage,” Anu said at last. “Candidate only. Not proof.”

“Returning along the curve.”

“If the curve represents migration.”

“When?”

The estimate depended on how much the cages delayed each response. Kessa ran minimum hold, published hold, and measured hold. Published values produced nonsense. Measured values narrowed arrival to a six-day window.

Its center landed thirty days from Day 12.

Kessa checked the arithmetic through a second tool. “Day 42.”

“Plus or minus three days.”

“Tern did not give us three days of warning before it opened.”

“Tern had a Charter hold and an incompatible cut.”

The dead entered the room without sound. Sixty-three people reduced to roster files on Halvek's cruiser and damage marks in Threadbare's gallery. Kessa had made the cut. Halvek had maintained the hold. The juvenile had followed a biological pressure neither of them understood. Causes did not divide cleanly enough to let her step outside them.

Sena restored local power.

The repair gallery dropped as Threadbare's drum began turning. Lights flickered. Somewhere aft, Pell applauded once before Vasken told him to save it for escape.

“Forward sensors for eleven minutes,” Sena said over comms. “After that the berth notices our power draw.”

Kessa and Anu carried the isolated slate to the cupola. Charter seals crossed both acceleration couches. Sena had cut around hers so neatly that the torn strip still appeared intact from the hatch.

Nacre-9 lay beyond the berth structures, its cage bright under reinforced output. All nine stations projected hold power into the juvenile. Two security cutters patrolled the wheel. The supposedly stabilized center pushed one duplicated star outward by a visible fraction.

Anu loaded the wake map onto the manual console. “If our delay correction is right, the hold does more than suppress motion.”

“It stores the difference.”

“Biological release continues underneath. Cage geometry keeps dragging it back.”

Kessa overlaid Tern's accumulating excess. The curve matched. Every forced correction had increased the energy available when the cage lost control.

Each hold stored more violence for the larger body's arrival.

She sent the model to Halvek under emergency engineering priority. He answered from Continuity within four minutes. His face occupied the central display, composed against a bridge still carrying Tern damage alerts.

“You accessed seized records,” he said.

“I accessed my brother's tool and data stored aboard my ship.”

“Your ship remains Charter evidence.”

“So do sixty-three bodies. Read the map.”

Halvek looked past her toward someone off display. “My analysts see an extrapolation built from mislabeled maintenance noise.”

“Twelve anchors carry the same three-beat load in physical order.”

“Your sequence depends on estimated hold delay.”

“Tern measured the delay by killing everyone near the cage.”

His expression tightened. “Do not use casualties to substitute for validation.”

“Then validate it. Remove one hold and measure the relaxation.”

“After Tern, no responsible authority will weaken another cage on your recommendation.”

Anu entered the frame. “Reinforcement increases stored gradient. That claim comes from your own resonator response, not from animal interpretation.”

“We have millions dependent on corridor stability and no tested alternative.”

“Your corridor stability has begun moving,” Kessa said.

Halvek brought up a new order. Twelve anchor designations filled it. “All cages in the proposed chain will increase reserve power and maintain continuous hold. Local crews will inspect for control drift. Threadbare remains impounded pending inquiry.”

The order propagated while they watched. Nacre acknowledged first. Sable accepted four seconds later and requested authority to exceed the reserve increase if its inner cage moved. Brine's local controller asked whether the instruction applied during food-transfer traffic. Orison Gap reported insufficient coolant, then accepted after Charter logistics promised a tanker. Each acknowledgment added a hard blue ring around one point on Kessa's wake map.

She opened engineering projections for the commanded increase. At Nacre, two more percent would consume the thermal margin by the next pulse trough. Sable already ran beyond published output. Tern had taught the control system nothing because no cage remained there to receive the update.

“Your analysts can see those margins,” Kessa said.

“They see active routes carrying medical and evacuation traffic.”

“Those routes now sit on twelve loaded releases.”

“Your phrase assumes the conclusion.”

Kessa transmitted the raw Tern ratchet trace again, this time with each cut step removed. The hold corrections alone still climbed after the juvenile's physical response. “No cut in that segment. No collision. The cage adds energy each time the animal moves.”

Halvek glanced away. His staff had put another display beyond the camera, close enough for its pale reflections to cross his cheek. “A correlation under uncontrolled conditions.”

“You created the conditions.”

“I inherited a network carrying civilization.”

Around his face, the blue rings continued appearing. Ten anchors acknowledged. Eleven. The last delay came from Brine, where a local engineer had attached a request for worker review. Charter command overrode the request and marked the order accepted.

“You are tightening every restraint before the return.”

“I am preventing another uncontrolled breach.”

Kessa looked at the date on his order. Day 12. Thirty days remained if their model held. Less if the cages had hidden more delay than Anu estimated.

“Marshal, Tomis ordered Lark-3 evacuated before the automatic hold overrode him.”

For the first time, Halvek's gaze shifted to the brass key in her hand.

“You have the local trace beyond the corrected command extract?”

“I have forty-eight minutes. It shows the physical load arriving before any control action.”

“Transmit those channels.”

“After you suspend the reinforcement order.”

“You are withholding safety evidence.”

“I learned the procedure from you.”

She ended the call.

Sena locked the cockpit hatch. “That exchange shortened our lawful future.”

“Can we leave the berth?”

“The security grapple carries a bonded release. Our drives remain tagged. Berth control owns the outer doors.”

Kessa inserted Tomis's torque key into the manual console's obsolete service slot. It did not fit by design. Brass edges caught on the newer collar.

She turned it anyway.

The key bridged two contacts. Manual maintenance authority opened for a half second, long enough for Sena to isolate the drive tags. Pell killed the tug's external network. Vasken released the security grapple by firing drum two against its hinge rather than its bonded latch.

An alarm spread through the berth.

Crew voices filled the ship channel. Some demanded an explanation. Others reported seals, pressure, and power. Kessa did not ask for loyalty while the outer door remained closed.

“Stations only,” she told them. “Nobody has consented to a destination. We decide that after Sena gets us clear.”

Berth control denied departure. Sena opened both grapple drums and caught the inner door rails. Threadbare pulled herself forward while the drums reeled, an eighty-three-metre tug climbing through an impound lock by her hands.

The door protested in red across every display.

Kessa reached the exterior release cabinet and used the torque key as a striker. The manual pins withdrew one at a time. Vacuum took the remaining pressure. The outer doors separated by two metres, four, then enough for Sena to turn the tug on edge and scrape through.

A security cutter moved to block them.

“Do not ram it,” Kessa said.

“I appreciate specific limits.” Sena rolled Threadbare beneath the cutter's exclusion cone and used the berth structure to hide their first burn. “Where?”

Twelve anchor points glowed on the manual console. Four lay within the tug's unassisted fuel reach. Every one carried a continuous hold.

Kessa fastened Tomis's key beside the controls, no longer closed around its hidden seam.

“Out of Nacre first,” she said. “Then we unfasten the chain.”

Threadbare cleared the evidence berth as its doors jammed behind them. Ahead, Nacre-9 tightened under nine reinforced beams, and the wake map counted thirty days toward something large enough to bend the Reach.

Act 2

Chapter 10 — A Ship Called Threadbare

Kessa crawled into Threadbare's port grapple drum while Sena kept the tug hidden behind Nacre's ore moon.

The stolen cage keys rode in a tool roll tied to her ankle. Nine ceramic wafers, each the size of a thumbnail, knocked together whenever she pulled herself through the narrow housing. Charter technicians used them to open resonator service locks without waiting for local control. Vasken had lifted the roll from the impound team during Tern decontamination and told no one until the evidence-berth alarm began.

Possession could cost every person aboard a route license. Use could bring prison.

The drum offered a more immediate problem. Its emergency release had bent when Sena tore the security grapple off Threadbare. One jaw remained half open. Unless Kessa reset it by hand, the tug had one working grapple for any cutting job ahead.

She wedged her shoulder beneath the guide plate and pushed.

Metal shifted by a finger-width. Pain drew a hot line across her left palm. The old resonator scar had opened under the glove liner at Tern and sealed badly. Kessa ignored it until blood made the guide slick.

“Chief,” Pell called through the drum wall. “Crew count begins in twelve minutes.”

“Call it a meeting.”

“Sena calls it a consent muster.”

“Sena enjoys words that prevent me from leaving.”

“Thirteen minutes now.”

Kessa braced both boots and forced the plate into its track. The jaw closed with a flat mechanical knock. No ghost load followed. No three-beat response moved through the tug. Behind the moon, Nacre-9 remained out of sight and held tighter than before.

She backed out of the drum with the cage keys still tied to her ankle.

All nineteen crew fit in Threadbare's centrifugal mess only by using the walls. The patched drum turned slowly to save power, giving them one third of standard gravity. Jori occupied a medical sling beside the galley hatch, his broken arm fixed across his chest. Anu stood near the repair-gallery door because she did not belong to the crew count and refused to pretend otherwise.

Sena had placed the ship covenant on the central table. Its display showed no proposed destination.

Vasken noticed the tool roll first. “You brought the keys.”

“I brought them to the people who would be charged for having them.”

Pell looked around the crowded room. “Should we admire the distinction?”

“No.” Kessa set the roll beside the covenant. “You should know exactly what we stole before anyone agrees to fly with it.”

She listed the damage without reducing it to a speech. Both grapple drums worked, but port release required manual confirmation. Their cold-shear cutter had no lawful tool bond. Reactor output remained good. Fuel stood at sixty-eight percent after Tern and the impound escape. No Charter berth would service them. Nacre control had revoked water, repair, and docking access across the network.

Then she showed the wake map.

Twelve anchors curved across the mess display. Blue rings marked Halvek's reinforcement order. A moving field estimate approached the chain with thirty days remaining.

“Candidate adult veilback,” Anu said from the wall. “Candidate matters. We have measured one large lens body through indirect effects. We have not observed it.”

Kessa enlarged Tern's stored-gradient trace. “This part needs no candidate. A hold that opposes biological release accumulates stress. The cages are adding restraint at all twelve anchors.”

Jori shifted in his sling. “And you plan to cut them.”

“I plan to find out whether local crews carry the same load. If they do, I want to stop another Tern.”

“That isn't an answer.”

“No.” Kessa looked at the sling around his arm. “I do not know how to release a juvenile safely. The cold-shear sequence failed under a hold. Anu thinks tissue response may show us another order, but she does not have enough data. We can inspect cages, share the Tern trace, and refuse any cut while a hold remains active.”

A drive mechanic named Hara Senn folded her hands on the table. “Halvek says your cut caused the breach.”

“My cut opposed his hold. I made it. He maintained his system after I stopped. Both actions entered the failure.”

“Would you make the cut again?”

“Not the same one.”

“Would you make any cut without Charter authority?”

Kessa considered the cage keys. “Yes, if the crew at that anchor requests it and we can remove the hold. Not before we understand more.”

The room remained quiet enough for the drum bearings to carry through the deck. Kessa wanted to tell them that good hands could solve it. She wanted to turn uncertainty into a repair schedule and loyalty into a resource already aboard. Both desires came from the lie she had used since Tomis died.

Sena tapped the covenant. “This is not a majority vote that binds dissenters. Each person chooses whether to continue beyond Nacre's local volume. Anyone who declines keeps earned share and receives transfer on the neutral ore tender at the moon's far depot. It arrives in fifty-one minutes.”

“The tender will report us,” Vasken said.

“It already knows we're here.”

Kessa looked at Sena. “You arranged a transfer before the meeting.”

“I arranged a real choice.”

That correction belonged among the expensive ones.

Pell opened the first consent line. “What are we consenting to? ‘Unfasten the chain’ has poor contract language.”

Kessa entered terms one by one. They would carry Anu and the evidence to any route crew requesting inspection. They would not approach a cage under active Charter targeting if a worker aboard voted the approach unsafe. No one would be ordered outside for an unauthorized cut. Local crews controlled their own hardware. Threadbare would publish every measurement and keep no exclusive release method. Any crew member could withdraw at the next physical transfer point.

“And if there is no transfer point?” Hara asked.

“Then we use a shuttle, tug, or occupied station that accepts you. We do not leave anyone in a capsule to make the sentence true.”

Jori lifted his good hand. “Medical authority says I cannot work exterior.”

Anu said, “Medical authority sits here and says the same.”

“Do I count as crew if I cannot do my berth?”

Kessa answered before anyone could make usefulness a condition. “Yes.”

Sena added a line assigning Jori to internal load monitoring until cleared. “Consent does not require pretending you're uninjured.”

The covenant circulated around the table.

Nobody signed quickly. Vasken read every clause twice. Hara asked whether the ship's debts would pass to families if Charter seized their accounts. Pell wanted raw evidence attached rather than summarized. Two junior riggers left the mess to call dependents through the ore tender's relay, then came back with faces Kessa did not try to interpret.

Anu signed as passenger and scientific authority, not crew.

Kessa waited last. Seventeen crew marks appeared. Sena's joined them. Jori held the slate against his sling and entered the nineteenth with his off hand.

No one chose transfer.

The ore tender still came alongside. Its captain extended a personnel tube and waited because Sena had paid for the option. Kessa opened both hatches and left the passage lit for five full minutes.

Hara stood nearest it. Her partner worked a pressure plant on Nacre and had sent one message before Charter blocked the relay: Choose the ship only if they let you choose. Hara read the line to the mess without asking anyone to answer. Then she closed the tube's inner hatch herself.

One junior rigger, Deni Sol, kept his bag packed at his boots. “I signed for inspection,” he said. “Not sabotage.”

“So did I,” Jori replied. “The difference may become inconvenient.”

Kessa faced Deni. “If I order sabotage, invoke the unsafe-work clause and stop me.”

“Against the chief and co-owner?”

“Against whoever gives the order.”

Sena entered that sentence as an amendment to the covenant. Deni approved it and slid his bag beneath the bench, still packed.

The tender captain requested separation. Before Sena closed the outer lock, Kessa transferred a copy of the wake evidence and the crew's consent terms into its public mail queue. If Threadbare vanished, their departure would not become a Charter story about hostages or a chief fleeing discipline alone.

Mooring seals released. The personnel tube withdrew. Nineteen crew remained because each had passed an open hatch after seeing a route back.

The result did not feel like absolution. It felt like nineteen people placing their own names beside a risk Kessa could no longer call hers alone.

She signed the chief's line.

Sena closed the covenant and immediately opened the fuel plot. “Now for the part where consent meets range.”

Twelve anchor points spread across the table. Sena drew four reachable arcs from their present fuel state. Nacre sat behind them. Lark-3 had no functioning corridor and no service mass. Brine lay three days away if they took a minor transfer route before Charter closed it. Orison Gap required refueling. Sable lay beyond two corridors they could not trust.

“With reserve for approach, failed grapple recovery, and leaving each cage,” Sena said, “we can reach four of twelve.”

Pell leaned over the plot. “Five if we stop heating the aft bunks.”

“Four if we intend to arrive with crew.”

“We own blankets.”

“Your optimism consumes oxygen too.”

Sena made them price each anchor in actual work. The nearest required a standard approach but no legal coupling, which meant Threadbare would spend fuel matching drift while Kessa opened the service lock manually. The second lay behind a corridor whose receiving cage had begun throwing clock errors. Reaching the third required a braking burn their port engine could perform once before replacing its throat liner. Brine consumed the smallest useful margin and placed them beside food traffic already threatened by closure.

Kessa opened the tool inventory. One cold-shear cutter remained aboard. Its thermal pack had survived Tern. The severed control bus had not. They owned two spare ceramic jaws, six metres of field braid, three isolation forks, and enough suit propellant for fourteen full exterior shifts if nobody needed rescue.

“That count assumes Jori stays inside,” Hara said.

“It assumes every exterior pair carries reserve for a third suit,” Kessa replied. “After Tern, we do not budget retrieval as an exception.”

Vasken rolled the stolen cage keys across his palm. “Nine wafers. Twelve cages.”

“Local crews should have their own.”

“Local crews may have had them seized.”

“Then we use one wafer per service system and preserve the serial records.”

Pell brought up water. Impound had left their main tanks at seventy-one percent. The recycler's membrane carried a tear he had repaired with galley filter mesh. It would hold for days, not weeks.

“The ship can reach four anchors,” he said. “The people can reach two unless somebody sells us water.”

Anu looked toward the specimen bench. “The filament cultures require less than one person's ration.”

“No one suggested dumping them,” Pell said.

“You looked at their line.”

“I look at every line that leaks.”

Kessa separated scientific water from domestic load rather than letting the argument harden. The cultures might reveal why Nacre's tissue closed after cutting. They also occupied a resource every crewmember drank. Both facts belonged on the same slate.

By the end of the inventory, four reachable anchors had narrowed to one sensible first destination. Brine offered a requesting crew, local service access, food substrate, and enough habitat industry to trade for water or a throat liner. It also carried six point two million people around a cage Halvek had ordered reinforced.

Kessa moved fuel between scenarios. Abandoning the cold-shear cutter gained almost nothing. Its mass mattered less than the propellant needed to correct in unstable lens geometry. Trading spare radiator panels could buy fuel at a sympathetic station, if any station accepted them. No lawful berth meant no standard tank couplings, no certified water, and no repairs beyond what nineteen hands could perform aboard.

“The chain cannot be unfastened by one tug,” Vasken said.

“No,” Kessa replied.

“So what did we consent to?”

“Finding out whether other crews already know part of the answer.”

She opened a draft broadcast.

Her first version contained Tern's casualty trace, the wake map, Tomis's Lark-3 log, and a demand that routekeepers reject Charter authority. It sounded decisive. It also asked workers to risk their stations and families on Kessa's conclusion before they could test it.

She deleted the demand.

The second version called the creatures juvenile veilbacks and described the approaching lens body. Anu removed both claims.

“You have evidence for juvenile tissue at Nacre,” Kessa said.

“I have evidence for a regenerative filament associated with the hollow. We need local crews to inspect their own cages, not agree with my classification.”

Sena crossed out migration chain. “You want answers from people who still think Tern came from your cutter.”

“It came partly from my cutter.”

“Then do not make them accept your innocence before they answer a gauge question.”

Kessa stared at the empty message field. Repair work began with the condition nearest the hand. Not who owned the machine. Not what failure meant for civilization. First find the load.

She typed one question.

Does your cage carry the three-beat load?

Below it she attached a ten-second vibration trace from Nacre, the matching raw trace from Lark-3, and the Tern segment recorded before any cut began. Anu added instructions for separating physical strain from control traffic. Sena included an address that bounced through old union maintenance relays rather than Charter routing.

Pell read the message. “No accusation.”

“No.”

“No plan.”

“Not until we have one.”

“No stirring statement about roads built on bodies.”

Kessa looked at him.

“I can write one if morale requires it,” he said.

“Repair the water recycler.”

“Cruel but lawful under the covenant.”

They sent the broadcast from behind the ore moon.

Nacre security found them four minutes later. Two cutters cleared the moon's northern limb on intercept burns. Their weapons remained cold, but targeting cones spread across Threadbare's escape path.

Sena had predicted the approach. She rolled the tug close to the ore tender's thermal wake, then burned toward the minor transfer corridor with the tender between them and direct fire.

The tender captain called in outrage. “You said no fighting near my hull.”

“We are not fighting,” Sena said.

“The Charter cutters look emotionally prepared.”

Kessa opened the cutter channel. “Nineteen crew and one civilian scientist aboard. We are under independent ship covenant. Any grapple attempt near the tender risks collision.”

“Cut thrust and surrender stolen Charter property.”

Kessa put the ceramic cage keys on camera. “These open worker service locks. If you want to argue ownership, begin with the workers.”

The lead cutter accelerated.

Sena fired Threadbare's starboard grapple into the ore tender's discarded slag mass, reeled hard, and swung the tug around it. The cutter followed the expected thrust line. Threadbare crossed beneath its cone and released the slag. For several seconds the cutters had to choose between pursuit and avoiding fifty tonnes of molten tailings.

They chose their radiators.

The minor corridor opened ahead as a thin lens between two utility anchors. Its control system denied Threadbare authorization. Kessa inserted one stolen cage key into the manual reader.

SERVICE ACCESS recognized the wafer. The throat widened enough for one tug.

“Using it records the serial,” Vasken said from load control.

“They know our name.”

“I meant the worker who loses it.”

Kessa hesitated. The wafer carried a local issue code from Nacre. Someone on the cage crew had signed for it.

She copied the access log, added her own authorization, and transmitted the record publicly before crossing. Theft remained theft, but she would not let Charter place the consequence on an unnamed worker.

Threadbare entered the corridor.

Stars drew into paired arcs around the cupola. The drum bearings changed pitch. Every crew station reported manual readiness. No fixed-route passage had ever felt so much like a door they might not deserve to use.

Kessa kept her hand near the drive cut until ordinary stars returned on the far side.

The first answer waited in their maintenance relay.

A routekeeper at Glass Minor had laid a palm sensor on an outer feed and recorded three measured impacts. The crew attached raw clocks and no interpretation. A second report came from a decommissioning team near Sable. Their cage carried the load beneath continuous hold, strongest after each Charter correction.

More answers arrived as Threadbare moved into ordinary flight.

One crew accused Kessa of manufacturing the trace and still sent its data for comparison. Another had heard three knocks through a thermal conduit for six months. A fifth reported branching frost at two resonators. The sixth local crew offered complete unaveraged station records under the condition that no names appear until their families left Charter housing.

Anu built each trace into the wake map. All six matched the physical sequence within their instrument limits.

None offered a safe cut.

Kessa had not asked for one.

A seventh packet arrived from Brine. Condensation blurred the sender's camera. Behind the routekeeper, green algae light reflected from a habitat transfer spine. The local cage trace showed three beats, a pause, then a hold correction strong enough to drive the outer feeds past thermal margin.

The routekeeper looked directly into the lens.

“We carry it,” she said. “Food traffic has us locked under continuous hold, and the juvenile is moving. If Threadbare can cut without making another Tern, come to Brine.”

The packet ended with approach data and a local service key.

Sena laid in the three-day burn. Fuel reserve dropped across the plot, leaving the other eight anchors beyond anything one tug could reach.

Kessa touched the open seam in Tomis's torque key. Six crews had given them data. Brine had given them a place where delay already had a temperature.

“Accept the approach,” she said.

Threadbare turned toward Brine with nineteen crew aboard, no lawful berth behind her, and a question traveling faster than the ship could follow.

Chapter 11 — Brine Habitat

Condensation struck Accord of Dust's docking collar hard enough to sound like rain.

Irian listened from the transfer control bay while Brine's outer cylinder rolled past the port. Green algae lamps shone through wet glass. Agricultural drums occupied every orbit around the red dwarf, dozens of long habitats threaded together by transfer spines and cargo loops. Their cooling skins shed water vapor into capture nets. From a distance the swarm had looked lush. Up close, every surface carried patched seals, mineral streaks, and painted capacity marks.

Brine grew food for systems that referred to it as substrate capacity.

Its docking controller denied Accord's first cargo request.

“Priority evacuation convoy,” Irian said. “Fourteen mobile kitchens reach reserve limit in thirty-one hours. We require sixteen thousand tonnes of food substrate, water concentrate, and nutrient cultures.”

A Brine coordinator appeared on the bay display. Lio Varen had silver hair cut to the scalp and condensation beaded across his collar. The room behind him contained algae pipes rather than a command table.

“We received your requirement,” he said.

“Then open freight access.”

“No.”

Irian checked the channel header in case some negotiation layer had failed. “Brine holds a Charter emergency reserve sized for three convoy transits.”

“It held that reserve before the anchor began moving.”

“We carry four hundred ten thousand evacuees.”

“Brine houses six point two million people.”

The answer entered the bay like a shifted load. Irian had not forgotten Brine's population. She had placed it in a separate column: local demand, protected by local stores. Her convoy occupied the urgent column because it moved and consumed reserve without replacement.

Varen sent a transfer schedule. “We will distribute food substrate when your convoy accepts thirty-eight thousand Brine residents and moves six vulnerable cylinders out of the anchor plane.”

Irian enlarged the list. Dialysis wards. Two old residential drums with failing radiation shells. A school cylinder whose drive collars had been removed for parts. Twelve thousand agricultural workers housed along the transfer spines nearest the cage.

“You are conditioning emergency food on transport,” she said.

“We are conditioning exported food on shared evacuation.”

“Those cylinders cannot attach to Accord without load review.”

“Then review them.”

“My convoy remains split. One third has no fixed corridor. We lost fuel recovering a hospital at Tern. Halvek offers a departure sequence in eighteen hours if Brine releases our cargo now.”

Varen wiped moisture from his jaw. “Your departure sequence crosses our anchor while our least mobile residents remain beside it.”

“We cannot absorb every threatened habitat along the route.”

“No. You can absorb the people whose food you want.”

The channel ended without a docking assignment.

Irian stood over the dark display until Marl Quade entered the bay. He wore Brine condensation on his shoulders and carried a paper band around one wrist, local access issued without connection to the Charter network.

“You went ashore without clearance,” she said.

“I went through a tube they opened.”

“They refused our freight request.”

“They refused your supply-stop request.”

Irian turned toward him. “Our kitchens have thirty-one hours.”

“Brine's outer spines may have days.”

“We cannot plan a mass carrier around every local demand.”

Marl set a wet glove on the console. “You called this habitat a supply stop before we docked. People here heard you. They spend their lives sending food into route schedules written elsewhere. Now the routes are breaking, and the first emergency fleet to arrive asks them to load our holds and remain under the cage.”

“Their reserve exists for transit failures.”

“They have one.”

Irian looked through the port. A transfer spine passed beneath Accord's collar, crowded with handcarts and people in green work coats. Condensation made the outer windows opaque in patches. Beyond them, Brine's anchor bent the red dwarf into a dark-edged crescent.

“Thirty-eight thousand residents alter life support, berth, and fuel.”

“So does food substrate.”

“You think I should accept the condition.”

“I think you should stop treating their people as an unexpected surcharge on cargo already owed to us.”

Marl rarely raised his voice. He did not now. That made the correction harder to classify as anger and easier to recognize as accurate.

Irian reopened Varen's schedule. “Six vulnerable cylinders cannot all ride Accord's truss.”

“Brine knows that.”

“Then why ask?”

“To find out whether a convoy means one carrier or many ships.”

The phrase touched the same defect Tern had exposed. Irian could move mass through a planned route. She could not pull every module herself once geometry failed. Manual slates had kept the hospital alive because many ships reported what they saw. Brine wanted the same distribution applied to people.

“Convene passenger delegates,” she said.

Marl did not move. “For what question?”

“Taking Brine residents aboard and changing departure.”

“Under which authority?”

“Emergency convoy command.”

“That gives you rescue authority for immediate life threat. Destination changes require the Free Convoy Articles.”

Irian knew the Articles. She had signed them at departure because no civilian convoy of this size could sail on naval precedent alone. Captains retained ship safety. Passenger delegates held consent over uncharted mass movement, abandonment of inhabited modules, and destination changes beyond published emergency ports.

Brine appeared on the published route as a supply stop, not a destination.

“We are not choosing a final destination,” she said.

“We are choosing whether thousands board ships that expected to leave in eighteen hours.”

“The anchor may fail before a full delegate session finishes.”

“Then give them a deadline and the information.”

Irian disliked procedures that consumed the margin needed to carry them out. She disliked unilateral command more when someone forced her to name it.

“Two-hour session,” she said. “Ship captains and passenger delegates. Brine sends representatives. Raw capacity tables stay open.”

Marl removed his glove from the console. A wet handprint remained. “Now you have a question.”

The assembly met across eleven linked rooms because Accord had no chamber large enough for every delegate. Faces occupied the logistics pit wall in a grid. Captains joined from bridges. Passenger delegates spoke from habitat messes, clinic bays, machine shops, and one agricultural drum where people stood ankle-deep in loose soil after Tern's burn.

Lio Varen appeared from the algae-pipe room.

Irian presented capacity first. The main convoy segment could take thirty-eight thousand people if it converted freight berths, doubled six habitat occupancy limits, and moved water bladders outside pressure. Six Brine cylinders required eighteen tugs. Eleven were available without stripping tow from weak convoy habitats. The remaining seven could come from Brine if local crews accepted fuel support later.

Then she presented cost. Taking the cylinders delayed departure by at least thirty-six hours. Halvek's timetable closed in eighteen. Missing it removed the Charter guarantee of two stabilized corridors toward Orison staging. Fuel after the hospital rescue allowed no rapid alternative.

A delegate from Kindly Argument asked, “What does Charter guarantee mean after Tern?”

“Halvek commits cutters, route clearance, and reserved transit order,” Irian said. “He cannot guarantee cage geometry.”

“Can anyone?”

“No.”

A freight captain leaned close to his camera. “We carry machine seed for three systems. If we take local residents, their cylinders displace protected cargo.”

Varen answered, “If you leave without substrate, your seed arrives with hungry operators.”

“That reserve belongs under intersystem emergency compact.”

“It belongs in Brine tanks maintained by Brine workers.”

Marl called the next speaker before ownership could consume the question.

A passenger delegate from Cylinder D-19 held up a manual navigation slate. “At Tern, a borrowed tug kept our air processors aligned. We should lend tow here. But destination cannot follow every rescue request. Where do we stop?”

Irian could have answered with capacity. Instead she gave the boundary. “We stop where accepting more people makes survival less likely for those already aboard. Brine's request does not cross that line if we distribute load. It does erase schedule and fuel margin.”

“How much survival margin?”

“For food, it improves. For water, it holds if Brine transfers concentrate. For berth pressure, we fall to twelve percent spare. For fuel, we lose Halvek's sequence and take an unreserved approach toward Orison.”

Another delegate asked whether Accord could carry the food and leave the six cylinders for local tugs.

Varen's face hardened. “Our condition includes the cylinders.”

“Why those six?”

“Because every evacuation plan written before yours moved working adults first. Those cylinders contain people who cannot cross transfer spines quickly. If we let the strongest board your ships and call that rescue, the route fails around everyone else.”

The school cylinder's delegate appeared beside him, a young instructor with algae stains on both sleeves. She turned her camera toward a corridor lined with folded sleep pallets. Children sat strapped into travel webbing, each wearing a paper band like Marl's.

“We can supply two tugs,” she said. “We need four. Our drive collar has not moved the cylinder in nine years.”

No one on the assembly spoke for several seconds.

Irian refused the easy use of the image. “How many aboard?”

“Five thousand eight hundred twelve. Eleven hundred children.”

“Pressure endurance detached from Brine?”

“Eighty days with water resupply. Forty-three without.”

“Spin collar?”

“Functional at present mass.”

“Bring your maintenance file into the common stack.”

The instructor did. Work replaced spectacle.

Over the next hour, captains broke the six cylinders into tow problems. Two could pair behind a water barge. One residential drum needed bearing service before any translation. The dialysis wards could divide between Kindly Argument and three clinic modules if their patients consented, reducing tow mass. Brine crews offered seven local tugs but lacked fuel for the full approach.

Irian moved resources while everyone watched.

She had spent years protecting reserves by keeping them concentrated where trained crews could account for them. Tern had turned that efficiency into a target. One closing lens nearly took the hospital and its pharmacy together. One corridor breach had separated convoy kitchens from food barges.

“No single ship carries a survival monopoly after Brine,” she said.

Load control opened the distribution board.

Food substrate would go to twenty-four ships, not only Accord's central kitchens. Water concentrate would divide among sixteen mobile tanks and six habitat reservoirs. Machine seed, packaged as fabrication cultures and tool-pattern cores, would leave the protected freight spine and travel in twelve sealed lots. Every weak habitat received seven days of independent food. Every tug cluster carried enough water to survive separation from the carrier.

A cargo officer objected. “Breaking machine seed lots voids contamination certification.”

“Keep original seals and split by complete culture family.”

“We cannot duplicate every pattern core.”

“Then distribute overlapping sets. No one ship receives all of anything we need to live.”

A passenger delegate from the recovered hospital raised her hand. “Test it.”

Irian looked up from the board. “Specify the loss.”

“Remove Accord.”

Several bridge officers objected at once. The carrier provided central water treatment, machine shops, navigation staff, and most of the convoy's protected storage. Planning for its loss during a supply transfer sounded like inviting panic.

Irian blanked the eleven-kilometre truss from the distribution model.

Red shortages spread across thirteen ship groups. Three habitats had food but no nutrient starter for their kitchens. A tug cluster carried water concentrate without a membrane capable of processing it. Five machine-seed lots overlapped in pattern cores yet all depended on one calibration culture still assigned to Accord.

“There,” the delegate said. “You distributed containers. Not use.”

Load control began repairing the design. Brine offered compact membrane racks from two agricultural drums. Kindly Argument could carry three calibration cultures in separate sterile wards. Captains moved starter stock into habitat kitchens rather than leaving it aboard freight tenders. Every change cost berth space and handling time.

Marl asked for a second test. “Remove Brine's largest substrate carrier.”

The model lost eleven days of food from the outer convoy group. Irian split its cargo among four smaller ships, including one whose captain had refused evacuation passengers. The captain accepted common reserve cargo under the Articles while retaining his refusal on berths. Consent did not require every choice to point the same way.

A third simulation stranded the water barges. A fourth removed Kindly Argument. Each failure found a dependency they had mistaken for distribution. By the fifth run, no single ship loss starved the convoy. Some combinations still did. Irian published those combinations rather than sanding uncertainty from the board.

“This increases transfer time by six hours,” her cargo officer said.

“It buys survival after separation.”

“It also guarantees we miss Halvek's sequence.”

Irian watched Brine's six vulnerable cylinders on the adjacent plot. The schedule had already become a cost, not a plan she could keep by refusing to name the exchange.

Marl looked at the board. “Including authority?”

“That question belongs to the Articles, not my load office.”

“It belongs here too.”

Irian let the comment remain.

The redistribution created work across the convoy. Cargo crews had to open holds designed for arrival at distant ports. Habitat delegates had to choose storage rooms, displacing beds and workshops. Brine workers would fill unfamiliar couplings under time pressure. None of it matched the clean transfer promised by Halvek's schedule.

A captain from a seed barge asked who carried loss if a distributed lot spoiled.

“All of us,” Irian said. “We publish each location. Any ship that loses refrigeration announces immediately, and the nearest overlapping lot replaces it.”

“That makes private cargo common reserve.”

“During separation, yes. Ownership claims survive. Hunger does not wait for adjudication.”

The captain requested the statement entered verbatim. Irian entered it herself.

At the two-hour mark, the assembly had not reached unanimity. The Articles did not require it for accepting passengers. They required recorded consent from each affected captain and passenger delegation, plus a right to refuse a destination change that exceeded their ship's safety margin.

Three freight captains refused Brine cylinders. Two passenger groups declined extra occupancy because their air systems lacked reserve. Irian accepted those refusals and reassigned no tug from them. The remaining ships produced enough berths and tow to carry the request.

The vote passed by ship group, not by head count. Each approval included capacity conditions anyone could inspect.

Varen reopened the freight valves.

Food substrate began moving through Brine's crowded transfer spines in warm green tanks. Water concentrate followed in silver bladders. Along a parallel line, the first residential cylinder detached from its damp berth under four tugs. Condensation broke from its hull in a glittering sheet.

Irian went down to the transfer spine rather than watch icons.

Heat pressed through her uniform. The corridor smelled of algae, wet metal, and too many bodies waiting beside packed bags. Brine residents had marked cargo lanes with green tape. Convoy crew rolled empty substrate tanks inward while local workers pushed personal cases outward. Neither flow yielded naturally to the other.

Marl walked beside her. “Still look like a supply stop?”

“It looks like a collision between two manifests.”

“That is almost progress.”

At the school-cylinder junction, children waited in paired lines with paper bands around their wrists. Each band named a destination ship, responsible delegate, water assignment, and reunification point. Adults checked the marks by hand because Charter identity routing had become unreliable.

Irian stopped beside a girl whose band listed Accord, Drum Twelve. “Who gave you that berth?”

The girl pointed to a Brine clerk.

Irian asked the clerk for the matching load entry. He found it in the common stack. Drum Twelve had capacity and an assigned school group. The paper band agreed with the convoy file.

One checked child could not prove a system. It could show whether the system reached a hand.

Her wrist display lit with Halvek's call.

He did not waste time. “My reserved sequence begins in thirteen hours. Route control cannot hold the slots beyond that.”

“We need at least twenty-seven.”

“Then detach the Brine cargo and depart with your original convoy.”

“The cargo includes residents under accepted Articles.”

“You are risking four hundred ten thousand people for a local evacuation Brine had years to plan.”

“Planning did not give their cylinders drives.”

“Your duty does not expand with every need you encounter.”

“No. It expands when I take the food that lets my people survive.”

Halvek sent the revised route table. Two corridors showed reserved green. After thirteen hours, both became unassigned traffic under reinforced hold. Irian understood the value he offered. Cutters would clear approach. Fuel tenders would wait at Orison staging. The schedule could pull her scattered convoy back toward one plan.

It also required leaving while Brine's vulnerable cylinders remained beside a moving anchor.

“Commodore,” Halvek said, “Tern proved the cost of improvisation.”

“Tern proved the cost of calling one system safety while it pulled against another.”

“This is logistics, not your routebreaker's biology.”

“Logistics includes who gets left under the machine.”

She declined the reserved sequence.

The green corridors vanished from her display. Fuel tenders, cutter escort, and guaranteed transit order disappeared with them. Her navigation staff would have to build an approach from direct sightings, local tugs, and whatever geometry survived the delay.

Irian entered a new convoy commitment under the Free Convoy Articles: complete Brine's agreed evacuation, distribute survival stores, then depart by ship-specific readiness rather than one centralized slot.

Marl read it before signing as delegate witness. Lio Varen added Brine's obligations: substrate, water concentrate, seven tugs, repair access, and resident manifests. Captains began attaching their accepted loads.

Outside the spine windows, Threadbare crossed toward Brine's anchor, small beside the nine resonators and the dark they held. Six local routekeeper teams moved around the cage with instruments on their suit rails. The next three-beat pulse had not yet arrived, but every Brine transfer schedule now ended with a margin for it.

The school cylinder pulled free under four tugs.

Its wet hull carried five thousand eight hundred twelve people away from the anchor plane. Food tanks moved the opposite direction into Irian's scattered convoy. Nothing followed Halvek's timetable now.

Irian watched both flows cross without colliding and committed the rest of her ships to the same difficult exchange.

Chapter 12 — The Anchor Choir

Kessa tightened the recorder clamp until the old transfer spine complained through her boots.

Brine's anchor hung beyond the cylinder swarm, nine resonator stations arranged around a patch of dark that refused every honest outline. Condensation from the habitat locks had frozen across the spine in cloudy scales. Each time the structure shifted under traffic, frost snapped free and drifted toward the red star. Six routekeeper craft held position at measured intervals outside the cage. Their work lights gave the dark center six different edges.

“Clamp is carrying,” Kessa said.

Anu floated beside the recorder rack with one knee hooked through a handhold. “It carries your mass. That does not make it quiet.”

“Nothing here is quiet.”

The three-beat load had crossed their hull twice since Threadbare matched Brine's anchor drift. Kessa felt it now through the spine: pressure, release, pressure, release, pressure, release. A cutter captain from Gannet had called it a knock. The Hush team called it a harmonic. One Brine technician had submitted possible intentional transmission in a preliminary log and started an argument that consumed forty minutes they did not have.

Kessa attached a second brace to the rack. “Run it.”

Anu opened the six-team feed. Lines climbed across the portable screens. Each recorder caught cage strain, local gradient, filament conductivity, resonator output, and the motion of reference lights placed beyond the anchor. Six clocks had been uncoupled from Charter timing. Six crews watched one physical event without letting a central system decide when it happened.

The first beat reached Gannet's frame.

A fraction of a second later, the Brine spine flexed beneath Kessa. The load crossed Threadbare, passed through the dark center, and touched the Hush recorder last. Its order matched position. Nothing in the station network routed it.

“Mark peak interval,” Anu said.

Voices answered from the six craft.

“Gannet has point-eight.”

“Hush has point-nine-four.”

“Mica Hand, one-point-one and falling.”

“Brine local, filament lag increasing.”

Two other teams reported numbers. Nobody called the pattern a message this time.

Kessa watched the traces settle. The peaks carried the same three-beat shape they had recorded at Nacre, Tern, and along the twelve-anchor wake map. Their spacing had changed. The second pressure rise arrived early. The third dragged across a shallow series of tremors that set the recorder teeth chattering.

Anu enlarged the troughs. “Stress response.”

“From traffic?” asked Orra Senn aboard Mica Hand.

“From restraint. Traffic adds a smaller modulation at the corridor mouth.” Anu tagged two bands in different colors. “The timing shift follows resonator load. If this were language, the cage would be composing most of the sentence.”

A routekeeper on Gannet said, “You can't rule out encoded content from six samples.”

“I can rule out your preferred translation.”

“My preferred translation is that I don't lose my license over animal weather.”

Anu's mouth tightened. “It is not weather. It is also not language. Those categories do not exhaust nature.”

Kessa pushed away from the recorder and crossed the spine toward the manual cage board. Brine had built its control platform for technicians in pressure suits, with broad keys and mechanical covers over the dangerous commands. A Charter lock icon glowed above every resonator channel. Local crews could trim power. Full release required authority that had already left Brine aboard a cruiser.

Below them, habitat cylinders crowded the transfer lanes. Passenger ferries moved families from damp homes to convoy berths. Cargo pods followed with algae stock and food substrate. The evacuation had turned every schedule into a negotiation between bodies and tanks. One spine carried outbound traffic from three cylinders because another had jammed under an overloaded coupling. If Brine's corridor failed violently, six million people would not fit anywhere else.

If every hold remained, the returning adult would find twelve injured juveniles pinned across its path.

Kessa opened the routekeeper channel. “We test a partial release. Outer three stations only. No cutting. We watch contraction and stop.”

Gannet's captain answered first. “Charter denied that sequence.”

“Charter denied everything except reinforcement.”

“Reinforcement keeps the corridor stable.”

“For how long?”

Nobody supplied a duration.

Orra sent her vessel's local authorization key. “Mica Hand consents.”

Brine local followed. Then Hush. The other teams waited while Kessa studied the cage diagram. Outer stations Two, Five, and Eight sat at the widest load nodes, each separated from the next by two working resonators. Shutting them together would loosen the cage without dropping a continuous side. At Tern, the safety hold had fought Kessa's cold-shear sequence and stored the mismatch until the spindle broke. Here, no blade would touch filament. No inner field would move.

Anu pulled herself to the board. “The juvenile may contract. It may expand. It may rotate its topology out of our measurement plane. None of those responses grants permission to infer preference.”

“Can any kill the transfer spine?”

“Expansion could change local gradients beyond our present margin.”

“How far beyond?”

“Ask after we measure it.”

Kessa gave her a flat look.

Anu glanced toward the ferries. “My conservative estimate keeps the spine inside tolerance if we cut outer power by twelve percent per minute. My confidence does not deserve the word conservative.”

That sounded more useful than certainty.

Before authorizing the ramp, Kessa made every team prove its stop path. She called stations in physical order rather than by ship name. Gannet showed a hand on its local breaker. Brine's pair pulled the sealed cover from a mechanical interrupt nobody had tested since the last cage overhaul. Hush discovered that its command still passed through a Charter timing filter and rerouted the lead through an inspection socket.

The work consumed four minutes of their traffic window. It also exposed three different meanings of manual. On Mica Hand, Orra's breaker separated the control beam from its power amplifier. Gannet's switch merely requested separation from a processor. The Brine interrupt dropped the whole outer station into passive cooling, after which restoring it would take forty minutes and a fresh alignment crew.

“Then Brine owns the honest switch,” Kessa said.

Gannet's captain muttered something about obsolete equipment.

“Obsolete equipment can still fail without asking permission.”

Vasken came across from Threadbare with two portable load cells. He fixed one above Kessa's boots and crawled to place the second past the nearest spine joint. The cells would tell them whether a gradient change crossed the structure or pulled the whole segment at once. Kessa checked both attachments herself. A sensor clamped to frost measured optimism.

While she worked, a ferry eased from the lower lock with Brine families visible through its small ports. Bags filled the aisle behind them. One child had pressed both hands to the glass to watch the routekeeper craft. The ferry pilot asked whether to hold departure until the test ended.

Kessa read the traffic plot. Keeping a loaded vessel beside the spine created one danger. Sending it through an altered corridor created another.

“Clear the local plane and wait outside cage control,” she said. “You leave after we restore the published geometry or establish a new one.”

The pilot accepted without asking which outcome Kessa expected. Below the platform, the ferry drifted to a marked refuge box. Its presence put people inside every margin on her slate.

Kessa transmitted the ramp to all six teams. “Any recorder loses independent timing, call stop. Any reference light departs its box, call stop. No one completes the shutdown on remote instruction.”

The Gannet captain swore softly, then sent consent. The last two teams followed.

Sena spoke from Threadbare's cupola. “Traffic window closes in eleven minutes. After that, a loaded ferry crosses the near plane.”

“We'll finish in eight.”

“You said that about replacing my port manifold.”

“The manifold lacked biological cooperation.”

“Still does.”

Dry laughter moved across two channels. It ended when Kessa lifted the cover over Resonator Two.

She pressed the manual trim.

Power fell by twelve percent.

The anchor's star arcs widened. Frost lifted from the spine in a sheet and hung without choosing a direction. Kessa felt no pulse through her boots. The juvenile's bent-dark outline remained broad and low beneath the corridor mouth.

“Two stable,” Brine local reported.

“Gradient lag point-three seconds,” Anu said. “Continue.”

Kessa lowered Two through four more increments. At sixty percent, something moved behind the lens distortion. A pale branching trace appeared where no surface should have caught light. Filaments drew inward from the outer node, not as a recoil from failing power but as a coordinated contraction around the denser center.

The three-beat pulse came again.

Its first rise struck softer. The second held longer. The third ended without the ragged tremors.

Every recorder captured the change.

“What does that mean?” Gannet asked.

“Less strain,” Anu said.

“You said no translation.”

“Less strain is measurement. ‘Thank you’ would be translation.”

Kessa closed Resonator Two completely. No alarm sounded. The corridor narrowed by two percent, still wide enough for the scheduled empty cargo pods. She moved to Five.

Under the second ramp, the juvenile shifted away from the disabled station and distributed its topology across the remaining field. Kessa knew that motion. Any suspended load sought a path. A living load also protected damaged structure, thickening one filament web while another loosened. The distinction showed in sequence rather than intention.

“Reference light Seven drifting,” Hush called.

Kessa stopped the ramp.

Sena overlaid the external track. “Seven is inside its box. Recorder compensation misread the red-star flare.”

“Hush confirms. Continue.”

Five stepped downward. The pulse crossed again before the station reached zero. This variation carried a shorter interval between first and second beats, followed by a clean trough. Anu marked the timing against local field strength. Her screen filled with converging slopes.

“We're changing the relay delay,” she said.

“Relay?” Orra asked.

“Biological propagation. The load passes through filament architecture, not empty geometry. Reduce restraint and conduction changes.”

Gannet's captain said, “Across how many anchors?”

“Twelve measured. Possibly more.”

“And every one feels what we do here?”

Anu looked at Kessa before answering. “Not what we do. A migration state passes along the chain. Local injury changes its timing. The next juvenile receives that altered state through the shared gradient.”

The channel stayed silent long enough for Kessa to hear Brine's coolant pumps through the spine.

People wanted a voice because voices could agree, accuse, or forgive. A bodily chain allowed none of those comforts. It carried condition. One trapped animal tightened against a cage. Another responded to the changed rhythm because its life cycle had shaped it to do so. Humans had inserted machinery into that loop and mistaken the resulting stability for ownership.

Kessa moved to Resonator Eight.

A priority signal overrode the work channel before she touched the trim. Halvek's image appeared on the cage board, compressed by relay delay but unmistakable in his dark Charter uniform.

“Routebreaker Rho, cease unauthorized manipulation of Brine Anchor Three.”

Kessa left the station cover open. “Local routekeepers have accepted the test under worker-stop authority.”

“Worker-stop authority permits suspension. It does not permit modification.”

“Two outer resonators are down. Corridor geometry remains inside traffic tolerance.”

“That condition will be reversed.”

“Not through this board.”

Halvek glanced offscreen, reading data from analysts she could not see. “Brine carries famine substrate for three systems. Your experiment narrows its only outbound corridor during an active evacuation.”

“Your hold injured the animal at Tern and killed sixty-three people.”

“The failed cut killed them.”

“Your beam fought the cut.”

“And your present action risks repeating the failure on an inhabited station.” His voice held no heat. Capacity moved through every syllable. “Restore Two and Five. Stand clear of Eight. I will not trade six million lives for a hypothesis.”

Anu entered the pickup. “The stress reduction is measured. We have six independent records.”

“You have eighteen minutes of data.”

“We have a response across the chain.”

“Then send it for review.”

Kessa saw the remote-control icon change before Halvek finished. A second authorization wrapped around each resonator channel. Continuity had reached the network through a relay outside Brine's local lock.

“Orra,” Kessa said, “cut Charter reception.”

“Trying.”

The icon turned red.

Resonator Two ignited at full hold strength.

The spine kicked sideways.

Kessa struck the board with her shoulder. Her tether caught, wrenching her across the rail as the stars beyond Brine folded into sharp pairs. Five came online half a second later. The juvenile had contracted around reduced pressure. Restoring both fields without a ramp trapped that contraction and forced its topology outward through the remaining nodes.

“Hold override!” Brine local shouted.

“Eight is climbing on its own.”

Anu clung to the recorder rack. Every filament trace on her display surged. “Kill the inner six. It needs somewhere to contract.”

Kessa reached for the emergency bank. Charter locks covered those controls too.

A sound traveled through the transfer spine, too low for the suit microphones and strong enough to move her teeth. It came without words or intention. Metal took the load because the juvenile's changing field had nowhere else to go.

Below them, a passenger ferry rolled hard against its docking collar.

Sena fired Threadbare's grapples. One line caught the ferry's forward tow lug. The other struck the spine beyond Kessa and locked. “I have the near segment. Far segment is opening.”

Kessa looked down the spine. Two hundred metres away, a joint ring stretched into an oval. Its bolts did not snap together. They failed around the circumference in a running pattern, each bright fracture chasing the next. Beyond the joint, three ferry locks and a crowded staging tube began to drift.

“Evacuate far segment,” she called. “All locks release ships. People in the tube move inward now.”

Brine controllers took over the order. Ferries fired side thrusters inside margins meant for docking. One scraped a radiator bank and spun clear. The staging tube's emergency shutters began closing, slow against the changing geometry.

The three-beat pulse struck through Kessa's tether.

Its intervals had collapsed. The third beat broke into rapid trembling before falling away.

Anu shouted over the alarms. “That is acute stress. Not speech. Record it.”

The demand sounded brutal until Kessa understood. If they lost the spine and saved only people, Halvek could rename the event. The cage would report a control fault. Charter timing would smooth the pulses. Every independent recorder needed to survive.

“Hush, take the raw feed. Gannet, mirror Hush. Mica Hand, publish while we still have relay.”

Orra answered, “Already moving.”
