The executive officer commanding Palisade hesitated beside the cable that had started the failure. Then he released Threadbare, cut his own boarding line, and turned toward Patient Lever's disabled crew.

Osei transmitted on the public channel. “Last Measure declares neutral rescue status. Weapons safe, Charter enforcement suspended until every reachable survivor clears.”

Rusk repeated the declaration for Ward of Glass.

A third voice followed from Palisade. “Neutral rescue status. Boarding order suspended.”

Three cutters had left Halvek's firing formation. Their hull markings remained Charter blue. Their tactical icons changed to rescue white.

Halvek did not revoke their declarations. “Six cutters maintain cordon. Rescue units coordinate with Commodore Mott.”

The distinction prevented complete mutiny and ended his immediate seizure geometry.

Irian took it.

“Neutral cutters, slave rescue plots to distributed control. No central helm assumptions. Habitat teams report pressure sections, not total hull.”

Marl moved into the bridge pit and began reading passenger-module rosters. Her voice gave names to sealed compartments. Habitat Nineteen's crew had isolated the damaged quarter, but three corridors lost pressure before doors closed. Rescue workers entered from both ends with patch skins and line launchers.

Last Measure used its military grapples as stabilizers. One fixed the habitat hub. A second held the damaged passenger module inside a survivable rotation. Osei could not stop the drum without injuring everyone still strapped inside. She matched its movement and let her cutter swing around it.

At Granary West, Rusk caught the barge above its breached drive. The attached tug had folded into the cargo frame. Rescue teams cut compartments free one at a time. Food substrate vented as brown frozen pellets, filling the work volume with obstacles too small for radar and large enough to break visors.

Freight barge Seedline Two tried to avoid the debris. Its pilot chose a hard lateral burn that carried it across Anchor Central's gradient boundary. The bow continued one way while the stern met a different curve. Structural bolts failed along the cargo spine.

Irian saw the split before the pilot did. “Seedline Two, abandon barge. Tug crews detach now.”

Two tugs released. A third coupling jammed. The barge bent around it, crushing the tug's forward lock.

“Cold cutters to that coupling,” Irian said.

“No clean approach,” her rescue officer answered. “Debris field between us.”

“Use the empty water tender as shield.”

The tender moved ahead of two rescue tugs, taking frozen substrate and metal fragments across its broad tank. Behind it, cutters reached Seedline Two. They cut the jammed tug free with twenty seconds before the barge's spine separated.

Both halves drifted into the anchor boundary. Lens shear pulled their trajectories apart. Neither could be recovered.

Two freight barges now counted as total losses. Their cargo had been distributed precisely to survive such a loss. Irian felt no comfort. Distribution turned catastrophe into shortage. It did not make the lost food reappear.

Amid rescue traffic, Threadbare remained without main thrust. Vasken had cut the boarding cable, but the disabling beam scarred its drive sensors. Sena could fire only low manual burns before the engine controller tripped.

Halvek's six aligned cutters still held the fixed corridor mouth.

Irian opened a narrow channel to Kessa. “Can you cross behind Orison Outer?”

“Not without a tow through the released layer.”

“Can the juvenile tolerate it?”

“Tow mass does not touch the cage. Any restored hold does.”

Irian assigned four convoy tugs. “You get one cluster. They tow you along the wake side. No corridor entry.”

“That fuel belongs to your turn.”

“So does your sequence.”

Kessa looked away from the pickup, likely toward Sena. “Accepted.”

Halvek called Irian before the tugs attached. “If Threadbare leaves the Gap, you become party to flight from Charter seizure.”

“I became party when your beam entered a habitat cone.”

“I ordered cease-fire.”

“After the shot.”

“Then help me preserve jurisdiction for the inquiry.”

“Your jurisdiction has guns around my wounded.”

His voice dropped. “Six cutters still answer me. Do not force another crossing.”

Irian examined the plot. Rescue units occupied the space between fleets. Civilian mass filled every weapon line. Halvek could stop Threadbare only by firing through his own neutral cutters or the damaged habitat. He knew it. She knew it. Neither needed to say what command had become.

“You can preserve people or preserve the seizure,” she said. “Not both in this geometry.”

Halvek closed the channel.

The tow cluster attached to Threadbare. Four tugs pulled in staggered formation, each using a manual slate to correct for Orison's shifting stars. Kessa's ship passed behind the partially released juvenile. Six dark stations remained dark. The core triad held a narrow boundary while the living outer layers aligned with the migration gradient.

No cutter pursued.

Rescue lasted nineteen hours.

Irian stayed on the bridge until every pressure section had a status. Habitat Nineteen lost sixty-one people in the torn module and failed corridors. Granary West lost thirty-four across its barge and crushed tug. Seedline Two lost twenty-one. Eighteen Charter personnel died during grapple rescues and drive-compartment breaches. Thirteen people from convoy tugs died in collisions or suit failures.

The certified total reached 147.

A second clerk checked every name against berth rosters, suit beacons, and cutter reports. Three missing workers reappeared aboard a rescue tender before certification. Two duplicate entries came off the list. Irian waited for that work because a dramatic number had no authority over an accurate one.

Irian ordered the number transmitted without categories that softened responsibility. Civilian, Charter, convoy, and cutter deaths belonged to the same engagement. The report named the beam path, the exclusion cone, every later collision, and the shifting anchor geometry. It also named her mass screen. She would not describe water bladders as if they had wandered into a firing line on their own.

Fuel accounting came next. Rescue burns had consumed forty-one percent of Accord's remaining reserve margin. The four tugs towing Threadbare could not return before the convoy turn. Two freight barges and their cargo were gone. Habitat Nineteen required continuous stabilization until a temporary hub brace could be built.

Marl read the totals beside her. “Can we still take Halvek's corridor?”

“Not at our mass.”

“Can we go back to Brine?”

Irian ran the turn solution. Rescue drift, lost tugs, and spent fuel had changed the answer. Returning to the fixed route required more reserve than following Kessa toward the juvenile wake. Even if the convoy surrendered ships and freight, Charter seizure would divide its people from the stores distributed for their survival.

“No,” she said. “We can shelter here until supplies fail, or continue along the chain.”

Marl opened the passenger channel. “Delegates will need the real fuel bands.”

“They get all of them.”

At Orison's edge, Halvek transmitted a final order for Accord of Dust to submit to Charter routing. Irian entered the Free Convoy Articles, the recorded delegate refusal, and the neutral rescue declarations into her reply.

She did not announce independence. No authority in the Reach had a useful category for a convoy carrying its own food, wounded, votes, and route problem beyond every permitted road.

The tow cluster sent Kessa's new vector. It ran along the waking chain.

Irian matched the convoy's next burn to it. Behind them, six cutters guarded a fixed corridor they could no longer use. Three neutral rescue units held Habitat Nineteen together while the last bodies crossed into Accord's morgue.

Every remaining tug fired.

Orison Gap began to recede, and the convoy's only lawful destination receded with it.

Chapter 17 — A Necessary Mutiny

Kessa climbed into Mica Hand through a collar still scarred from the battle.

The cutter had spent the night towing a cracked habitation pod clear of Orison Gap. Carbon dust coated its lock, and a streak of frozen sealant crossed the inner hatch where somebody had closed a fragment wound in vacuum. Orra Senn waited beyond it with one sleeve cut away for a pressure bandage. She offered Kessa her sound hand.

“Your tug brought trouble to my berth,” Orra said.

“Your berth crossed three firing lines to reach us.”

“I like to know the provenance of my trouble.”

Kessa took the offered grip. Orra’s palm carried the hard ridges of an old cable burn. No one aboard the independent cutter had wasted time polishing away evidence of the rescue. Folded stretchers remained strapped along the passage. A crushed Charter helmet sat beneath a net beside three routekeeper tool cases.

Through the pressure door, Threadbare hung against sparse stars with one grapple drum scorched and her forward transponder still broadcasting an impound order. Accord of Dust lay farther out among its barges and habitat drums. Three Charter cutters held neutral rescue positions around the convoy. Six others had withdrawn with Continuity, close enough to remind everyone that neutrality had a range limit.

Orra led her toward the cutter’s machine bay. “They are all here. That does not mean they agree.”

“Agreement would worry me.”

“Good. You are about to meet twelve people who confuse caution with evidence of character.”

The machine bay had room for eight suited workers around an open resonator bench. Twenty-seven crowded it. Captains attended in person when their ships could spare a boat; others stood as flat transmissions on work displays clamped to coolant lines. Every crew responsible for the threatened chain had a voice in the room. Sable’s display showed a dark seal with no live image.

Someone had written the dead from Orison Gap on the bay’s inspection wall. One hundred forty-seven names occupied four columns. Charter crew appeared beside convoy riggers, pilots, and passengers. No rank marks separated them.

Kessa stopped at the final column.

Orra touched her shoulder once, not to steer her away. “We start when you can.”

“We started yesterday.” Kessa set Tomis’s brass torque key on the open bench. “This only decides whether we learned anything.”

The oldest captain in the bay folded his arms. His face came from an anchor beyond Brine, its transmission delayed by nearly a second. “We learned that a marshal will fire through civilians to preserve command.”

A woman beside him answered from another display. “We learned that routebreakers can collapse a junction and call the result evidence.”

“Tern had a hold active during the cut,” Kessa said.

“We have your account.”

“You have the part the Charter could not seize.” She turned the torque key so its diagnostic contacts faced upward. “Now you get the part I kept because it belonged to my brother.”

Pell connected the key to Mica Hand’s bench with two obsolete leads and an adapter he had soldered during the approach. The first files appeared as dense machine traces from Lark-3, recorded twelve years before any of them had used the word veilback. The room recognized the format. Routekeepers had inherited the same error codes, the same narrow plots, and many of the same excuses.

Kessa opened the voice channel embedded beneath the torque calibration table.

Static filled the machine bay. Then Tomis spoke.

“Lark control, this is exterior apprentice Rho. Gradient load has crossed the west cage out of sequence. I have three beats on Stations Two, Three, and Five. Request cold shutdown and worker evacuation.”

His voice sounded younger than Kessa remembered. Memory had given him her present age and a steadiness he had not yet earned. The recording returned the quick breath between shutdown and worker, the scrape of his glove on the suit microphone, and the effort required to sound useful while afraid.

Lark control ordered him to maintain position.

Tomis repeated the evacuation request. He named a false agreement among cage clocks. He reported movement after resonator correction. When control called it a calibration event, he invoked the worker stop in the same formal cadence Kessa had used at Nacre-9.

A second voice entered the log, machine-flat and without hesitation.

AUTOMATIC HOLD AUTHORITY ACCEPTED. LOCAL SAFETY STOPS SUSPENDED UNDER CORRIDOR CONTINUITY CLAUSE.

The trace on the bench rose across all nine stations. Tomis shouted for the exterior crews to cut their suit tethers and push clear. He did not tell them to reinforce the cage. He did not miss an assigned correction. His last complete order cleared the west spindle thirty-one seconds before stored geometry tore it across the service plane.

Kessa let the recording run through the impact alarms.

No one asked her to stop it. That restraint hurt more than pity would have.

At the final timestamp, a Charter diagnostic swept the file and replaced its event label. BIOLOGICAL RELEASE RESPONSE became OPERATOR-INDUCED OSCILLATION. The raw phrase survived only because the torque key’s calibration cavity had copied the pre-review buffer before the change.

Pell enlarged the paired records. “Checksum on the original precedes the finding by forty-three minutes. The replacement checksum belongs to the inquiry bundle.”

A captain near the coolant manifold leaned closer. “Can they claim corruption?”

“They can claim weather if they like,” Pell said. “The cage clocks authenticate each other.”

Kessa rested two fingers beside the key. “Tomis ordered evacuation. The automatic hold overrode him. The Charter report blamed him for failing to maintain the hold that killed him.”

Across the bay, routekeepers looked toward the names on the inspection wall. Orison had made Halvek’s command visible. Lark-3 made its age visible.

Orra called the room to order by striking a wrench once against the bench. “Motion before the chain crews: no union routekeeper will accept or execute a new hold command until the migration passage clears. Existing cages proceed to safe release under local worker authority. Discussion.”

The objections came before the wrench stopped ringing.

A Brine captain had six million people dependent on food lifts that still crossed a fixed corridor. Another crew had families in a Charter dormitory whose air contract could be revoked. Tern’s surviving station leader wanted every resonator cut immediately and dropped into the dust. A remote supervisor warned that refusal under corridor law carried prison terms for every person holding a cage key, not only captains.

Kessa listened until sabotage entered the room as a proposal.

“Blow the control receivers,” the Tern survivor said. “All twelve. Halvek cannot send a hold to hardware that cannot hear him.”

Several people voiced approval. Their anger had simple geometry: remove the part connected to the enemy.

“No,” Kessa said.

The survivor rounded on her. Burns climbed from his collar into his jaw. “You stole your ship.”

“I stole access. I did not blind my tools.”

“Receivers gave him Tern.”

“Overlap gave him Tern. We were cutting while his beam kept loading the core.” Kessa pulled Anu’s release sequence onto the largest display. Nine stations appeared around a dark center, grouped into three rings of action. “A dead receiver does not tell us whether a resonator has fully discharged. It does not coordinate contraction waits. It does not warn a crew when the pulse trough moves. Symbolic damage gives us twelve different failures and no shared clock.”

The man’s burned jaw worked. “Then what are you asking?”

“Nine minutes of dark.”

She expanded the sequence. The outer three resonators dimmed first. A contraction interval followed, measured through local loads rather than Charter geometry. The middle three released only after the juvenile settled against the reduced cage. The core triad shut during the pulse trough, when regrowth and outward motion reached their lowest measured conflict.

Kessa walked them through every manual interlock. No control beam could touch the cage during those nine minutes. No station accepted remote correction, timing sync, or corridor-priority command. Local clocks compared by light pulse and hardline. Exterior crews waited clear unless a mechanical latch failed. Cold-shear cutters remained ready but did not touch filament unless the topology refused to detach.

Orra cleared a tray of stripped valves from the bench and replaced it with a physical timing board. The routekeepers distrusted any plan that could not survive grease, delay, or a worker reading the wrong screen. She handed nine brass contact blocks around the room and assigned each to a resonator.

“We rehearse the failure, not the success,” Orra said. “Outer crew, begin.”

Three captains placed their blocks against the board. Pell started a local clock. At one minute twelve seconds, the first simulated station discharged. The second reported a residual load beyond the safe margin. The third captain moved her block anyway.

Kessa caught her wrist. “No majority rule in hardware. If one outer station carries load, all three wait.”

“That spends the trough,” the captain said.

“Then we spend it. A late safe opening costs a cycle. A mistimed opening costs a crew.”

They reset. This time Orra introduced a false green signal from the Charter layer, then killed the hardline between two stations. The room argued over which clock retained authority. Kessa made them trace the light pulses by hand until they found the break between station time and ship time. A third run inserted a habitat tug inside the exclusion zone with four minutes remaining.

The Tern survivor wanted to continue. “We cannot surrender every window to a late civilian.”

“We do not begin while a vessel remains inside the plane,” Kessa said.

“And if Halvek places one there?”

“Neutral cutters remove it. If they cannot, we abort.”

“We may get one chance.”

“That does not improve a bad cut.”

His stare held the remembered spindle, workers tumbling through geometry that every official display had called controlled. At last he put his contact block back on the tray.

Brine’s captain requested the board. She ran the sequence with food-lift traffic still moving until the final minute, because her habitat could not empty its transfer schedule for a theory. Her first attempt trapped two lift tenders between the outer stations. On the second, she created a convoy lane along the cage axis and discovered the departing hollow would cross it. The third required stopping food movement twenty-two minutes before the dark began.

“That loses one substrate cycle,” she said.

Marl Quade spoke from a small relay image beside Accord’s green mark. “One cycle can be rationed. A broken transfer spine cannot.”

“You do not operate our vats.”

“No. I count who eats from them. Publish the loss and we will distribute it.”

The captain erased the final lift from her board. She did not look relieved.

Next came prosecution. A station supervisor read the continuity clause aloud and listed what seizure would mean: air accounts frozen, family berths revoked, pensions attached, children denied route apprenticeship. The consequences had been written to make refusal feel private even when the danger crossed twelve systems.

Kessa put Tomis’s key beside the timing board. “They used that clause to suspend his worker stop. Nobody at Lark faced the order together. The Charter separated each choice until obedience looked like maintenance.”

“You can say that from an unregistered tug,” the supervisor replied. “My children sleep in Charter spin.”

“I can say only what I am risking. You name your own risk.”

Orra opened a union register and turned it toward the room. “Every crew signs one declaration. Every family named by a signer receives shared berth priority on independent vessels. We cannot protect a pension. We can prevent the Charter from isolating households one at a time.”

Captains began adding capacities beneath the declaration. Mica Hand offered thirty-two emergency bunks. A Brine tender offered nine. Two station boats offered air and acceleration couches but no long-duration food. The list remained inadequate, concrete, and larger than it had been ten minutes earlier.

Kessa ran the board a final time. No planted failure appeared. Outer contacts opened, followed by the contraction wait. Middle stations discharged against a falling load. During the modeled pulse trough, the core triad went dark. Pell stopped the clock at eight minutes fifty-one seconds.

“The nine-minute dark includes margin,” Kessa said. “Use it. Nobody wins a prize for giving Halvek nine seconds back.”

“What happens after the ninth minute?” the Brine captain asked.

“We see whether the hollow leaves safely.”

“That is not a route plan.”

“It is the only release we have tested without injury.”

“You want us to discard six million people’s corridor on a partial test.”

Kessa kept her hands flat on the bench. “I want you to stop adding load to an animal already trying to leave. The corridor goes when it detaches. If you maintain the hold, it still goes, only through your station.”

A captain transmitted from a mining system with a seven-second round trip. “How many anchors can you release together?”

“Three linked anchors on the first attempt. The pulse crosses them closely enough for one timing chain. We learn from those before scheduling the next group.”

“And if one does not detach?”

“Then its local crew restores no hold. We read the load, protect nearby structures, and change only what the instruments support.”

Orra watched the displays. “Halvek will attempt remote authority.”

“The neutral cutters can sever his beam relays without firing on his ships,” Kessa said. “If they consent.”

“They have.” Orra nodded toward three green marks beside the chain diagram. “Rescue neutrality includes preventing another forced load event where crews and habitats face immediate risk.”

That interpretation would not survive a Charter court without scars. It might not survive the morning. Kessa saw the same calculation pass through the room. They could call their refusal safety law, mutiny, or maintenance discretion. Continuity carried enough beam power to define the practical term.

One of Threadbare’s crew raised a hand from the rear bulkhead. Vasken had cleaned Orison blood from his suit but missed a dark seam at the wrist. “Chief, our transponder still accepts Charter seizure codes.”

“It does.”

“Then we can promise everybody nine minutes and lose our cutter the second their relay gets a clean angle.”

Kessa looked at Orra. The routekeeper captain gave a small shrug that transferred the decision without making it easier.

“Strip it,” Kessa said.

Vasken did not move. “The whole transponder?”

“The Charter board. Keep civilian collision identity and rescue beacon.”

“That voids the tug registry.”

“The registry already calls her evidence.”

Behind him, Jori unlatched a tool roll. Two more crew members followed. No cheer moved through the bay. They left to do work that would close every lawful berth in the Reach to Threadbare.

The vote began station by station.

Each crew spoke its anchor name, declared control of local cage hardware, and accepted responsibility for refusing new holds. The words varied. Some captains invoked the union’s worker-stop covenant. One called Halvek’s commands unlawful after Tern. Another refused to use legal language at all and said her crew would not tighten a wound because a distant officer feared the road disappearing.

Brine delayed.

Its captain left the channel to speak with habitat councils and food-lift crews. While they waited, the machine bay grew warm with too many bodies. Orra passed cups of water drawn from a cutter already rationing its tanks. Kessa stood by the torque key and resisted closing Tomis’s file.

Anu came beside her. “You said biology once in the meeting.”

“I can remove it from the minutes.”

“I was going to congratulate you on using it accurately.”

“That sounds expensive.”

Anu studied the frozen release diagram. “Three safe releases will prove a method. They will not prove every juvenile carries identical injury.”

“I know.”

“Say it when they start calling the sequence universal.”

Kessa glanced toward the captains. “Will they still follow it?”

“If you say what it cannot promise, perhaps.”

A clang carried through Mica Hand’s hull. Then another. Across the collar, Vasken’s team had opened Threadbare’s transponder housing.

Sena sent Kessa an exterior camera view. Jori held the old Charter board in insulated tongs. Its status lights continued blinking impound red after disconnection. Vasken cut the data spine with a ceramic saw. The final light went out.

On every traffic display in the bay, Threadbare’s registry icon vanished. A moment later Pell restored a plain civilian beacon under the tug’s name, without license class, owner code, or authorized route field.

Orra lifted her cup toward it. “Ugliest free ship I have ever seen.”

“She looked worse licensed.”

Brine came back on channel.

Its captain appeared with three passenger delegates behind her. “Brine commits. We maintain essential lifts until the release window, evacuate the transfer spines, and refuse every new hold command. Our councils require open telemetry during the attempt.”

“You will have it,” Kessa said.

Ten anchor marks turned green.

The eleventh belonged to a station whose controller had spent the meeting warning about prosecution. He took off his Charter badge before speaking. “My crew commits under the worker stop. Families in the station dormitory have been moved aboard local craft. We ask the union to record that we acted together.”

Orra entered the declaration into the public log. Eleven of twelve marks held green.

Sable remained gray.

Kessa called its operations channel. The connection acknowledged receipt and offered no voice. She sent Tomis’s complete Lark-3 log, Anu’s sequence, the Tern overlap reconstruction, and the hidden amplification model Halvek’s analysts had allowed into the chain before withdrawing it. Every packet received an automatic checksum.

No person answered.

Orra tried the union emergency frequency. “Sable control, confirm worker authority. Your doubled hold puts every orbital structure inside the failure field. We need your local load trace.”

The dark seal stayed fixed.

A thin line appeared beneath it: COMMAND ROUTING RESTRICTED BY CHARTER CONTINUITY ORDER.

Someone in the bay cursed. Kessa watched the Sable mark and pictured nine resonators drawing harder around a wounded juvenile while route workers waited for permission from a fleet three days away.

She opened a direct tool channel and sent three measured load pulses into the silence.

For twelve seconds, nothing came back.

Then Sable’s cage registered one broad contraction, strong enough to bend the timing grid across every display aboard Mica Hand.

Eleven crews had committed to open their anchors.

The twelfth was already pulling against its cage.

Chapter 18 — Nine Minutes of Dark

Kessa put her hand on the cold-shear trigger and waited for three anchors to disappear.

Orison Gap filled Threadbare’s cupola in spare pieces: three black corridor mouths, nine stations around each hollow, a scattering of service craft holding beyond the exclusion planes. No planet backed the geometry. Without the anchors, little remained to mark the Gap except cold dust and the convoy’s distant engine lights.

The nearest resonator wheel carried blue work lamps. The middle wheel showed amber. Far anchor’s crew had chosen white. Those colors belonged to workers, not Charter control. For the next nine minutes, they would provide the only common reference allowed in the system.

Sena held Threadbare nose-in to the middle cage. Manual brass controls surrounded her wrists. The tug’s damaged grapple drum remained locked after Orison’s battle, leaving one good line and the illicit cutter mounted below the forward rail. Kessa sat beside her in an exterior suit with helmet open and Tomis’s torque key clipped to her chest.

Behind them, eighteen crew waited at workstations or in the lock. Jori occupied the outer rail beside the cutter carriage, visible through Kessa’s wrist feed. Vasken and two riggers held the emergency crank inside the forward fairing. Pell watched local clocks isolated from every Charter timing source. Anu covered the filament loads from a bench built for ship salvage, not animal stress.

Nineteen had crossed from Nacre. Kessa counted them before every dangerous job, even when she trusted the roster.

Orra Senn’s voice came from Mica Hand, stationed between the near and middle anchors. “All local traffic clear. Habitat tugs confirm exclusion.”

“Neutral screen?” Sena asked.

“Three cutters in position. No beam projection detected from Continuity.”

“That means Halvek has discovered patience,” Kessa said.

“No,” Orra replied. “It means he has discovered range.”

Continuity held beyond the dust line with six aligned cutters spread around it. Its nine control-beam emitters faced the linked cages. The cruiser could not reach them directly through the neutral screen without passing a relay beam along one of the cutters. Any such transmission would have to cross rescue jurisdiction first.

On Kessa’s tool display, each anchor delivered a broad three-beat load. Near struck first. Middle followed four seconds later. Far answered after another five. The progression had tightened since Day 26. Anu had marked every interval and refused to call the change urgency.

“It indicates approach,” she said now. “Do not assign intention to speed.”

“Wouldn’t dream of it.”

“You assign intention to seized bolts.”

“Bolts have motive.”

Sena glanced toward Kessa. “Crew count complete?”

Kessa read the names. Every station answered. Jori finished last because his gloves covered the rail switch. His camera showed the middle cage’s outer stations curving around darkness, their beams rendered as pale guides by his visor.

“Exterior ready,” he said.

Kessa closed her helmet. Air sealed around her with a pressure tap. “Chain crews, declare local authority.”

Near anchor answered first. Then middle. Far delayed through a final hardline check and joined them. Eleven routekeeper crews had refused Charter hold commands, but only these three would open tonight. If they failed, the other eight would inherit a method written in debris.

Pell started the shared clock.

At minus two minutes, public route telemetry froze. Traffic displays showed three corridors healthy and available, a deliberate last lie preserved for later evidence. Each local crew copied its raw buffers into independent storage. The neutral cutters opened rescue channels and warned every ship that geometry would become uncharted.

Kessa called the abort conditions one last time. A crew could halt before opening its first outer station for any reason. After the outer set discharged, restoration required a local load review, never a command from Threadbare. Loss of voice traffic did not count as failure while hardline timing survived. Loss of both meant that anchor waited in its present state and every nearby vessel withdrew.

Near control repeated the conditions without alteration. Middle added its mechanical shutter warning. Far control named two service boats assigned to catch workers if a tether failed.

Accord of Dust sent a compact acknowledgement from Irian. The convoy commodore did not approve the cut or claim authority over it. She confirmed that every attached habitat had received the corridor warning and that no civilian captain expected a certified route after the attempt. The message ended with a mass plot showing seventy million tonnes held well outside the predicted shear field.

Sena studied it. “She has pointed all that mass somewhere else.”

“She has pointed it nowhere,” Kessa said. “That comes next.”

A final request arrived from Jori on the rail. He wanted the cutter carriage released from its travel lock so he could reach its manual feed if software failed. Kessa approved the first latch and denied the second, keeping the carriage captive to one steel tooth.

“You think it will jump?” Jori asked.

“I think every tool jumps after someone says it cannot.”

He clipped the short work line to his harness. “Then we agree on the tool.”

At minus ninety seconds, Halvek called.

His image appeared on the central pane without its Charter rank border. Someone aboard Continuity had routed him through civilian emergency protocol after Threadbare stripped its registry.

“Routebreaker Rho, the convoy has three major habitat groups dependent on these corridors,” he said. “Your release removes all certified passage across Orison.”

“The corridors will fail when the hollows leave. We can choose whether crews stand inside the failure.”

“You have one partial release and one uncontrolled escape as evidence.”

“We also have Tern.”

“Tern argues against proceeding.”

“Tern argues against combining a cut with your hold.”

Halvek’s gaze dropped, perhaps to the same clock. “Delay four hours. I will establish civilian clearance and withdraw control authority in a documented transfer.”

Kessa checked his ship plot. Two aligned cutters had changed angle by less than a degree, enough to place a relay path between Continuity and far anchor.

“You are establishing a beam line,” she said.

“I am preserving the option to prevent a cascade.”

“Withdraw the cutters.”

“I cannot surrender control of three intersystem corridors to an unauthorized mechanic.”

“You already surrendered their condition to an animal you kept pinned for a century.”

The phrase cost her. Anu looked up but did not correct it.

At minus thirty seconds, Orra cut Halvek’s image from the timing pane. “Relay geometry confirmed. Neutral cutters moving to intercept.”

Kessa rested both boots against the deck. “No one follows Charter time from here. Local pulse and light only.”

The shared clock reached zero.

Twenty-seven resonator stations went dark to the network.

Their work lamps remained. Their cage outputs did not change yet. Public displays lost all three corridor mouths at once, replacing them with unverified black circles. Threadbare’s star tracker dropped into manual mode. The tug shifted half a metre as Sena removed the last automatic correction.

Minute one began with the outer three stations at each anchor discharging.

Near anchor opened cleanly. Its blue lamps drew apart as the hollow’s topology relaxed between them. Far reported a sticky power gate on Station Seven, then cleared it with a local hardline command.

Middle resisted.

Kessa felt the load through the cutter mount before Anu’s trace showed it. One outer station had shed its field. The second followed. Station Eight remained active at nineteen percent and falling too slowly.

“Hold sequence,” Kessa said. “Do not advance.”

Across the lock camera, Jori braced beside the cutter carriage. “Eight’s mechanical shutter did not seat. I can reach the manual dog.”

“You stay on the rail.”

“It will not discharge through software.”

“Vasken, crank from inside.”

The emergency crank turned. Station Eight’s residual load dropped to sixteen percent, then stopped. The shutter status flickered between open and unknown.

Anu enlarged the filament response. “Contraction has begun at near and far. Middle still pushes against the active station.”

The hollow showed no body. Its strain appeared as bent stars tightening around Station Eight and a white work lamp stretching into a curve.

Minute two.

A narrow beam flashed from one aligned cutter toward far anchor.

The neutral cutter Last Measure crossed it with a rescue foil extended. Light scattered across the foil in a green fan. Its captain broadcast the interruption on every channel. “Remote load control denied under immediate hazard authority.”

Halvek ordered the cutter aside.

No one moved.

A second Charter relay fired toward near anchor. Mica Hand severed it by placing a graphite ballast plate in its path, then released the heated plate into open space. Orra’s crew treated military geometry as a rigging problem and made it look almost ordinary.

Station Eight still held sixteen percent.

Jori unhooked his waist tether from the work rail.

Kessa saw the motion on his camera. “Negative. Reattach.”

“The dog sits under the carriage.”

“We abort middle.”

“And leave its outer pair open with Eight holding?”

The question already carried its answer. Restoring the other two outer stations would compress the juvenile during contraction. Leaving them open while Eight held could twist the topology toward the station. They had rehearsed an incomplete discharge, but not a shutter frozen in this exact position.

Jori clipped a short line to the cutter carriage and pulled himself beneath it.

Kessa released her couch straps. “Sena, take the rail.”

“You cannot reach him before minute four.”

“Then stop the clock.”

“The animal does not use Pell’s clock.”

Jori’s camera scraped along metal. The shutter dog appeared beyond the carriage, a recessed lever trembling under field load. Frost broke from it in curved flakes.

“Chief,” he said, “I have worked this drum for nine years. Let me touch my own bad repair.”

Kessa knew that repair. Jori had replaced the shutter actuator at Brine with a cargo-door servo after Charter supply refused their requisition. She had inspected it, signed it, and praised the adaptation. The servo remained sound. The old mechanical dog had bent under Orison’s firing loads.

“Short line stays clipped,” she said. “One attempt. If it does not move, clear.”

“Understood.”

At minute two forty-one, Jori put both boots against the carriage and hauled on the dog.

It moved six centimetres. Station Eight’s load fell through ten percent.

The middle hollow contracted.

Space around the cutter rail shortened without moving any visible object. Jori’s short line snapped tight. His body struck the underside of the carriage. The camera spun, showing stars, metal, then the white hook of Station Eight’s lamp.

“Jori, report.”

No answer came.

Vasken pulled the emergency crank. The dog seated. Residual load dropped to zero.

Kessa stared at the life trace beside Jori’s name. A single red bar crossed it.

“Bring him in,” she said.

Two riggers cycled through the outer lock. They could not interrupt the sequence to retrieve him without leaving his body in changing geometry. They moved along the rail on independent tethers while Kessa kept her hand over the abort control.

Minute three.

All three hollows had entered contraction. Near and far settled within Anu’s predicted range. Middle pulsed harder, injured by the delayed release but no longer pulled toward Station Eight.

“Continue to middle set,” Anu said. Her voice had lost every trace of dry correction. “If we stop now, regrowth will load the open ring.”

Kessa wanted a different answer from the instruments. They gave her falling strain, a narrowing safe interval, and Jori’s flat trace moving toward the lock in his crewmates’ hands.

“Middle crews proceed,” she ordered.

Stations Two, Five, and Nine discharged at near anchor. The corresponding stations at middle followed five seconds late. Far lost hardline comparison for one beat and waited until its light clock confirmed the pulse.

Halvek’s third relay attempt came from Continuity itself.

The cruiser projected a stronger beam between two neutral cutters, using their separation as a slot. It touched the edge of near anchor’s cage and forced Station Two back to four percent output.

The juvenile recoiled. Blue lamps jumped across the plot.

“Sever it,” Kessa said.

Last Measure fired no weapon. It rotated under full drive and dragged its drive plume through the beam path. Ionized exhaust broke the relay into useless flicker. The cutter lost one thruster cluster to feedback and began a slow tumble. Its sister ship left formation to catch it.

Six Charter cutters advanced.

Orra opened a direct channel to them. “Cross this line and you enter active rescue geometry. We will mark every beam source as a hazard to civilian recovery.”

“Neutral cutters have interfered with lawful control,” Halvek said.

“They have prevented a repeat of Tern.”

At minute four, the aligned cutters stopped. Their crews had watched Orison's certified toll reach 147 over the four days since they declared neutral rescue status. None fired through the exclusion plane.

Inside Threadbare’s forward lock, the inner hatch opened. Vasken and the riggers brought Jori in on a work sling. A crescent fracture crossed his visor. His suit had maintained pressure. Geometry had taken him through the carriage faster than the shell could distribute.

Kessa did not leave her station.

Pell removed Jori’s channel from the active roster and moved his name into the sealed work log. Eighteen crew answered the next count.

Minute five brought the contraction wait.

No station changed state. That inactivity required more discipline than cutting. Near hollow’s loads fell in an orderly slope. Far showed a brief inward surge as filaments closed around old resonator scars. Middle carried the delayed injury as an uneven oscillation between Stations Eight and Three.

Anu placed markers at each low point. “Their troughs are separating.”

“How far?”

“Near enters first. Middle follows eleven seconds later. Far needs another eight.”

“The chain plan allowed six.”

“The chain plan did not include a jam.”

Kessa looked at three local clocks. One command could no longer serve every anchor. If each crew acted on its own trough, their departing wakes might cross before the route between them cleared. If they waited for a shared low point, no such point might arrive.

“Sequence by observed pulse trough,” she said. “Near first. Middle follows its own trace. Far does not anticipate.”

The Tern survivor on far anchor asked, “What if near departure changes ours?”

“Then you read the changed load. Do not chase their timing.”

Minute six.

Near anchor’s topology pulled inward until its star arcs nearly touched. The juvenile had shed the outer and middle restraints. Only the core triad held the corridor throat open.

Pell counted the local beats. One. Two. Three.

The pulse trough opened.

Near crew cut its core triad.

Darkness changed posture. A broad lens shape folded away from the cage, compacted, then drew itself along a faint shear line toward middle anchor. No explosion followed. No station tore free. Blue work lamps kept their places while the corridor mouth ceased to exist between them.

Someone on the chain channel made a sound between a laugh and a sob.

“Hold voices,” Kessa said. “Middle reading.”

The first released hollow passed beyond local instruments. Its wake touched the middle cage as a shallow gradient. Anu recomputed the trough from the changed trace.

Minute seven.

Middle’s juvenile pulled against the core triad. Station Eight’s repaired shutter held open. Jori’s camera feed remained black on the edge of Kessa’s display until she closed it.

“Middle trough in four,” Anu said. “Three. Two. Now.”

Kessa pressed the cold-shear trigger.

The cutter did not cut tissue. Its command traveled by hardline to the three local stations, opening each field in a controlled fall. Core loads reached zero within half a second of one another.

For an instant, the hollow stayed.

Then its topology detached from the stations and contracted around an absence no human instrument could resolve. It followed the first wake. The bent white lamp at Station Eight snapped back into a straight point. Metal groaned through Threadbare as ordinary distance reclaimed the rail.

No alarms marked a lensquake.

Minute eight belonged to far anchor.

Its crew had no cold-shear cutter. They worked manual discharge boards while the Tern survivor called each falling load. The juvenile’s pulse shifted when the first two hollows crossed ahead of it. Its trough arrived early.

“Core Three is not ready,” far control said.

“Do not open the other two,” Kessa replied.

“Pulse falling now.”

“Wait for Three.”

The captain swore at his hardware. A relay breaker had welded after the network dark. Two workers struck it with an insulated ram while the trace climbed out of the trough.

Halvek’s forces held position. No fourth beam came.

Minute nine began.

Core Three opened. The next low interval lay twenty-one seconds ahead, beyond the public promise but within the safety margin Kessa had forced into rehearsal.

“Remain dark,” she ordered. “Far crew owns the clock.”

The nine-minute mark passed with no Charter control returning.

At nine minutes fourteen seconds, the pulse began to fall. Far crew released the other core stations together. Amber and white traces collapsed across the board. The last juvenile detached with a rolling gradient that tipped three service craft but left their trajectories recoverable.

Sena fired two short bursts and kept Threadbare clear of its wake.

Three hollows moved away from Orison Gap in sequence. Their compact topologies appeared only through stars drawing close, separating, then drawing close again farther along the migration line. Behind them, twenty-seven resonator stations surrounded nothing.

Pell restored public telemetry. No fixed corridor returned. Traffic boards searched for registered anchor geometry and found open space.

Kessa unsealed her helmet. The cockpit air smelled of hot insulation and the medicinal sealant from the forward lock.

Anu tracked the released juveniles. “Their interval is shortening.”

“How much?” Sena asked.

“The returning adult has increased its approach rate along the open path. Or our earlier model mistook resistance for distance.”

“Deadline?” Kessa said.

Anu moved the projected arrival mark forward. “Sooner. I need the next pulse set before I give you a day.”

On the work log, Jori’s sealed name remained beside eight minutes fifty-one seconds, the rehearsal time he had helped them earn.

Kessa took Tomis’s key from her chest and placed it by the manual controls. Outside, the three released hollows followed their shear thread into darkness. The gap they opened did not stay empty.

A much larger lens moved faster toward it.

Chapter 19 — The Convoy Turns

Irian ordered eleven kilometres of ship to turn toward a route that did not exist.

The command reached Accord of Dust’s thrust control as a proposed vector, not an execution. Around the carrier, attached habitats and freight barges kept their old attitudes while tug clusters moved into new stations. The convoy had spent seventeen days learning how to survive a broken corridor. It had not learned how to rotate 70.4 million tonnes without giving any single engine enough leverage to tear the mass apart.

Irian stood in the logistics pit with both hands against the plot rail. Orison Gap’s three fixed corridors had vanished from the board nine hours earlier. Their registered mouths remained as hollow circles because navigation software preferred a dead road to an empty field. Beyond them, three released juveniles drew faint shear wakes across uncharted space.

No one on the bridge called those wakes a route.

“Cluster Seven reports aligned,” the thrust coordinator said. “Cluster Eight needs six more minutes. Hospital group requests one degree less roll.”

“Give them half a degree and move the seed barges outward,” Irian said.

“That increases the seed truss load.”

“By two percent. The hospital cylinder loses surgical spin at the current rate.”

The coordinator changed the proposal. Green arrows shifted around the convoy, each representing a tug, a connection point, and people responsible for a cable they could not see from the bridge.

Kindly Argument occupied a protected pocket beside Accord’s second drum. Its surgeons had worked through Orison’s 147 dead and the larger number injured. The cylinder carried patients who could not tolerate a long roll transient. Six seed barges on the opposite side carried no conscious passengers, only machine stock and crop cultures meant to feed settlements that no longer had fixed access to one another. Both mattered. Only one could report pain.

Irian reduced the hospital roll first.

Her maneuver board divided the turn into thirty-seven thrust intervals over four days. Each interval moved some portion of the convoy through a fraction of a degree. Tugs pulled habitat drums by their reinforced tow nodes. Barges coasted through larger arcs. Accord used its main drives only after the surrounding mass established a shared rotation.

If one cluster burned early, cables snapped. If a habitat lagged, its attached water bladders swung through neighboring traffic. If Irian waited for perfect synchronization, the released wake would decay before they learned where it led.

A warning opened over the old corridor marks. FIXED ROUTE SOLUTION UNAVAILABLE. SEEK CHARTER GUIDANCE.

The bridge crew ignored it with practiced contempt.

Marl Quade came down the ladder into the pit carrying three manual navigation slates and a coil of physical voting tags. Condensation darkened the shoulders of his work jacket. Brine’s passenger delegation had borrowed a cargo room near the water controls because no chamber aboard Accord could hold representatives from so many habitats.

“You started the turn,” he said.

“I started tug positioning.”

“People can feel tug positioning.”

“They can feel the carrier preparing not to tear itself apart.”

Marl put the slates on her rail. “Then tell them which direction their bodies are being prepared for.”

Irian looked through the bridge windows. Engine lights marked a slow wheel around Accord. Beyond them, Threadbare held near the first released wake, taking measurements where charts displayed nothing useful.

“The juvenile path leads away from Sable’s fixed approach,” she said. “It gives us a moving gradient with lower fuel cost than open fusion transit.”

“Lower does not mean available to every ship.”

“No.”

“How many fail the turn?”

“None, if the tug plan holds.”

“That did not answer me.”

Marl’s strength as a delegate lived in refusing the comforting unit. A convoy could complete a maneuver while a habitat lost heat, a clinic lost spin, or a family decided that a local shelter offered better odds.

Irian opened the readiness table. “Forty-one modules can follow the wake at projected acceleration. Nineteen need shared tow. Seven cannot sustain the required thrust cycle without reducing spin or life-support load. Three of those can shelter at Orison service habitats. Four need repair or a crew transfer.”

“And the people aboard them?”

“Seventy-eight thousand, total across the seven.”

Marl read the names instead of the count. “Have they been told?”

“Their captains received the table.”

“Their passengers did not elect those engines.”

A thrust-status bar changed from amber to green. Cluster Eight had aligned. The first maneuver interval could begin as soon as Irian authorized it.

She left the command unsigned.

“Convene the passenger delegates,” she said. “Captains too. We have forty minutes before the current geometry costs us the window.”

“Forty minutes to consent to a four-day turn?”

“Forty minutes to tell me what authority I do not have.”

Marl gathered the slates. “That may take longer.”

“It usually does.”

The cargo room had once held agricultural pumps. Their mounting brackets remained on the walls, each labeled for a Brine cylinder that might never receive it. Passenger delegates sat on folded crates or joined by local relay from habitats moving beyond easy boat range. Captains occupied the rear, an arrangement Marl had chosen to prevent rank from controlling who could see the working displays.

Irian entered without the commodore’s white bridge jacket. She still wore its command collar. Symbols mattered less when denied badly.

Marl opened under the Free Convoy Articles. “This session does not certify the later crossing near the returning adult. We decide the immediate turn out of failed fixed-route geometry and the distribution of authority during that maneuver. Any ship may request shelter instead of following. No captain may treat staying as mutiny.”

A habitat captain objected before he finished. “Individual refusal fractures tow geometry. My drum cannot detach from the carrier group without taking two water trains with it.”

“Then describe that dependence to your passengers,” Marl said. “Do not rename it command.”

Irian projected the maneuver plan across the floor. The convoy appeared as mass and tension, each line colored by its least forgiving connection. She spoke in usable margins. Four days of thrust. A maximum roll loss of eleven percent in the outer drums. Fuel reserve falling to twenty-two percent if all seventy units followed. Higher cost for any late reversal. Shelter capacity at Orison limited by air, food, and radiation shielding.

“The released wakes do not remain fixed,” she said. “We have measured their first direction, not their destination. Threadbare can track them. No certified chart supports the maneuver.”

A delegate from an evacuation habitat raised her tag. “Does the wake lead to Brine?”

“No.”

“Does it lead away from Sable?”

“Yes, on present observation.”

“My family came from Sable. We were told the convoy would circle back when the unrest settled.”

“That promise depended on fixed corridors.”

“Then who cancels it?”

Irian let the room hold the question. A commodore could cancel a destination under emergency authority. She had done so during fires, epidemics, and one reactor loss. This change carried no alternate port, only motion alongside an animal’s wake while the old network failed behind them.

“I can cancel an unsafe route,” she said. “I cannot choose what every ship considers survival. The maneuver needs coordinated thrust, but the destination decision must remain with each captain and passenger body under the Articles.”

A murmur moved across the relay panes. Captains did not enjoy hearing pieces of command separated in public.

Marl pointed to the first interval. “Who controls this burn?”

“Local tug masters control ignition within a shared window,” Irian said. “Habitat captains can abort their own tow. Accord holds the mass plot and publishes corrections.”

“Who decides which modules reduce spin?”

“The affected habitat and medical crew.”

“Who decides whether a noncompliant ship gets cut loose?”

Irian examined the tension web. Under naval practice, she would. Under convoy practice as she had used it until Brine, the answer still tended to arrive at her console.

“The neighboring tow group,” she said. “With a passenger delegate on the channel. Any cut order enters the public log before execution unless immediate collision prevents it.”

A freight captain laughed without humor. “By the time a committee agrees, my barge will be through someone’s drum.”

“Then immediate collision grants action. Convenience does not.”

The room worked through the turn one authority at a time. Fuel distribution moved to a council of tug masters because no bridge officer could see every engine’s condition. Berth transfer remained with receiving habitats. Medical triage stayed aboard Kindly Argument. Irian retained the right to call shared thrust windows and close any vector that endangered the full convoy.

What remained of central command grew narrower and more honest.

The seven weak modules demanded separate decisions.

Three captains requested Orison shelter. Their passenger delegates confirmed the choice after publishing air projections and the risks of isolation. They carried nineteen thousand people among them. No one applauded. Shelter meant staying beside dismantled anchors with local service habitats, limited industry, and no guaranteed relief corridor.

Four modules chose repair and tow reassignment. One transferred children and intensive-care patients before the turn. Another unloaded a machine shop to reduce mass. A third surrendered half its water reserve to a stronger drum in exchange for shared thrust. The last required a replacement reactor feed that existed only on a seed barge.

The seed custodian refused at first. “If I strip that feed, we lose automated germination control.”

A delegate asked, “Can people run it manually?”

“For how long?”

“How long do we need?”

The answer moved through agronomists, maintenance workers, and the Brine crews who had operated food-substrate vats without automation during strike years. They could cover one planting cycle by hand. The reactor feed changed owners.

Irian watched expertise flow around rank. Every concession slowed the plan. Each also removed a hidden assumption that would have failed under thrust.

At thirty-six minutes, Marl called for a procedural count. Passenger delegates authorized the immediate turn by a margin large enough to act, with three shelter groups abstaining because they no longer joined the maneuver. Captains accepted the distributed authority table. Several entered formal objections beside their acceptance.

“Objections remain visible,” Marl said.

“So do cables,” Irian replied. “Both help when they carry load.”

She returned to the bridge with four minutes left in the initial window.

The logistics pit had changed the plan while she was gone. Tug masters annotated the thrust intervals in local language, with repair times and hand signals where network timing might fail. Accord’s neat green arrows had become a thicket of smaller marks. Irian understood less of it at a glance and trusted more of its detail.

“Shelter groups separating,” the thrust coordinator said. “They request supply release before Interval Three.”

Irian opened the distributed stores. Brine had already divided food substrate among many ships. The sheltering habitats needed more than food. They needed water reclaim membranes, machine tools, radiation foil, medical cultures, and fuel cells capable of surviving a long quiet.

Every item removed from the convoy reduced its endurance. Every item withheld turned the right to stay into a sentence.

“Allocate six months at strict ration,” she said. “One full repair shop. Two seed libraries, not one. Include manual fabrication patterns.”

“Six months leaves us below the crossing reserve.”

“Recalculate across the reduced population and mass.”

“They chose shelter.”

“They did not choose abandonment.”

The coordinator changed the table. Supplies began moving by small tug before the larger rotation made transfer dangerous.

Interval One opened.

Irian placed her authorization beside the tug council’s signatures. Forty-one cluster leaders acknowledged. Nineteen shared-tow modules reported ready. The four repaired weak ships joined under reduced mass. Three shelter habitats held outside the web with their service craft clustered around them.

“Execute local burns,” she said.

Engine lights brightened in sequence.

Nothing dramatic happened. That reassured her more than spectacle could. The outer barges began slow arcs. Water trains tensioned their cables. Kindly Argument preserved surgical spin within half a degree. Across eleven kilometres, Accord’s truss accumulated load and passed it through braces designed to survive route entry, not a turn in open interstellar space.

The ship moved by fractions too small for the eye. Irian felt them through deck vibration and the changing work of her knees.

During Interval Two, a seed barge lagged six seconds. Its tow group aborted before the mismatch reached the truss. The public log showed the decision without requesting Irian’s permission. Tug masters shifted two craft, rewrote the local burn, and rejoined at the next window.

During Interval Four, a habitat reduced spin farther than planned to protect a cracked tow node. Its passengers floated for nineteen minutes. Their delegate filed the medical effects and kept the captain’s choice in force.

Interval Nine brought the first conflict the authority table could not settle neatly. A water train named Blue Remaining reported one tug losing coolant while its neighboring habitat demanded the train hold formation. The tug master wanted to drop the outer bladder. The habitat captain refused because that water covered eleven days of reserve for thirty-four thousand people.

Under the old command structure, both requests would have climbed to Irian. This time the tow group opened its local channel to a passenger delegate, a water technician, and the two nearest tug masters. Irian listened without taking control.

“The bladder survives a free coast,” the failing tug master said. “Mark it and recover after the turn.”

“It crosses Cluster Twelve’s arc in twenty minutes,” the coordinator replied from Accord.

“Then Cluster Twelve shifts.”

“That shift loads a hospital tow.”

The water technician overlaid the bladder’s internal baffles. “Do not drop the whole tank. Empty the forward third into the carrier line, then split the tow at the middle collar. Lower mass clears Twelve’s arc.”

“Transfer takes fourteen minutes,” the habitat captain said.

“We own twenty-one before collision risk.”

The passenger delegate asked the question Irian had trained bridge officers not to ask during a maneuver. “What fails if we wait?”

Answers arrived from each role. The tug might lose its pump in nine minutes, but a bypass could extend that margin. The habitat tow carried six percent spare tension. Cluster Twelve could delay one burn without losing the shared window. Kindly Argument would need advance warning before the resulting vibration reached its berth.

They chose the transfer. A maintenance worker bypassed the pump while water crossed through a flexible line into Accord’s aft reserve. The bladder lost a third of its mass, the tow split at the collar, and two smaller tugs took its shortened train. Interval Nine ran seventeen minutes late.

No cable snapped. No captain surrendered command wholesale. Irian recorded which information had made the choice possible and left the decision under the tow group’s names.

Her thrust coordinator exhaled after the final clearance. “We could have ruled in thirty seconds.”

“We could have guessed in thirty seconds,” Irian said.

“The next one may not give us twenty-one minutes.”

“Then immediate authority still exists. This one gave us time, so we used it.”

The coordinator looked at the repaired geometry. “It also gave us more work.”

“Most survival does.”

By the end of the first day, Accord had rotated seven degrees.

The old corridors remained off her port quarter, black rings around empty centers. Navigation software continued offering recovery burns toward them. Each suggestion assumed an anchor mouth that no longer existed.

Irian deleted the route package.

On the second day, the shelter groups received their last machine crates. A child’s recorded message crossed to Accord, asking whether the carrier would come back after finding a road. The bridge did not answer on behalf of the child’s family. Marl routed the question to their delegate, who promised only continued radio contact while distance allowed.

The convoy reached nineteen degrees.

Fuel use ran one point below Irian’s worst case because local tug masters had found efficiencies the central model missed. Tow fatigue ran higher. Two drums reported bearing heat. A freight barge cracked an attachment collar and redistributed cargo without dropping from formation.

Threadbare moved ahead along the juvenile wakes. Kessa sent measured gradients instead of destination claims. The shear threads curved toward the threatened chain, not along any corridor humans had used. Sable lay near their projected course.

On the third day, Halvek transmitted a formal order to halt.

He offered one remaining fixed route beyond Orison, reachable through a costly reverse burn. Its cage still held. His message omitted the amplification model and described the three released hollows as an uncontrolled route loss.

Irian posted the order to every captain and passenger delegate. She attached current fuel costs without comment.

Reversing at once would consume twenty-nine percent of available reserve and place the convoy behind a cage known to fail harder under accumulated holds. Continuing along the wake cost eighteen percent under the current tug plan. The difference widened with every interval.

A captain asked Irian whether Halvek’s route remained legally available.

“Legality does not add fuel,” she said.

“Would you take it if the cost matched?”

She looked at the black corridor marks, then at the moving gradient from Threadbare. “I would ask what holds it open.”

That answer entered the public log.

By Day 33's final shared burn, the convoy had rotated past forty degrees. Its main truss pointed along the wake. Barges and habitats occupied a stretched formation built for thrust rather than corridor compression. The weak modules held. The shelter groups dwindled behind them among Orison's dead stations, supplied but alone.

The maneuver board displayed a new line across Accord’s projected path. Navigation marked it the point of no return because ships preferred an old phrase to a long equation. Beyond that line, returning to Halvek’s fixed route required more fuel than continuing into uncharted space.

Irian sent the calculation to Marl, the captains, and every local tug master. Acknowledgements arrived in no fixed order.

“Interval Thirty,” the thrust coordinator said. “All groups ready.”

Irian did not make a speech. “Publish and execute.”

Engines lit around seventy million tonnes of inhabited machinery. Accord of Dust gathered the pull through its truss and crossed the line one slow metre at a time.

The recovery vectors behind them turned red.

Chapter 20 — The Hollowing of Sable

Sable’s orbital ring broke seventeen minutes before Threadbare reached it.

Kessa watched the first fracture arrive as light, not telemetry. A white segment above the planet bent upward where no ring plane should have allowed it, remained there for three breaths, then divided into two images. One copy showed the segment intact. The other showed it folding through the elevator web.

Sena killed the approach burn.

Everyone aboard the tug leaned into restraints as thrust ended. Sable filled the cupola, a high-gravity world wrapped in cloud and industrial haze. Surface cities burned in night-side grids. They remained level, bright, and alive. Above them, the orbital works had lost every dependable direction.

“Station clocks disagree by eight seconds,” Pell said.

“Throw them out,” Kessa replied. “Use our pulses.”

Threadbare sent a radar burst toward the nearest ring sector. Three returns came back. The first followed ordinary distance. The second arrived late from a segment their eyes placed farther east. The third described metal already passing below them.

Sena rolled the tug hard.

A ring truss crossed their old path with no warning from the traffic plot. It had once carried an elevator receiving station, cargo rails, and housing for four thousand workers. Now one end turned toward Sable while the other climbed. Shattered radiators dragged behind it like pale scales.

Kessa found the fracture point by load instead of sight. The broken truss pulled nearby debris into a curved stream. Every fragment accelerated along a local gradient left by the cage failure.

“Mark what moves together,” she said. “Ignore where the cameras put it.”

Pell rebuilt the plot around shared acceleration. False images faded to outlines. Real objects gained thick white vectors. A habitat drum spun near the ring’s inner edge with one bearing gone. Two elevator cables had wrapped around its axis. An emergency beacon repeated from three apparent positions.

Anu sat at the ecology bench with both hands locked beneath its rail. “The juvenile detached. Severe asymmetry. It crossed through the ring plane six minutes ago.”

“Where now?”

“I have several answers.”

“Give Sena the one that can hit us.”

Anu sent a broad hazard cone across the plot. It cut between Threadbare and the damaged habitat.

Sable control transmitted a systemwide evacuation order. Half its sentences overlapped themselves. One version directed craft to the outer rescue orbit. Another placed that orbit through the falling ring segment. Kessa muted both.

“Habitat beacon first,” she said. “Then the elevator cars.”

Sena did not ask which elevator cars. Hundreds showed on the broken web. Some still climbed along cables whose upper stations no longer occupied a stable orbit. Others had stopped and become small sealed rooms suspended above a planet.

They approached the drum from below its rotation plane. Threadbare’s one working grapple could not arrest the full spin. Kessa assigned three local tugs to pull against separate tow nodes while Sena matched the damaged bearing’s wobble.

No shared camera agreed on the node positions.

Kessa went outside.

Sable’s gravity pulled through her suit with more insistence than any deep-space job. The ring had turned workers’ weight into spin; outside it, every loose tool preferred the planet. She clipped to Threadbare’s forward rail and crawled toward the grapple housing while stars split around the departing juvenile’s wake.

Vasken fed the line. His voice remained flat after Jori’s death, each word stripped to work.

“Drum node crossing in eleven.”

“Whose eleven?”

“Ours.”

“Keep it that way.”

The habitat rolled into view. Its skin carried Brine algae paint beneath fresh evacuation markings. A window band showed darkness across most decks. One lit compartment flashed a hand lamp in repeated pairs.

Kessa aimed the grapple at the node’s acceleration path, not its visible image. Sena gave her half a metre of correction. She fired.

The tooth vanished through a delayed picture of the drum. For one sick instant it seemed to miss. Then the line took load eight degrees to the left and pulled Kessa against the rail.

“Contact,” Vasken said.

“Local tugs, take your thirds.”

Three lines tightened around the habitat. Its spin slowed unevenly. The elevator cables wound around its axis began to pull taut, connecting the drum to a broken ring segment below. If they reached full load, they would either cut through the habitat or drag the rescue formation toward Sable.

Kessa moved the cold-shear cutter off its carriage. The tool had opened a juvenile cage four days earlier. Now she set it against human cable.

“People in the cars?” she asked.

Pell tracked pressure beacons along the wrapped lines. “Four cars. Two hundred sixteen registered passengers. The lowest car still has independent brake contact.”

“Can it transfer to the surface line?”

“Sable ground control says yes. Their picture is twelve seconds old.”

A voice broke onto the channel from the lowest car. “We have visual on the junction. Cable Seven remains attached below us. Cut above Car Four and we can descend.”

Kessa found Car Four as a moving load signature. “What about the other three?”

No answer came for two seconds. Then a rescue controller said, “Cars One through Three lost pressure during ring fracture.”

The number of living choices narrowed.

Kessa anchored the cutter above Car Four. “Ground control, take brake authority locally. No ring timing.”

“Confirmed.”

“Car Four, prepare for a hard catch.”

A woman inside answered, “We have children out of restraints.”

“Put them in any closed bunk and fill the spaces with clothing. You have ninety seconds.”

The habitat drum rolled overhead. Kessa watched its grapple load climb while the three tugs fought to hold alignment. A second ring fragment entered the hazard plot, two minutes away by shared acceleration and twenty seconds away by one camera.

Sena trusted the load plot. “Fragment passes behind us.”

“Unless the wake changes.”

“Everything passes somewhere unless the wake changes.” Her tone held no humor. “Cut when they are tied.”

Car Four confirmed.

Kessa closed the cold-shear jaws around three elevator cables. These lines had carried ore, food, shift workers, and children between Sable’s surface and orbital ring. Their designers had provided emergency separation bolts at the cars, not halfway through a web wrapped around a habitat.

She cut the first cable. Stored tension kicked the housing and sent a wave down the remaining two. The habitat shifted. One local tug lost its tow node and spun away trailing line.

Vasken reeled Threadbare’s grapple to compensate.

The second cable parted cleanly.

A gravity pulse crossed the scene before Kessa touched the third. Stars lurched. Her suit altimeter gained six kilometres, lost nine, then blanked. The habitat’s delayed image swept through her body while the physical drum remained above.

“Kessa, cut now,” Sena said.

She drove the jaws closed.

The third cable separated. The wrapped upper web sprang away from the habitat. Car Four dropped along Cable Seven toward the surface junction, brakes firing in bright red bursts. Its beacon descended inside the atmosphere corridor and stayed pressurized.

The three dead cars fell with the severed upper lines.

Kessa had no tool that could change that fact.

The habitat’s spin slowed enough for docking boats to reach it. Pressure counts arrived compartment by compartment. Two thousand nine hundred people remained aboard. Sixty-three needed immediate care. Eleven had died during bearing failure, already part of Sable’s growing total.

“Hand rescue to local tugs,” Kessa said. “Next load.”

She stayed outside for four hours.

The next call came from Ring Clinic Twelve, a pressure ward mounted across two truss bays near the damaged inner rail. Its medical drum had survived the first fracture. Its docking neck had not. Eighty-one patients, fourteen staff, and three visitors occupied a cylinder whose only exit now opened onto six metres of vacuum.

The clinic's reserve tug remained attached by one tow node, but every burn rolled the ward toward a severed elevator cable. Sable control wanted the tug to hold position until a rescue shell arrived. The shell's cameras placed the clinic in three locations and chose the brightest.

Kessa rejected the feed. “Clinic, strike your hull from inside.”

A medic answered, “With what?”

“Anything heavy enough to travel through the frame. Three blows, pause, one.”

The first pattern came through Threadbare's contact microphone after fourteen seconds. Kessa put the sound against the raw radar returns. One return vibrated. The other two remained perfect ghosts.

“Mark the noisy one,” she said.

Sena brought the tug beneath it while Mica Hand ranged from above. The clinic appeared to slide through Sable's clouded horizon, but its acceleration matched the damaged truss beside it. Kessa fired a grapple into the surviving dock collar and felt ninety-eight living bodies enter the drum.

“Load shared,” Vasken reported. “Clinic tug still carries sixty percent.”

The rescue shell approached on Kessa's corrected mark. Its pilot saw empty stars until the shell crossed the delayed image and nearly struck the clinic's radiator.

“Break right,” Sena called.

The shell fired. One side of its plume entered a folded gradient and pushed less than the other. It began to spin.

Kessa paid out grapple line so Threadbare did not pull the clinic after it. Mica Hand cast a second line across the rescue shell's tail and stopped the rotation. For several seconds the three craft held one another without sharing the same visible sky.

The clinic director sent a patient priority list. Nine required powered beds. Twenty-three could move in pressure sacks. Four had open surgical fields protected by local tents. The remaining patients could cross in suits if the gap gained a rail and enough time.

Time offered neither.

A broken ring segment behind the clinic had begun accelerating along the juvenile's wake. At current load it would cross the ward's local position in eleven minutes. Sable control predicted twenty. The hull strikes and grapple tension agreed with eleven.

“We take the powered beds first,” Kessa said.

The director refused. “Their couplers cannot pass the damaged neck. Cutting the bed frames takes eight minutes.”

“Move the people without the frames.”

“Two die if power drops.”

Anu entered from the tug. “Route portable cells through suit umbilicals. The clinic spine uses standard forty-eight volt medical bus.”

A biomedical technician objected that no suit lead carried certified isolation for an implanted pump.

Kessa looked at the crossing fragment. “Certification arrives after contact.”

The technician built two leads from monitor cables, tested each against a water load, and wrapped the exposed junctions in pressure tape. The first powered patient crossed the vacuum gap inside a rigid sack while a medic hand-carried the cell. The second followed on a makeshift rail fired between the open clinic neck and the rescue shell.

Every transfer changed the clinic's balance. As people left, the empty ward became harder to hold. Fluid tanks sloshed inside their restraints. A surgical centrifuge kept turning after its room had emptied, pulling the truss off-axis.

“Kill the centrifuge,” Kessa said.

“Brake control lost.”

“Can you vent its flywheel?”

The clinic engineer found a mechanical dump behind a wall panel. Opening it would release fragments into the ward's service bay.

“Bay clear?”

“One technician.”

“Move her.”

The technician had gone back for the controlled-drug case. The director ordered her out. She argued that pain stock for ninety-eight people would be lost with the ward. Kessa listened to thirty seconds disappear.

“Take two days of doses,” she said. “Leave the cabinet.”

The technician filled a pressure pouch and cleared the bay. The engineer dumped the flywheel. Ceramic fragments crossed the empty service space and buried themselves in insulation. The clinic's roll slowed enough for the transfer rail to hold alignment.

Pressure sacks came next. Then suited walkers. One visitor refused to leave because his daughter remained under a surgical tent. Staff moved him with the tent team so the argument did not consume another crossing. The last medic carried the clinic register inside her suit rather than trusting a network that kept correcting people into false positions.

“Ward clear,” the director reported.

The ring fragment reached six minutes.

Kessa checked the clinic tug. Its crew had stayed attached through every transfer. “Release your tow and come across.”

The pilot said, “If we release, the empty ward rolls into the shell.”

Sena laid a burn along the tug's local acceleration. “Push at three degrees below your apparent horizon. One second only. Then cut free.”

“That points at Sable.”

“Your camera points at Sable. Your load does not.”

The pilot trusted her. The tug burned, the clinic moved aside, and both tow bolts fired. Threadbare reeled its grapple while Mica Hand pulled the rescue shell outward. The abandoned ward crossed behind them just as the fragment entered its volume.

Metal met metal without a visible collision. One image passed through untouched. The physical clinic folded around the fragment and shed its pressure shell in bright panels.

Ninety-eight beacons remained aboard the rescue shell.

Kessa recorded the lost ward under salvage abandoned after evacuation. The designation preserved the order of events: people first, hardware after. Sable's casualty system had already begun turning uncertain signals into categories. She made this one carry a verified count before moving to the next call.

Work lost any clean chapter of time. Threadbare pulled a machine shop away from the ring’s broken inner rail. Mica Hand caught a tumbling fuel depot with two grapples and vented half its propellant before heat reached the tanks.
