Neutral cutters established corridors by repeated radar pulse, each craft publishing raw transit times instead of corrected positions.

Elevator cars descended where lower cables survived. Others transferred passengers into climbing rescue shells. Kessa crossed a snapped web line hand over hand to reach a car whose docking collar had bent shut. She and Vasken opened it with mechanical wedges while Sable dragged at their boots.

Inside, thirty-eight people shared air meant for twenty. A boy held a ceramic worker token in both fists and would not release it when the rescuers fitted his suit hood. Kessa clipped the token inside the hood so he could see it.

“Does the ring fall on the city?” he asked.

“Ground crews are moving the pieces they can.”

“That is not no.”

“No.” Kessa checked his seal. “It is not no.”

Sable’s surface defense tugs pushed the largest fragments into ocean corridors or higher temporary orbits. Some debris entered the atmosphere over uninhabited industrial basins. White tracks crossed the clouds. Surface grids dimmed beneath them, then came back. The planet survived because worlds carried more mass than cages, rings, and human certainty.

The orbital habitats did not share that scale.

At the west transfer cluster, six cylinders had torn free. Four retained pressure. One opened along a maintenance seam and emptied before any tug could reach it. The sixth drifted inside the wounded juvenile’s chaotic wake, appearing on instruments at positions separated by hundreds of kilometres.

Kessa refused camera lock and read its distress beacon through Doppler shift. Sena matched the acceleration pattern. They found the cylinder thirty kilometres below its brightest image.

Its spin had stopped. People floated behind the windows, moving hand over hand through emergency lights. A routekeeper tug remained attached to the cylinder’s tail with engines dead.

“Route crew reports seven alive,” Pell said. “They stayed to move the habitat clear of Cage Station Five.”

“Why were they at the cage after the union refusal?” Vasken asked.

No one answered until the tug transmitted its work orders.

CHARTER CONTINUITY PRIORITY. MAINTAIN DOUBLED HOLD. LOCAL WORKER STOP SUSPENDED. STATION EVACUATION DEFERRED UNTIL CORRIDOR STABILITY.

Halvek’s authorization code occupied the bottom of every page.

Kessa read the timestamps. Sable’s operations chief had received the union declaration on Day 26. The chief had kept the cage crews at their posts under Charter order. They had loaded all nine stations beyond nominal limits while orbital evacuations waited for a stable corridor that never came.

“Seven alive,” she said. “We get them.”

Station Five’s wreckage turned between Threadbare and the routekeeper tug. Its resonator bowl still carried a residual field. Each rotation pulled the dead tug sideways and released it again.

A direct grapple would cross the bowl’s load path. Kessa used the remaining elevator cable wound around their rail. Vasken objected once because its cut strands could unzip under tension. She showed him the doubled splice she wanted, and they built it together.

Sena placed Threadbare above the wreckage. Kessa and Vasken paid the cable out until its weighted end curved around the resonator bowl. A local tug caught the end on the far side, creating a loose loop. When Station Five rotated, the loop guided its residual pull into both ships instead of the dead tug.

“Load coming,” Kessa said.

The cable tightened. Cut strands lifted from the splice like silver hair. Threadbare’s hull complained from lock to engine brace.

The routekeeper tug drifted free.

A rescue boat crossed the cleared path and docked with it. Seven suited workers emerged. Two could move without help. Five came on rigid stretchers.

Behind them, the cylinder’s passengers transferred to local boats. The last person left twelve minutes before another lens pulse folded the empty structure in half.

Kessa watched it go without counting the machinery. Pell counted people.

The seven route workers refused evacuation to Kindly Argument until they knew what had happened to their station crew. Two had broken ribs. One carried a pressure burn across his neck where a suit collar had sealed late. Their shift lead, a woman named Esra Vale, kept asking for Station Five’s work log between doses of pain control.

Kessa brought the log to the rescue boat herself.

Esra lay strapped to a rigid board beneath portable lamps. Sable’s gravity reached them even in orbit, faint but persistent, pulling every loose line toward the planet. She read the final commands on Kessa’s wrist display and stopped at the order extending doubled hold through local evacuation.

“We requested release twice,” Esra said.

“The requests appear here.”

“Operations told us Continuity had accepted responsibility.”

Kessa enlarged the authorization chain. Halvek’s code approved the general priority. Sable operations supplied the local order and the names of supervisors who kept the crews in place. Responsibility had traveled through enough hands for every person to claim they held only one small part.

“Did the chief survive?” Esra asked.

“Not Station Two.”

“He had a son groundside.”

Kessa gave her the family-liaison channel rather than a phrase intended to soften it. Esra pressed the display against her chest until a medic removed it for transfer.

The rescue boat descended to a surface freight terminal because orbital beds had filled. Threadbare escorted it through an elevator corridor cleared of falling cable. Kessa watched atmosphere turn the cupola edge blue. Below the cloud deck, industrial cities extended across dark plateaus. Power districts had separated themselves from the damaged elevator grid and kept hospitals, pumps, and shelters running.

No impossible tide lifted streets. Buildings did not leave their foundations. Sable’s surface carried alarms, falling debris tracks, and the sudden loss of orbital industry, but the world endured beneath the local violence.

At the terminal, cargo halls received people instead of ore. Floor crews painted berth rectangles onto concrete. Water tanks meant for elevator counterweights fed field kitchens through temporary pipes. Families waited beneath assignment boards for names arriving from incomplete passenger lists.

Kessa crossed the loading apron in a high-gravity support harness to inspect the rescue boat’s damaged collar. Every tool pulled hard at her belt. Surface workers had no time to recognize Threadbare’s unregistered crew as mutineers. They passed her sealant, asked for measurements, and moved to the next hull.

A shelter coordinator caught her beside the collar. “Are you carrying more down?”

“As long as orbital control can give us a real vector.”

“Current displacement planning uses 31,000. That includes everyone removed from ring housing and the elevator stations. If your manifest duplicates a transfer, mark it before landing.”

Kessa checked Pell’s roster. Six names appeared twice because a damaged habitat and a rescue tug had both claimed them. She corrected the entries and added the seven route workers under the rescue boat’s actual berth.

The coordinator watched the count change by one digit. “People think this board tells them whether someone lived.”

“What does it tell you?”

“How much air to move into this hall.” She pointed toward the temporary pipes crossing the ceiling. “Living takes administration after rescue.”

Kessa sent the corrected roster to Sable control. On the far side of the cargo hall, the boy with the ceramic worker token sat inside a painted rectangle with three adults. He still held the token against his suit hood. A meal worker handed him a cup and waited until high gravity stopped his arm from shaking.

Threadbare could remain on the surface for eleven minutes before its engines lost the orbital window. Kessa finished the collar patch in nine. As the tug climbed, she saw the first managed ring fragment enter atmosphere over an empty industrial basin. It burned orange across the clouds. The city grids below did not blink.

By the seventh hour, Sable control had one reliable orbital picture built from thousands of raw pings. The ring no longer formed a circle. Three long sections remained in managed orbit. Two had entered atmospheric disposal paths. Fragments surrounded the elevator web, most drifting away from surface corridors. Emergency shelters on the ground reported crowding but no city loss.

Thirty-one thousand people had been displaced from orbital structures. The number included families in surface terminals, workers aboard rescue craft, and patients packed into industrial bunkers. It did not turn them into cargo. It told water crews how many mouths arrived before the next filtration cycle.

The fatality register kept changing.

Kessa came inside when her suit joints stopped answering cleanly. Sena pushed a heated drink into her hands and returned to the manual plot. Anu had tracked the wounded juvenile through the entire rescue. Its wake cut a ragged course outward from Sable, crossing and recrossing itself as damaged topology tried to align with the migration pulse.

“It escaped,” Anu said. “But the core filament load remains asymmetric. It cannot follow the others cleanly.”

“Can we reach it?”

“Not without abandoning rescue range. The adult may reach it first.”

Kessa looked at the bent path. The animal had left its cage. Freedom did not repair what the doubled hold had done.

Pell brought the final route-worker list to the cockpit. Eighty-four names. Some had died in the stations. Others died aboard tugs sent to maintain beam alignment while the ring began to twist. Every one had an acknowledged Charter order attached.

Tomis’s death had taught Kessa to distrust a report that used error as a grave marker. Sable supplied eighty-four new versions of the same work.

“Keep the orders with the names,” she said. “No separate evidence file.”

Pell nodded. “Casualty control wants our rescued count before they lock the register.”

They gave it.

Near the end of Day 33, Sable published the stabilized total: 2,406 dead. No estimate hid behind the figure. Search zones remained, but control had accounted for every occupied ring sector, elevator car, habitat, and rescue craft. The surface survived. Thirty-one thousand displaced people had berths or ground-shelter assignments, though many held only a floor marker and a ration code.

No one aboard Threadbare spoke when the total arrived.

A new drive signature appeared beyond the debris field.

Continuity approached under rescue lights with six Charter cutters behind it. The cruiser had taken three days to follow the convoy’s turn and the wounded wake. Its control beams stayed dark.

Sable traffic assigned it a holding vector above the broken eastern ring. Halvek accepted without claiming priority.

Kessa opened the casualty register on a public pane. She placed the eighty-four route workers first, each name linked to the doubled-hold order. Beneath them came the remaining 2,322 dead and the shelter distribution for the living. She attached the amplification model his office had suppressed.

“Send direct to Continuity,” she said.

Pell transmitted it.

The cruiser acknowledged receipt.

Through the cupola, Continuity crossed behind a ring segment twisted into an open arc. Its hull lights traced the broken metal for a moment, giving Halvek a view no corrected Charter plot could flatten. Below both, Sable’s surface cities continued burning in orderly grids.

The ring no longer closed around them.

Act 3

Chapter 21 — The Routekeepers Divide

Halvek read the dead into the navigation table one name at a time.

The bridge of Continuity had dimmed its main plot because Sable’s damaged orbital ring kept throwing false mass into every solution. Beneath the red emergency lamps, the casualty list occupied the surface where Halvek had once displayed seventy-three reliable routes. He used his finger rather than voice control. Each touch opened a service record, a berth assignment, a recovery note when anyone had recovered enough to identify.

Aris Pell, resonator electrician, thirty-four. Ordered to Hold Station Six after the first gradient alarm.

Mera and Dol Tann, married riggers, fifty-one and forty-nine. Found inside an elevator-web junction they had kept powered for evacuation traffic.

Taren Ves, apprentice routekeeper, nineteen. Last transmission requested permission to abandon the core triad. Permission denied by Charter control twelve seconds before the lensquake.

The list continued past the edge of the table. Two thousand four hundred and six people had died in Sable’s orbital structures. Thirty-one thousand had lost their homes above the planet, though the surface cities remained intact. Halvek had known both numbers before he entered the bridge. Numbers gave command a surface it could grip. Names passed through that surface.

Behind him, Commander Rusk waited without shifting his boots. Six loyal cutters held close formation around the cruiser. Three others, still claiming neutral rescue status, kept station near the broken ring. Their captains had sent a joint demand for Halvek’s arrest and preservation of all command records.

“Marshal,” Rusk said. “The outer rescue cordon reports another unauthorized tug entering Sable space.”

“Routekeeper?”

“Mica Hand. Orra Senn has refused inspection.”

Halvek closed Taren Ves’s record. “Let her work.”

Rusk’s hesitation showed in the reflection above the table before it reached his voice. “That contradicts the containment order.”

“Yes.”

A warning pulsed at the plot’s lower margin. The wounded hollow had left a wake through Sable’s orbital plane, and every automated system still tried to render that wake as a defective corridor. Cargo drones followed the false line until local pilots cut their guidance. Rescue crews worked with hand signals and laser ranges. The machinery kept attempting to restore an order that no longer existed.

Halvek opened the amplification model he had hidden on Day Twenty.

Its first page carried his private command seal. Charter analysts had shown how each forced hold stored lens stress across the twelve-anchor chain. The curve rose slowly through the early interventions, then steepened after Sable doubled its hold strength. At the time, he had called the projection incomplete. He had ordered distribution restricted until staged evacuations could begin. Sable had received enough of his plan to reinforce, not enough to understand what reinforcement would purchase.

A model could not kill anyone. Orders could.

He dragged the casualty list beside the projected curve. The two panes aligned by timestamp. Station Six had failed precisely inside the analysts’ predicted band. The orbital ring twisted seven minutes later. Elevator cables crossed paths that their tension systems could not recognize. Habitats moved out of stable planes while controllers saw delayed images and issued corrections to positions already gone.

“Do you want the command record sealed?” Rusk asked.

“No.”

“Three captains have requested direct access.”

“Give it to them.”

Rusk stepped closer. “All of it?”

Halvek looked up. Rusk had served under him during two famine escorts and the evacuation of Jellan Deep. He understood discipline as a tool for keeping frightened crews pointed toward the same work. What he did not understand, because Halvek had prevented the knowledge from reaching him, concerned the difference between a hard order and a concealed premise.

“All of it,” Halvek said. “Including my restriction on the model.”

The commander’s face tightened. “That will fracture the cutters.”

“They have already fractured.”

Outside the forward ports, Sable filled half the view. Its cloud bands looked undisturbed. Above them, the orbital ring had become a broken silver line. Sections still carried power. Others drifted under tug control, separated by black gaps where thousands of homes and work bays had once completed the circumference.

Rusk lowered his voice. “We can still restore command here.”

“Define restore.”

“Seize Threadbare. Detain Senn and the other union captains. Put local cage control under Continuity. We have six cutters and the strongest movable hold in the Reach.”

The proposal matched Halvek’s own standing contingency. It had once seemed severe but coherent. Destroy Kessa Rho’s tug if seizure failed. Reassert the holds at every cage still reachable. Force the civilian convoy toward shelter while fixed corridors remained. Arrest enough routekeepers to make refusal expensive.

He brought the contingency onto the table.

Six cutters appeared as blue marks. Continuity carried nine resonator control beams, each able to override a local station at military range. Threadbare had no armor. A concentrated cutter pass could split the old tug before Kessa brought her cold-shear tool near another cage. Loyal crews could occupy three anchor stations within a day.

Then the model extended beyond the tactical display.

With three more holds, predicted shear crossed the Tern failure by a factor of eleven. With six, uncertainty widened past the limits of every Charter instrument. Local control might preserve a corridor for hours. The adult return would reach the chain on Day Forty-two, and any surviving cages would release their stored geometry into inhabited space. Destroying Threadbare could preserve authority. It could not preserve the network.

Rusk watched the projection. “We do not know that Rho’s plan will work on the adult.”

“No.”

“We do know what happens when routes close. You taught us that.”

Halvek touched the famine-relief record without opening it. Eight hundred thirty thousand dead had followed a route closure forty years earlier. He still remembered cargo pallets waiting behind a silent anchor while field stations sent ration estimates down to hours. He had built his career around refusing that helplessness. Every promotion had widened the number of people whose survival he could convert into capacity, schedule, and protected throughput.

Yet the arithmetic now rejected his oldest answer. A route held through violence could not deliver relief after it tore apart the receiving orbit.

A call request flashed from Cutter Bound Meridian. Captain Eles Var had placed herself at the head of the six loyal ships. Halvek accepted.

Var’s face appeared above the table, helmet clipped open, a shallow cut sealed along her jaw. “Marshal, the neutral cutters have targeted our relay vanes. They say any hold transmission constitutes an unlawful attack.”

“They are correct about the effect.”

Her eyes narrowed. “Are they correct about the law?”

“I no longer control that determination.”

“You command the force.”

“For the next few minutes, perhaps.”

Rusk moved beside the display but said nothing. Var looked between them through the channel, reading what the bridge had not announced.

“Rho caused the first breach,” she said. “Her people stole Charter property, broke cages, and put the convoy on an uncharted wake. We lost one hundred forty-seven at Orison because Mott shielded fugitives with civilian mass. If you yield now, every death becomes permission.”

Halvek heard the attraction in that sequence. It arranged disaster into disobedience, disobedience into remedy. He had used similar lines in briefings because crews moved more cleanly when cause pointed toward a person.

“At Tern,” he said, “my hold overlapped Rho’s cut after she warned us the systems opposed each other. At Orison, I fired through a habitat exclusion cone. At Sable, my restricted model allowed reinforcement without the failure projection. Her violations do not remove mine.”

Var went still. “Is this channel recorded?”

“Yes.”

“Then suspend it and speak to me as the officer who pulled eighty-seven people from Orison wreckage.”

“I am speaking to that officer.”

Her mouth hardened. “Give me authority to take the three neutral cutters. We can disable them without fatalities. Then we recover Continuity’s command reach before the union occupies the chain.”

“No.”

“You are permitting mutiny.”

“I permitted something worse. I gave incomplete risk to people bound to obey.”

Var cut the channel.

Within seconds, the six cutter marks changed formation. Patient Fire, Civic Burden, and Measured Force remained around Continuity. Bound Meridian, Quiet Mandate, and Civic Measure pulled outward, drives hot, their vectors pointed away from Sable and the approaching routekeeper craft. Rusk issued no pursuit order. Halvek had not granted him one.

The bridge hatch opened. Charter security entered in gray pressure armor, led by Lieutenant Cera Venn. She carried no drawn weapon. Her squad spread only far enough to cover the command pit.

“Marshal Enro,” Venn said, “the neutral captains have transmitted a warrant claim under emergency rescue law. Commander Rusk has also released the restricted model to fleet channels. I need to know whether you intend to resist confinement.”

Rusk faced Halvek. The command transfer rested with him if Halvek stepped down, unless the cutter captains rejected his legitimacy as well. Every path still passed through somebody’s willingness to follow.

“Not confinement,” Halvek said. “A restricted bridge watch until civil authority can receive me. I retain technical access by request, no weapons authority, no detention authority, and no unilateral command channel.”

Venn glanced at Rusk. “Who holds command?”

“No one exclusively,” Halvek said.

“That is not an answer this bridge can execute.”

“Then we will make one.”

He opened the fleet command charter and removed his personal seal from the top tier. The system demanded a successor. Instead, he divided permissions. Rusk received ship safety and maneuver. The three neutral captains received rescue coordination. Local routekeepers received control over cage access. Convoy command received civilian-mass timing. Weapon release required two keys from different groups.

Red objections covered the table. Charter software considered divided authority a fault condition.

Halvek overrode the objections with the same emergency clause he had used to centralize control at Orison.

“Exclusive command relinquished,” the bridge announced.

The words left the room altered. Security personnel shifted their stance. Two navigation officers removed Charter command pins from their collars and set them beside their consoles. Rusk kept his pin but moved from behind Halvek’s chair to the ship-safety station.

A new call arrived from Mica Hand. Orra Senn appeared with Sable’s broken ring behind her. Grease marked one temple. “Enro, your cruiser just opened its permissions tree to routekeeper certificates. Is that a trap?”

“No.”

“Bad answer. Your traps usually come with documentation.”

Halvek sent her the division order and the full amplification model. “Read both before bringing a tug near us.”

Senn scanned the first lines. “Kessa needs a movable gradient source. The adult lens will cross the edge of the Reach in two days.”

“I know.”

“You knew several things.”

“Yes.”

Anger moved through her face without becoming performance. “My people at Sable followed your orders. Some kept the core powered while their families evacuated.”

“The casualty list names them.”

“A list does not know them.”

“No.”

Senn looked away, perhaps toward another display. “I’m not granting absolution because you found a clean verb for surrender.”

“I did not request it.”

“What are you requesting?”

“Access for your engineers to Continuity’s resonator deck. Full hardware survey. No hold may fire without routekeeper authorization.”

The bridge crew reacted more sharply to that than to his confinement. Continuity’s beam architecture carried military protections older than several current treaties. Opening it exposed command encryption, range limits, and blind arcs to workers the Charter had recently ordered arrested.

Senn gave him a long look. “I’ll send six people.”

“Send twelve. We have nine projectors, and two lost calibration during the Sable quake.”

“You’re taking orders well.”

“I have little practice.”

“Learn quickly.”

After the call ended, Lieutenant Venn approached with a restraint band. She did not raise it.

“Required?” Halvek asked.

“Not if Commander Rusk accepts responsibility for the watch.”

Rusk nodded once. “He remains on this bridge or in assigned quarters. Every transmission copied. Every technical instruction advisory.”

Venn clipped the band to Halvek’s belt rather than his wrist. Its indicator lit amber. Restricted movement, recorded location, no physical humiliation. He found the restraint more difficult to bear for its professionalism.

Across the fleet plot, the six cutters finished choosing. Bound Meridian, Quiet Mandate, and Civic Measure formed a separate group under Captain Var. They declined the divided command and announced withdrawal to Charter-held space. Quiet Mandate requested escort for crew who refused Var’s departure. Patient Fire, Civic Burden, and Measured Force stayed with Continuity but surrendered weapons authority under protest.

One of them, Cutter Patient Fire, transmitted a roster of divided crew. Twenty-two wanted reassignment to Var’s group. Seventeen demanded neutral rescue status. The captain proposed sealing the weapons bay and carrying both factions until a civilian berth could receive them. Halvek had once punished a captain for less visible uncertainty. Now he approved the practical care in it, then remembered approval no longer belonged to him. Rusk accepted the proposal and assigned two rescue observers to the cutter. No one called the arrangement unity. It merely kept armed shipmates from turning a disagreement into another casualty entry.

“Do we stop Var?” Rusk asked.

Halvek examined the vectors. Pursuit would pull rescue ships from Sable and turn a political fracture into another engagement. Var’s cutters carried enough fuel to reach a sheltered system. They also carried records. Nothing in Halvek’s new arrangement granted him the right to compel their consent merely because he disliked the direction of it.

“No pursuit,” he said, then corrected himself. “My advice: no pursuit.”

Rusk entered the decision under his own seal.

Routekeeper shuttles docked along Continuity’s port spine. Their crews arrived with tool cases, independent clocks, and obvious distrust. Charter engineers met them at the sealed resonator hatch. For one tight minute, neither group offered credentials.

Halvek left the command chair.

He crossed the bridge under Venn’s watch and placed his palm on the hardware release. The cruiser read his identity, then requested confirmation from a routekeeper certificate under the new permissions. Orra Senn’s engineer pressed a scarred diagnostic tag to the second plate.

Locks withdrew through the deck.

The resonator hatch opened on nine control trunks descending into the ship, each bright with status lamps. These projectors had enforced holds from Tern to Brine. They could also cast a shaped gradient without closing it around a juvenile, provided someone supplied geometry the military manuals never contemplated.

“Kill automatic acquisition,” Senn’s engineer said.

A Charter technician bristled. “That leaves beam pointing manual.”

“Yes.”

“Across forty thousand kilometres of moving lens?”

“If you want the machine to decide what counts as a target, go read Sable’s debris plot.”

Rusk looked to Halvek. Habit pulled every face toward him.

Halvek kept his hands at his sides. “Routekeeper authorization controls the resonators.”

The technician disabled automatic acquisition.

For the first time since Halvek had taken command of Continuity, the main bridge plot showed no protected corridor. Fourteen ship markers separated into raw local tracks. Three wakes crossed them without destination labels, while damaged orbits blinked in the same uncorrected frame.

A tightbeam request arrived from Threadbare. Kessa Rho’s tug remained hours away, crossing the wounded juvenile’s chaotic wake toward Sable. Halvek asked Rusk for permission to answer. Rusk granted a monitored channel.

Kessa appeared against the tug’s scratched cupola. Fatigue had narrowed her face. A sealed cut crossed one cheek. She did not greet him.

“Senn says your resonators are open,” she said.

“They are under routekeeper control.”

“And you?”

“Restricted bridge watch.”

“That phrase can cover a large room.”

“Lieutenant Venn can provide its dimensions.”

Kessa’s gaze shifted, taking in the security officer and Rusk at the ship-safety station. “Why call?”

Halvek brought up Continuity’s beam envelope. Nine projectors could move together, independent of a fixed cage, while the cruiser supplied ranging and thermal capacity. No other ship could produce a gradient of comparable strength. Used as a hold, it might injure the approaching adult and multiply the disaster. Used differently, it might shape the animal’s approach long enough to clear an orbital plane.

He transmitted the raw specifications without Charter locks.

“You asked for a way to unfasten the chain,” Halvek said. “I kept giving you stronger fastenings.”

Kessa studied the envelope. “This can move the gradient center.”

“Thirty-two thousand kilometres from the cruiser at full coherence. Farther at reduced strength. Two projectors carry damaged calibration.”

“Can you repair them?”

“My engineers can. Your routekeepers must certify the result.”

The adult’s predicted path crossed behind her image, a broad curve approaching the twelve released and wounded points of the wake map. Sable’s juvenile drifted out of alignment. Brine lay beyond it. A fixed hold would turn that misalignment into stored violence. A movable beam might offer a slope the animal could follow without closing around it.

Kessa lifted her eyes. “If I use this, nobody fires on my tug.”

“I lack authority to promise that alone.”

Rusk leaned into channel range. “Continuity will not fire weapons on Threadbare. The three cutters remaining with us have accepted divided release.”

“Var?” Kessa asked.

“Departed with three ships,” Rusk said.

She absorbed the loss without calling it victory. “Send Anu the projector heat limits. Send Sena your manual pointing lag. I want independent clocks on every trunk.”

The orders reached Halvek as repair work, which made them easier to carry and harder to evade. He forwarded each package. Routekeepers entered the resonator deck while Charter technicians opened housings no civilian had seen.

Kessa kept the channel open. “What do you want in exchange?”

There it stood, the logic he had taught everyone to expect. Capacity purchased obedience. Rescue purchased restored authority. A movable hold purchased some future claim on the routes.

“Nothing,” Halvek said. “Record the transfer as evidence.”

Her expression did not soften. Good. Trust rebuilt too quickly would only repeat the concealment under kinder language.

Outside, the first projector housing rotated away from Sable’s wounded orbit. Its targeting marks disappeared from the bridge. A routekeeper’s independent clock replaced them.

Halvek sent Kessa the final release key.

“Continuity carries the one movable hold strong enough to shape the adult’s approach,” he said. “Use it as something other than a cage.”

Chapter 22 — The Adult Under Glass

The stars began sliding before Threadbare’s instruments admitted anything had arrived.

Kessa stood strapped into the forward cupola with one palm against the manual console. Elistor’s familiar white point drew itself into a hook. Three dim stars beside it widened into paired arcs, then swept apart until the glass held an empty curve between them. No edge marked the intruding body. Its passage appeared only as a rearrangement of everything behind it.

Sena killed the tug’s automatic tracker when it tried to correct for six directions at once. “Independent clocks agree within four hundredths.”

“Cage references?” Kessa asked.

“Agree with each other and nobody else.”

“Some habits survive catastrophe.”

Anu occupied the second couch under a web of temporary leads. Filament samples from Nacre, Brine, and the dead route hung in three shielded tubes beside her. Each thread had tightened along the same axis. None pointed directly toward the moving lens. They followed the curved gradient that preceded it.

“Effective diameter crosses 40,000 kilometres,” Anu said. “Likely larger if the weak outer topology sits below our clocks’ resolution.”

On the plot, the adult veilback had no single shape. Parallax gave them one bent field. Tug drift gave another. Heat instruments found nothing. Gravitational timing mapped a broad back crossing the Reach, then lost it whenever local wakes folded over the signal. Kessa could not look at the result without remembering dark cloth lifting beneath Nacre’s stars.

The name had felt too generous when Sena coined it. Now it seemed too small.

“Pulse rising,” Anu said.

Three measured loads entered Threadbare through every loose object at once.

The first pushed Kessa’s boot soles into the deck restraints. The second made the cupola’s old scratches brighten as starlight shifted inside them. The third passed through the tug without moving it and set Tomis’s torque key trembling in its clipped socket.

Across the wake map, eleven juvenile traces answered at different strengths. The three safely released hollows followed the adult’s curve with narrow, clean signals. Others strained inside cages or moved through damaged fields. Sable’s wounded juvenile answered late and from the wrong side of the approaching trough.

Anu expanded that trace. “There. Again.”

A ragged pulse arrived after the adult’s third beat. Its outer topology spread across Sable’s broken orbital plane, then folded toward Brine. The juvenile had escaped the doubled hold, but the lensquake had left its gradient asymmetrical.

Sena brought Brine’s projected orbit onto the cupola. The habitat swarm lay beyond the system’s red dwarf, days away by ordinary thrust and alarmingly close in bent geometry. Six point two million people occupied those agricultural cylinders. Thousands more evacuees crowded temporary berths after Sable.

“If it follows the current line,” Sena said, “the juvenile crosses Brine’s orbital plane in eleven hours.”

Anu shook her head. “Do not make following sound deliberate. Its injured topology keeps falling across the available gradient.”

“Can the adult pull it clear?”

“Perhaps during a matched trough. Not while their pulses oppose.”

Kessa studied the load paths. An injured rigger with one functioning arm did not need a command shouted from farther away. She needed a surface placed where leverage remained possible. The scale changed. The work did not.

A tightbeam from Continuity arrived with projector status. Seven of nine resonators had passed routekeeper calibration. One damaged trunk could operate below half strength. The last remained open on the deck, its focusing rings misaligned by the Sable quake. Orra Senn’s crews had removed automatic acquisition from all nine.

Halvek appeared in an inset under Commander Rusk’s channel authority. The amber mark of restricted bridge watch remained visible at his collar. “Cruiser can hold present relative position for ninety minutes before radiator limits require a roll.”

Kessa disliked the verb. “You will not hold the animal.”

“The ship can maintain position,” Halvek said. “The beams will do what you authorize.”

Anu leaned toward the channel. “We need a gradient slope no steeper than six parts in ten million across the juvenile’s core. Any closed contour risks another injury contraction.”

“Projector minimum exceeds that at normal coherence,” Rusk said.

“Then make it less coherent,” Kessa replied.

A Charter engineer spoke from the resonator deck. “Diffusion will heat the outer rings unevenly.”

“Your heat table allows eight minutes.”

“Seven minutes forty at current damage.”

“Seven forty gives us two pulse troughs. Build me a slope that moves.”

The engineer began objecting in capacities, then stopped when Halvek asked for a manual field sketch. Kessa transmitted her geometry. Three projectors would spread a shallow gradient ahead of Sable’s juvenile. Two more would move that slope sideways during the adult trough. No beams would cross behind the animal. Nothing would close.

Sena read the sketch and tapped one line. “You’ve placed Threadbare inside the ranging gap.”

“Yes.”

“Excellent. The old tug deserves a simple retirement.”

“We need local clocks.”

“We could also need them from farther away.”

Kessa enlarged the blind region. The adult’s lens warped signals coming from either side. Continuity could shape a field but could not measure its exact effect near the juvenile. Someone had to place instruments in the gap and call corrections faster than the distorted images came back.

Anu unfastened her couch web. “I am taking the sample rack aft.”

“You are staying in the couch.”

“The filaments provide our best local trough detector. Your hull blocks two axes here.”

“Kess,” Sena said, “choose which argument you want to lose first.”

Kessa looked at Anu’s thin face and the bruise yellowing beneath one eye from Sable salvage. She wanted to send the ecologist onto Continuity, keep Sena out of the ranging gap, and perform the approach alone. That familiar urge disguised control as care. Nineteen crew had already taught her the difference.

“Rack goes to the forward rail,” she said. “Anu stays inside the pressure blister. Pell handles the exterior clocks. Nobody crosses the lensward side.”

Sena turned Threadbare toward the adult.

Ordinary distance stopped behaving cleanly within minutes. Their drive pushed aft while Sable’s ring appeared to move sideways. A rescue cutter crossed the cupola twice in two different positions, one current and one delayed by a folded light path. Sena ignored both images and flew from laser ranges to three routekeeper buoys.

The adult occupied more of the star field with every correction. Kessa’s body insisted that something enormous approached the tug, though the instruments showed their relative line remaining stable. Darkness rose without surface. Bent stars traced a curve forty thousand kilometres long, then slipped over it like sparks around a shoulder.

No eye looked back. No intention entered the pulse. The animal’s danger came from mass, motion, injury, and the human machinery embedded along its migration path.

A routekeeper buoy reached the adult’s outer gradient ahead of them. Its beacon split into four images, each reporting a different range. Sena tagged the earliest signal, rejected it after its clock ran backward, then chose the dimmest image because its timing still advanced. The buoy’s casing appeared intact. Its tethered calibration masses did not. One mass climbed a curve no physical line connected to the buoy; another circled an absence for six seconds and shot outward with enough speed to puncture Threadbare if their paths crossed.

“Clear the calibration fan,” Kessa said.

Sena rolled beneath the first mass. A grappling arm unlocked in the repair gallery, struck its travel stop, and stayed there under a load the tug’s frame sensors could not locate. Kessa released the arm remotely before it tore the mounting plate. The old mechanism drifted away, valuable and harmless compared with the hole it might have opened.

Pell called from the forward blister. “Outer clocks have a nine-hundredth split across two metres.”

“That is our local shear,” Anu said. “Not instrument drift.”

Kessa asked Pell to retract the rack by half a metre. The clock difference fell. She had him extend it again, slower. As the rack crossed the same invisible boundary, the split returned within one thousandth. They had found an edge of the adult’s outer topology through a change shorter than Kessa’s arm.

She marked it for Continuity. “Keep every beam center at least three local-shear widths outside this boundary.”

The Charter engineer answered that his plot showed the boundary seven hundred kilometres away.

“Your plot receives folded light,” Kessa said. “Mine is trying to pull Pell’s bolts out.”

Orra Senn broke into the engineering channel. “Use Rho’s local mark. We can reconcile maps after nobody dies.”

Continuity shifted its proposed line. The adjustment cost beam reach and forced the cruiser closer to the adult’s wake. Halvek did not argue for the cleaner military geometry. Kessa noticed because two days earlier he would have built a doctrine around refusing.

Another three-beat pulse entered the buoy. Its surviving calibration mass changed orbit without thrust, settling along the curve toward the juvenile. The movement gave Kessa a natural slope to copy. She laid Continuity’s proposed gradient beside it and reduced the artificial field until both curves differed by less than their clock uncertainty.

Only then did she authorize the closer approach.

“Juvenile trace,” Anu said.

Sable’s hollow appeared as a knotted absence below the adult’s leading gradient. Its filament core measured only tens of metres, too small to see, but its damaged topology bent a region thousands of kilometres wide. One side contracted normally. The other dragged through a wake scar left by the failed cage.

Three beats passed through the hull.

The adult’s signal reached them first. The juvenile answered late again. Between both rhythms, local gradient rose sharply enough to pull a tool bin from its brackets in the repair gallery.

From the forward blister, Pell reported, “Clock Three just gained two tenths.”

“Relative to what?” Kessa asked.

“Everything I trust.”

“Throw it out. Use One, Two, and the torque key pickup.”

“That key does not have a clock.”

“It has a load cavity. Call the pulse edge from vibration.”

Pell muttered something about family tools and scientific standards. A second later, Tomis’s old key appeared as a crude line on Kessa’s plot. It caught each load change without trying to classify it.

Continuity moved into projector range. The cruiser looked small against the lens, a hard white splinter carrying nine open housings along its spine. Three neutral cutters spread around it as independent ranging points. Routekeeper tugs occupied wider positions with beams dark.

Rusk requested authorization.

Kessa checked every line twice. No automatic lock. No rear closure. Diffusion below Anu’s biological limit. A moving field center timed to local clocks rather than Charter cage solutions.

“Open Projectors Two, Four, and Seven at twelve percent,” she said.

Light did not appear. The effect reached them as a slow change in the tug’s grapple drums. Slack cables shifted against their guides. The wounded juvenile’s trace flexed toward the artificial slope.

“Contraction beginning,” Anu said. “Right side only. Reduce Four.”

“Four to nine percent,” Kessa called.

The command crossed distorted space. Continuity’s confirmation arrived before the request appeared to have left Threadbare. Kessa ignored sequence on the visual channel and trusted the independent clocks.

Pell counted load by touch. “Slope moving. Half a degree spinward.”

Sena matched the tug’s drift to keep the blister in the ranging gap. Thrusters fired in clipped bursts. Each plume bent around the juvenile and painted a false curve across the cupola.

A warning showed rising projector heat.

“Adult pulse in twenty seconds,” Anu said. “Maybe eighteen. Outer sample is changing faster.”

Kessa watched three different trough predictions separate. Continuity used military timing. Anu used filament tension. Tomis’s key registered the physical load after it crossed their hull. None agreed closely enough for a blind sequence.

“Give me the damaged side,” Kessa told Pell.

He sent a magnified trace. The juvenile’s wake scar opened and closed like a bad repair under alternating strain. Its intact side responded to the adult. The wound delayed the other half, forcing the animal across its own gradient.

A stronger beam would move it sooner. Strength had shaped every Charter mistake.

“Shift the slope ahead by eight hundred kilometres,” she said. “Do not increase power.”

The resonator engineer answered. “At current coherence, the field will take forty seconds to establish.”

“Let it.”

“Brine intercept narrows if we wait.”

“Then narrow it.”

Halvek’s voice entered, stripped of command polish. “Execute Rho’s displacement at current power.”

Rusk confirmed under his own seal.

Projectors slewed. The artificial gradient moved across the juvenile’s path without wrapping behind it. For several seconds nothing changed. Brine’s danger line turned orange.

Then the hollow’s intact side followed.

Its damaged topology stretched. Kessa felt the change through the console as a drawn cable reaching load. She had one hand over the emergency beam cut. If the scar opened farther, she would drop every projector and accept the orbital intercept rather than repeat Tern.

“Wound gradient stable,” Anu said. “It can move this far.”

The adult pulse arrived.

First beat: the cupola stars folded into long white needles.

Second beat: every filament in Anu’s rack contracted toward the same invisible curve.

Third beat: the wounded juvenile tried to answer.

Its late signal crossed Continuity’s movable beam and steepened the slope. Projector heat jumped. Threadbare rolled without thruster input. Sena caught the rotation manually, but the forward blister moved toward the lensward side.

“Pell, get inside,” Kessa said.

“Outer hatch cycling.”

“Now.”

“The clock rack is hard-mounted.”

“Leave it.”

A sharp load struck the forward rail. One of the three clocks vanished from the plot. The others showed the juvenile’s topology folding toward the adult, too quickly on the intact side and not enough through the wound.

Anu gripped her harness. “Cut Projector Seven.”

Kessa sent the order.

Nothing happened.

“Seven not responding,” the resonator engineer said. “Control ring jammed at twelve percent.”

Tern returned through Kessa’s hands: hold opposing cut, trapped geometry, men and women inside a service spindle as light bent wrong. She pushed the memory back into procedure.

“Can you shutter locally?”

“Manual housing access takes six minutes.”

They had less than one trough.

Sena pointed to Continuity’s ship vector. “Move the projector instead.”

The cruiser could not turn quickly, but it could roll. A rotation would sweep the jammed beam away from the juvenile before thermal cutoff.

“Rusk, roll Continuity nine degrees anti-spinward,” Kessa said. “Keep Two and Four stabilized.”

Halvek answered first. “That crosses our radiator shadow.”

“Your radiators survive ninety seconds. The juvenile may not survive the beam.”

Rusk called the maneuver. The cruiser’s attitude jets fired.

Because of the lens, Continuity seemed to bend before it moved. Its long hull curved across the stars, straightened, then began the actual roll. Projector Seven’s gradient slid off the hollow’s wounded side. Two and Four fought to preserve the shallow slope.

The bridge channel filled with thermal warnings. One radiator bank shut down. The open resonator deck climbed above safe work temperature, forcing routekeepers into shielded alcoves.

“Pell?” Kessa called.

“Inside. Clock rack has entered a better profession.”

A loose rail crossed the cupola, turning slowly toward the adult. No one tried to retrieve it.

The juvenile’s trace settled along the shifted gradient. Its damaged side remained distorted, but both halves now moved in the same direction. Brine’s intercept line stopped narrowing.

Anu held up one hand. “Do not celebrate. Pulse match has not happened.”

Kessa had no spare attention for celebration. Projector Four approached its diffusion limit. The cruiser’s roll had shortened their useful slope. Adult and juvenile entered another trough, this one obscured by overlapping wakes.

“Filaments disagree,” Anu said.

“Which sample do you trust?”

“None alone. Nacre closes first. Brine carries less scar. Dead-route tissue follows the adult but cannot show contraction.”

“Build the average.”

“That would invent a body that does not exist.”

Kessa understood the objection. Averages had helped the Charter flatten nine differing stations into one green lie. “Then give each sample a vote and tell me when all three descend.”

Anu recast the display. Three narrow traces moved independently. Nacre dropped. Brine followed. The dead-route filament held tension four seconds longer, then released.

“Now,” Anu said.

Kessa moved the shallow slope toward the adult’s wake.

Projectors Two and Four slewed in matched increments. The juvenile crossed the gradient without closure. Its intact topology lengthened first. The wounded side followed through a delayed curve, shedding a faint filament trace into the space behind it.

The adult answered with three loads.

For one breath, the juvenile remained late.

Then its first beat entered Threadbare inside the adult’s first trough. The second aligned more closely. On the third, both load curves occupied the same rhythm.

Every loose cable in the tug lifted and settled together.

Anu stared at the plot. “Matched pulse. Still not language.”

“No one asked it a question,” Kessa said.

Sena checked Brine’s plane. “Intercept clearing. Two thousand kilometres. Four. Eight.”

The juvenile moved along the adult’s gradient, not captured by it and not dragged through a closed field. As both lens bodies aligned, the bent stars between them opened into a narrow region of calmer geometry.

Kessa marked its edges. The safe corridor had no fixed throat. It traveled with the animals and would vanish when their pulses separated. Yet for several minutes, local clocks agreed. A vessel entering at the right angle could cross the wake without a cage.

Continuity cut its overheated projectors. The cruiser completed its roll with one radiator bank dark. Routekeeper tugs moved in to inspect the jammed housing. Nobody restored automatic acquisition.

Halvek requested channel access. Rusk granted it.

“Brine reports the orbital danger receding,” Halvek said.

“Keep them moving people inward,” Kessa replied. “This alignment may not hold.”

“What do you call the corridor?”

She watched the calm region travel across the plot. Names had a way of turning temporary work into property. Routes invited schedules, claims, and the old confidence that somebody could make motion stand still.

“A trough,” Anu said. “A temporary matched trough.”

Kessa nodded. “Log exactly that.”

The adult moved deeper into the Reach. Beside it, Sable’s wounded juvenile held the same three-beat rhythm. Between their bending fields, the temporary corridor opened another six hundred kilometres, just wide enough for the first convoy sighting buoys to enter.

Chapter 23 — Seventy Million Voices

Irian opened the vote while three habitat captains were still arguing about whether the crossing existed.

The bridge of Accord of Dust had become a logistics pit in the literal sense. Two deck plates remained open where technicians had rerouted guidance trunks after Orison. Temporary fiber ran across the overhead in labeled loops. Every working surface carried a ship slate, a ration estimate, or a range plot of the moving trough beside the adult veilback.

No chart certified that trough. It had opened when the wounded Sable juvenile matched the adult’s pulse, narrowed within minutes, and returned along a slightly different line on the next cycle. Kessa called it temporary geometry. Charter remnants called it an unlicensed corridor. Irian’s navigators called it a crossing because four hundred ten thousand people needed a noun before they could decide whether to enter.

Marl Quade stood at the central table with the Free Convoy Articles displayed between them. “The Articles require separate captain and passenger-delegate consent for uncharted mass movement.”

“They require informed consent,” Irian said. “We do not have complete information.”

“We have all the information anyone has.”

“That sentence should frighten you.”

“It does. Open the vote.”

Irian looked across the carrier’s plot. Accord and its attached habitats occupied a broad, ugly cluster along the adult’s wake. Tugs had spent eight days turning 70.4 million tonnes away from fixed routes. Freight barges now sat at angles chosen by fuel margin rather than design. Water bladders rode between habitation drums. Seed vaults had been distributed across nineteen ships. Kindly Argument kept its hospital cylinder inside a guard of three slow tugs.

Each mark represented mass. Each mass contained decisions made by people who would live inside the consequences.

She opened a convoy-wide channel. “This is Commodore Irian Mott. Under the Free Convoy Articles, we will vote ship by ship on the proposed moving-shear crossing. Captains hold one operational vote per registered vessel. Passenger delegates hold votes by inhabited module. Neither side can compel the other. Refusal preserves berth, stores, and local shelter rights.”

A status box demanded a destination. No destination lay on any certified route. Irian entered: BRINE-SIDE SHELTER ARC VIA MOVING TROUGH, CONDITIONAL.

The system rejected conditional as an invalid place.

She overrode the field.

Marl leaned closer to the microphone. “Published risk packet follows. Read it before voting. If your captain says there is no time, report that on the delegate channel. If a delegate prevents crew from stating a safety objection, report that too. Silence does not count as consent.”

The packet left Accord in thirty-one compressed bursts.

It contained every loss estimate Irian’s staff could defend. Crossing modules might encounter clock splits, unexpected shear, tug separation, and total guidance failure. The predicted severe-damage band ran from six to fourteen modules depending on trough width. Evacuation before entry could reduce deaths but not prevent material loss. No model promised every module would survive. No simulation covered an adult lens body interacting with 70.4 million tonnes of distributed infrastructure.

Irian had removed three reassuring phrases before release. Manageable risk hid who would manage it. Acceptable loss assigned consent to the speaker. Successful transit assumed an end none of them had reached.

Captain Veyr of Habitat Kestrel called first. “Commodore, you published hull loss but not projected deaths.”

“Because berth transfer can move people out of the weakest modules before crossing. If every transfer completes, the median model gives between zero and three hundred deaths. The outer band reaches two thousand one hundred. If transfers fail, the model no longer deserves a number.”

“You expect people to vote on that?”

“I expect them to vote on the danger we have, not one I can make polite.”

Veyr’s delegate entered the channel. “And if we refuse?”

“Local shelters at Sable’s inner moon, Brine’s far agricultural arc, and three powered freight shells. Twelve days of guaranteed convoy stores, then local allocation. Tugs will place each shelter group before the main crossing.”

“Will you remain?”

“No.”

The honesty cost her more than a command would have. A captain could order departure and call the abandoned unreasonable. Consent required Irian to tell people that she would leave after provisioning them.

Votes began arriving.

Each ship transmitted two marks, one from command and one from passengers. Green indicated consent. Amber requested amendment or delay. Red refused. Gray showed no quorum. The plot filled slowly, never as one verdict. Mica Shelter Three voted green from its captain and red from its delegate. Freight tug Low Arithmetic produced the reverse. A water train with no resident passengers submitted only a captain’s mark and listed the six riders by name.

An amber request came from Spin Habitat Far Lantern, population nine thousand eighty-two. Its delegate, Osric Vale, asked Irian to attend a public question period. She moved the channel onto the main display. Rows of evacuees filled an old recreation drum under half gravity, their feet hooked beneath floor straps while the habitat remained despun for truss inspection.

Vale had grease on both sleeves and a child asleep against his shoulder. “Our captain says Far Lantern crosses in the third mass cluster. Your packet gives risk by module class, not by our actual damage.”

Irian called up the inspection. “Your forward truss lost two diagonal members at Orison. Temporary braces carry current spin load. Crossing load depends on local shear direction.”

A woman near the camera raised her hand. “That means what?”

“It means the braces may carry a pull along the truss. A sideways gradient could buckle them.”

“Can you put us where the pull goes along?”

“Not with certainty. The trough changes between pulses.”

Another resident called from the rear. “Then detach the damaged end.”

Captain Sol Varo entered from outside frame. “That end contains air processing for four districts.”

“Move the processors.”

“Forty hours of work. Our window arrives in nine.”

Voices rose. Irian let the delay stretch until Vale restored order by asking residents to hold questions on their slates. No command phrasing could shorten a structural fact.

Vale read the next question. “If we vote no, do you separate us from the carrier before our truss inspection finishes?”

“No. A tug keeps you stabilized until the local shelter vector begins. The shelter course uses lower thrust than the crossing approach.”

“Which tug?”

Irian checked the board. No tug had yet received that assignment. Naming one now would steal it from another refusal whose needs remained unknown.

“I cannot give you a hull name yet,” she said. “The allocation follows the final refusal count.”

“So your guarantee depends on a vote you do not have.”

“Yes.”

The child on Vale’s shoulder woke and stared into the camera. “Do we get to vote?”

A nervous laugh moved through the drum.

Vale shifted the child higher. “Berth residents sixteen and older vote directly. Younger residents join household consultations. That part comes from our local compact.”

The child frowned. “I’m eleven.”

“Then make your household work harder,” Marl said from beside Irian.

This time the laughter carried enough breath to release some pressure without making the danger small.

A rigger in the front row asked whether Kessa’s moving trough had been tested with habitat mass. Irian answered no. A nurse asked whether Kindly Argument would remain close. Irian said hospital position still depended on its own captain-delegate agreement. Captain Varo described the braces without claiming they would hold. The public period lasted twenty-eight minutes.

Far Lantern submitted captain green and delegate amber. Vale attached two conditions: an exterior tug inspection before approach and live truss strain on every local sighting slate. Irian accepted both conditions into the crossing plan. Other damaged habitats copied the strain requirement, turning one ship’s doubt into a convoy measure.

The first aggregate showed sixty-one percent consent among captains and forty-eight among delegates. Too low under the Articles. More troubling, six habitat drums reported unanimous votes cast within ninety seconds of receiving a twenty-page packet.

Marl saw them too. “Those are managed returns.”

“Or frightened people voting quickly.”

“Both can happen.”

Irian called the six delegates privately. Four admitted their local captains had opened a public channel and asked for a show of hands before distributing individual risk slates. One had counted only household heads. The sixth had used an old evacuation roster that excluded children over sixteen despite their current berth status.

She invalidated all six returns.

A captain from Drum Elian appeared on her command channel, furious. “You asked for speed and now reject completed votes.”

“I asked for consent.”

“My people want to leave.”

“Then let them say so under the Articles.”

“We have nine hours before the next wide trough.”

“You have forty minutes to conduct a ballot that includes every eligible berth.”

“Who gave you authority to invalidate my ship?”

“The same Articles that limit mine.”

Elian cut the channel. His vote reverted to gray.

Irian’s operations chief, Leto Har, slid a fuel report across the table. “Every hour costs shelter placement margin. If the vote runs beyond two cycles, we cannot provision all refusals and still make Kessa’s entry window.”

“Then publish that.”

“It will sound like coercion.”

“It becomes coercion if we hide it.”

Har added the deadline to the public packet. The new line moved several amber modules toward red. Others shifted green, not from confidence but from understanding that delay carried its own cost.

Across the bridge, the mass board kept updating. Food substrate: divided among twenty-seven carriers after Brine. Potable water: sufficient through the crossing and forty-three days beyond at current ration. Machine seed: nineteen caches, three in modules now rated too weak for entry. Fuel: enough for one large correction per tug cluster, not two. Hospital capacity: overloaded by Sable casualties.

Seventy million tonnes did not speak. It resisted motion, consumed fuel, stored food, spun air around families, and carried tools no isolated habitat could replace. The title on Irian’s original convoy manifest had called it essential mass. She had once treated that phrase as permission to keep it together. Now every component had a constituency, and preservation might require division.

Marl touched the machine-seed line. “The three weak modules can move their caches before the vote closes.”

“Transfer time?”

“Two hours if captains release the cargo arms.”

“Crossing window begins in nine.”

“Then we have time.”

“We have time if nothing jams.”

Marl gave her a flat look. “That describes every useful action since Nacre.”

Irian ordered the transfers offered, not imposed. Two modules agreed. The third, agricultural carrier Sown Argument, refused because moving its seed vault would require stopping spin in a habitation section with two hundred injured passengers.

Its delegate, a woman named Pera Noll, came onto the public channel. “If we stay, does the seed stay with us?”

“Yes,” Irian said.

“Even if the convoy needs it beyond the crossing?”

“You hold lawful possession.”

Captain Har objected off microphone. Irian muted him.

Noll consulted voices outside camera range. “We refuse passage. We will take Brine far-arc shelter and keep the vault operational for local planting.”

“Recorded.”

The red mark from Sown Argument carried twelve hundred forty people and enough seed stock to restart six staple lines. It weakened the convoy beyond the crossing. It strengthened those who remained. Irian forced herself not to rename the outcome loss.

More refusals gathered around it.

Some came from Sable evacuees who had survived one lensquake and would not enter another moving field. Some came from maintenance crews who trusted Kessa’s method but not their patched hulls. A Brine delegation chose local cylinders over another uprooting. Two freight crews split, with half transferring to shelters while the rest volunteered to fly empty cargo shells through the trough.

The refusal count passed eight thousand.

Irian assigned a separate board to shelter provisioning. People became berth blocks, water allotments, waste capacity, medicine classes, and tug minutes. She had spent years translating lives into logistics without believing the translation erased them. Today every conversion faced witnesses.

A delegate from Kindly Argument requested priority passage for the hospital cylinder. Its captain refused.

“We cannot stabilize surgery during the shear,” Captain Nemi said. “If you put us inside the first cluster, every clock split reaches life support before we know whether the guidance works.”

The hospital delegate answered, “If you put us last, injured people remain in the wake while tugs spend fuel ahead.”

Irian let them argue. Her instinct supplied a clean order: hospital centerline, two tugs, early crossing. Yet the Articles did not exist to decorate her preferred solution.

Kessa joined from Threadbare when asked for technical evidence. She looked more comfortable discussing damaged machinery than hundreds of thousands of listeners.

“The first cluster sees the least mapped geometry,” Kessa said. “The middle gets better sightings but more wake disturbance from mass ahead. Last cluster risks a narrowing trough. There is no safest position.”

Nemi asked, “Where would you put the hospital?”

“Where its captain can keep life support and its delegates accept the timing.”

“That is not engineering.”

“It is the boundary of engineering.”

After twelve minutes, Kindly Argument chose the front third, behind two empty sighting barges and ahead of the heaviest habitat cluster. Both captain and delegate entered green.

Kessa remained on Irian’s private channel. “I need independent sightings from the convoy. At least one node per tug cluster, clocks isolated from Accord.”

“My bridge can fuse them.”

“That creates one failure point.”

“My bridge already coordinates thrust.”

“Through fixed-route assumptions. The moving trough will distort your incoming picture. If every cluster waits for your correction, half will act on old geometry.”

Irian disliked the proposal because it made tactical sense. “Who arbitrates conflicting sightings?”

“Nearest verified node. Others publish raw clock and range. No hidden smoothing.”

“You are asking me to distribute helm authority before the vote authorizes movement.”

“I am asking whether the crossing you offered can function.”

On the bridge wall, Accord’s navigation crown showed every attached module as a subordinate mass. That architecture had helped Irian turn the convoy after the routes failed. It had also pulled all meaningful correction through one place: her place.

“Draft the network,” she said. “Do not activate until consent clears.”

Kessa sent a simple pattern. Each tug cluster would choose a sighting ship. Manual navigation slates from the Tern breach would receive local clock, laser range, and neighboring thrust. Accord would publish mass timing but could not overwrite a cluster’s immediate shear correction. Two adjacent nodes had to confirm any large vector change.

Marl read it over Irian’s shoulder. “Delegates should see this before final returns.”

“It concerns navigation.”

“It concerns who can decide to move their ship after the crossing starts.”

Irian added the distributed sighting architecture to the ballot packet.

The second returns came slower. Drum Elian completed individual voting and shifted from its earlier unanimous green to seventy-three percent consent. Its captain accepted the result without apology. Two gray ships reached quorum. One captain withdrew a green mark after discovering a cracked truss pin in the latest inspection.

At six hours before the predicted window, delegate consent crossed the required threshold. Captain consent followed nine minutes later.

No cheer rose on Accord’s bridge. The refusal board had reached eleven thousand three hundred. Transfer requests continued. Irian kept the ballot open for the final verified modules while tugs began moving shelter supplies.

Pera Noll sent a new request from Sown Argument. “We have eight hundred open berths after reorganizing spin. Offer them to other refusals.”

“Your life support margin?”

“Thirty-eight days with convoy stores. Longer if Brine accepts our seed contribution.”

Irian connected her with Marl’s shelter team. The act changed refusal from scattered red marks into a local convoy of its own.

The count settled at twelve thousand people choosing local shelters.

Irian published the number beside the crossing approval. She also published what they would receive: food, water, medicine, machine tools, three tug escorts, and authority over the far-arc stores left with them. No captain could strip those allocations to improve crossing margin without a new vote.

Leto Har pointed to the fuel board. “Leaving three tugs means the heaviest cluster loses a correction reserve.”

“Yes.”

“Our published loss band assumes that reserve.”

“Recompute.”

The severe-damage band widened. Irian released the update before anyone could claim the vote had closed on better numbers. Four modules requested reconsideration. Three remained green. One moved red and joined the shelter group with two hundred eighteen people.

Marl watched the final tally lock. “Approved.”

“Conditional on the trough opening within Kessa’s biological limits.”

“Still approved.”

Irian opened the convoy-wide channel again. “The crossing has consent under the Articles. Twelve thousand people have refused passage and will receive local shelter provision. Published loss estimates remain in force. We do not promise every module will survive. We do promise no refusal will be punished by withdrawal of earned stores.”

She waited for the transmission to clear every habitat. Signal delay across the stretched convoy turned one statement into dozens of local moments.

Next came the authority change.

Irian removed Accord’s automatic helm priority from the guidance crown. The interface warned that convoy integrity could no longer be guaranteed. She accepted. Sighting nodes lit across tug clusters, hospital escorts, freight trains, and habitat pairs. Each carried one of the manual slates distributed after Tern. Raw clocks appeared without central correction.

Captain Har stood beside her. “We can take priority back if the network diverges.”

“No. We can request confirmation like any other node.”

“You are still commodore.”

“For mass timing and common stores. Not for local sighting.”

Irian entered Accord as Node Seventeen rather than command root. The bridge plot redrew itself. Lines that once converged on her carrier now linked ship to neighboring ship across the convoy’s whole length.

The arrangement looked untidy, slower to read, and harder to command. It also survived when she blanked Accord’s own sighting feed for a test. Nearby nodes carried the geometry around the missing place without waiting for permission.

Outside, tugs peeled twelve thousand people and their provisioned modules toward the Brine far arc. Ahead, the adult’s bent field gathered the stars toward another temporary trough.

Irian took her hands off the centralized helm. Across seventy million tonnes of moving infrastructure, thirty-seven independent sighting nodes took the load.

Chapter 24 — The Shepherd Net

Kessa built the formation with cable on Threadbare’s repair-gallery floor.

Nine short lengths represented Continuity’s resonator beams. She tied each to a brass washer and arranged them around a grease mark for the adult veilback. Three black fasteners stood for the neutral rescue cutters. Routekeeper tugs received mismatched nuts from Sena’s spares drawer. The convoy occupied half the deck as a coil of old power line too thick to bend sharply.

On the wall display, proper symbols kept sliding as the adult lens changed every reference. Cable stayed where Kessa put it.

“Your cruiser cannot sit there,” Halvek said over the open planning channel.

His image came from Continuity’s bridge under restricted watch. Commander Rusk occupied the command station behind him. Orra Senn and two Charter engineers stood at the resonator deck feed. Nobody on that ship spoke without a channel recorder visible.

Kessa shifted the washer by two finger widths. “Here?”

“Then the adult’s leading gradient masks Cutters Two and Three.”

“They are ranging points, not a firing line.”

“A range point nobody can see cannot report position.”

Sena crouched beside the floor model. “It can report local position to its neighbors.”

Halvek studied that. His habits preferred every geometry readable from one command center. Irian had just surrendered centralized sighting for the convoy. The same correction had to reach the military ships.

“Cutters pair with routekeeper tugs,” Kessa said. “Local clocks and direct laser. Continuity receives their reports but does not smooth them.”

Rusk answered. “Accepted.”

Halvek did not say under protest. He pointed to the adult’s projected curve. “You still lack leverage on the far side. Nine beams can shape one moving gradient. They cannot shape two while the juveniles release.”

Kessa laid three more cable ends beyond the grease mark. These represented cage projectors at Brine, Nacre, and the last inhabited anchor still holding a juvenile. Eleven of twelve routekeeper crews had committed to the release sequence. Sable’s cage had already failed. Three hollows moved free. The rest would need outer stations opened, then the middle three. The core triad could release only in the pulse trough.

“Local projectors do not hold,” Anu said from the workbench. “They create open slopes toward the adult’s wake. If any contour closes around tissue, we cut every beam.”

A Charter engineer asked, “How do we determine closure when the field folds our sensors?”

Anu held up the three filament tubes. “Contraction direction, independent clocks, and juvenile pulse delay.”

“That gives inference, not certainty.”

“Biology often does.”

Kessa moved the Nacre cable. “The formation does not need certainty. It needs failure boundaries everyone can see.”

She drew a circle around the adult with a graphite marker, then cut the circle open in three places. No ship could project across those gaps. If guidance turned into confinement, the gradient would leak away rather than tighten. The openings also created escape paths for tugs caught on the wrong side of a wake.

Halvek enlarged the floor image on his display. “Three gaps permit the adult to drift back toward Brine’s plane.”

Irian joined from Accord of Dust. Her bridge showed thirty-seven distributed sighting nodes across the convoy. “Then my mass moves the other way.”

“You cannot turn seventy million tonnes on command,” Halvek said.

“I can schedule existing thrust so our center of mass does not deepen the Brine-side gradient.”

“That separates your heavy modules from their tug screens.”

“It separates them by twenty kilometres, not by a route.”

Kessa put a second coil of power line beside the first. “Irian staggers mass. Continuity shapes the outer slope. Local cage beams open the juvenile wakes. Cutters and routekeeper tugs keep sighting between all three.”

Sena picked up one of the black fasteners. “And Threadbare?”

Kessa placed it where the cable crossings grew densest.

“Of course,” Sena said. “The least armored ship goes where every bad solution meets.”

“We carry the cold-shear tool.”

“We also carry a pilot with objections.”

“Log them.”

“I have. The file affects our trim.”

Dry laughter moved through the planning channel, brief enough not to hide the strain.

Anu left the workbench and knelt by the model. She removed Kessa’s washer for Continuity and placed it farther from the adult. “Your gradient reaches through the juvenile chain here. The adult may follow. A wounded juvenile cannot tolerate the same slope.”

“We used six parts in ten million at Sable.”

“For one injured animal across one trough. The others have different cage scars.”

“How much?”

“Start at four. Rise only if their pulse delays shorten.”

Halvek objected. “At four parts, Continuity cannot shift the adult before its current track crosses the empty edge of Brine’s freight plane.”

“Empty today,” Anu said. “Do not injure it to protect an orbit we already cleared.”

“That plane intersects the convoy approach six hours later.”

Irian answered before Kessa could. “Then the approach moves. My people voted for published losses, not for a fixed schedule.”

The cable model widened until people had to step around it. Kessa marked thermal limits beside each washer. Continuity could sustain a diffuse field for seven minutes at current radiator damage. Neutral cutters could cast narrow ranging beams but lacked strength to shape the adult. Routekeeper tugs carried salvaged resonator heads from opened cages. Their projectors varied in age, output, and legal maintenance history. Two had been designed to inspect freight collars rather than hold space.

That inconsistency helped. A single hidden command could not seize the whole formation.

Kessa divided control into four permissions. Routekeepers owned juvenile release. Anu owned biological stop limits. Irian owned convoy mass timing. Rusk owned ship safety and collision avoidance. Kessa would coordinate the moving gradient but could not override any stop.

Halvek watched her write the permissions onto the deck. “You have created four ways to abort.”

“Yes.”

“And no single way to continue.”

“Also yes.”

He looked almost offended by the elegance of it.

They moved to a live test using an empty freight shell released near the adult’s outer wake. The shell carried six clocks, two calibration masses, and a transmitter nobody trusted. Routekeeper Tug Mended Question projected the first open slope. Neutral Cutter Civic Grace ranged from the opposite side. Continuity remained dark.

Sena flew Threadbare along the test boundary. Kessa felt the tug’s structure through her boots while the shell crossed the artificial gradient. The forward grapple drum gained load. A panel in the centrifugal passage began to chatter at eleven hertz.

“Local slope five point one,” Pell reported.

Anu shook her head. “Commanded four.”

“Cage head has an old focusing bias,” Orra Senn said from Mended Question. “We calibrated it against Charter references.”

“Those references assumed a fixed hollow,” Kessa replied.

Senn dropped the beam. The shell drifted clear.

Kessa opened the projector’s raw output on her slate. The bias came from a correction table installed twenty-three years earlier. It increased power whenever target geometry moved away from center. Useful for preserving a corridor. Dangerous for offering a slope that needed to remain open.

“Strip motion correction from every released cage beam,” she said.

A routekeeper captain at Nacre answered, “That leaves pointing lag across the whole array.”

“Then humans lead the target.”

“By how much?”

“Sena?”

The pilot inspected the adult’s current curvature. “Depends which image you want to crash into.”

Kessa waited.

“Local rates,” Sena said. “Each tug leads from two pulse samples. Nobody imports lead time from another node.”

The routekeepers rewrote their tables. Seventeen projectors across the chain went dark for manual conversion. During those minutes, the adult advanced without human guidance and harmed nothing. Kessa kept noticing that fact.

The second test began at four parts in ten million.

Mended Question cast an open slope ahead of the freight shell. This time the local clocks measured four point two. Civic Grace tracked the shell’s far side. Two routekeeper tugs passed raw ranges to each other without routing through Threadbare.

“Add Continuity,” Kessa said.

Rusk authorized Projectors Two and Four at minimum diffusion. The cruiser’s field joined the tug’s slope from forty thousand kilometres away. Where both gradients overlapped, their combined curve steepened beyond Anu’s limit.

“Cut Four,” Anu said.

Rusk cut it before Kessa repeated the order.

Projector Two remained. Its field pushed the calibration masses along the intended path while leaving an open region behind them. The freight shell turned six degrees and settled into the adult’s outer wake.

Halvek highlighted the overlap. “Military formation would offset projectors by range and fire time. Your nodes are treating simultaneous arrival as the goal.”

“Can you produce an open slope with staggered arrival?” Kessa asked.

“Yes.”

“Without central fire control?”

He paused. “With a shared timing table and local release authority.”

“Build it.”
