Halvek moved to a side console under Rusk’s observation. His geometry divided Continuity’s nine beams into three traveling sets. Each set opened ahead of the adult and decayed behind it. No beam crossed another at full strength. Routekeeper projectors filled the gaps at lower power. Neutral cutters marked the edges and held no shaping authority.

The pattern came from convoy defense: guide an incoming mass away from vulnerable ships without concentrating fire into a debris cloud. Halvek stripped every weapon assumption and left only timing.

Kessa disliked how useful it looked.

Anu examined the decay. “Your second set closes for point eight seconds.”

“Within instrument uncertainty.”

“Not within tissue.”

Halvek widened the release interval. The closed contour disappeared. Brine-side leverage weakened.

Irian adjusted convoy thrust by three minutes. Her staggered mass moved the natural gradient enough to recover half the lost effect.

They ran the model again.

Adult path shifted outward. Juvenile wake alignment improved at seven of eight remaining cages. The wounded Sable juvenile remained matched. One Nacre hollow showed a predicted contraction above Anu’s limit during core release.

Kessa moved two tugs from Brine to Nacre. That left a sighting gap beside the convoy.

Irian assigned an empty cargo shell as a stationary node. “It has clocks and laser.”

“It has no pilot,” Sena said.

“It does not need to decide. Neighboring crews do.”

Kessa added the shell. The formation held in simulation for one full pulse cycle.

Before accepting it, Kessa forced a communication fold through the model. Every long-range channel received a different delay drawn from the adult’s measured lensing. Continuity saw Nacre’s release fourteen seconds late. Brine received Irian’s mass table before Accord transmitted it according to the bridge display. One neutral cutter vanished behind a false image of its own drive plume.

Central coordination collapsed immediately. The local pairs did not. Mended Question kept its beam dark when its neighboring tug failed to confirm the trough. Nacre delayed middle-station release rather than trusting Continuity’s stale permission. Two convoy clusters continued on their published thrust because neither had received a verified change.

Halvek marked the lost efficiency. “The adult shifts only half the required distance under this response.”

“It also receives no accidental hold,” Anu said.

Kessa moved a routekeeper tug into the missing cutter’s place. “We do not solve delay by guessing through it. Each node continues its last safe condition. Dark for projectors. Published thrust for convoy mass. Local drift for sighting ships.”

Irian added, “Any cluster that loses both neighbors exits through its nearest open gap. It does not chase the crossing line.”

Sena traced the escape vectors on the cable floor. One passed close to Threadbare. She shifted their nut aside. “Nearest open gap now has room for two frightened ships and one chief who thinks in straight lines.”

Kessa accepted the correction. The communication-fold run completed with a weaker path and every contour open. No model awarded points for elegance.

“Run failures,” she said.

Rusk disabled one neutral cutter. Sightings rerouted through two tugs. Orra Senn killed a cage beam during outer release. The gradient weakened but remained open. Irian removed Accord’s timing feed. Distributed nodes preserved local thrust order for ninety seconds, enough to carry the convoy until a new mass table propagated.

Anu failed every filament sample at once.

The proper response required all shaping beams to cut. Continuity did. Six routekeeper heads did. One old projector at Brine continued because its local operator had configured biological stop as advisory.

Kessa’s voice sharpened. “Who owns Brine Head Five?”

A rigger named Dalen Vos answered from the damp station. “I do.”

“You do not own the stop.”

“Local rules require supervisor confirmation for a full power cut.”

“The union refused those rules on Day Twenty-six.”

“Refused new holds. This beam guides.”

“Guidance without an enforceable stop becomes a hold the moment the animal crosses the wrong line.”

Vos went quiet. Condensation drifted past his camera. Behind him, old cage controls showed the Charter seal under layers of scratched annotations.

“Then who carries liability if we cut at the wrong moment?” he asked.

“I do,” Kessa said.

Marl’s voice entered from Accord. “No. That repeats the same problem with a more popular chief.”

Kessa closed her mouth.

Anu tapped the permissions slate. “Biological stop comes from measured contraction. Any two local nodes may call it. No supervisor may delay. Liability follows the published rule, not one person.”

Vos read the amendment. “Give me a physical cutoff.”

Orra Senn designed one from a grapple squib. Every released cage beam would carry a hard line to two neighboring tugs. Either could open the circuit. The system added mechanical veto to the four-part authority.

During the next failure run, Brine Head Five cut within three tenths of a second.

Vos grinned through the damp. “Now it listens.”

“Machines do that better when the wiring agrees,” Kessa said.

A young rigger beside him leaned into frame. “Looks like a shepherd’s net from here.”

The channel went briefly still.

Kessa looked down at the cable spread across Threadbare’s floor. The phrase carried an old human confidence: animals directed for human purpose, boundaries thrown around moving bodies, care and ownership braided too tightly to separate. She did not want a name that made domination sound gentle.

“It does not catch anything,” she said.

The young rigger shrugged. “A good net leaves the way open behind the flock.”

Anu’s expression sharpened at flock, but Sena spoke first. “Crews need a channel name shorter than ‘distributed open-gradient formation with biological veto.’”

“Call it Formation Forty.”

“Already used for a waste convoy.”

Halvek said, “Names affect discipline. If the name suggests enclosure, crews may tighten the geometry under stress.”

Kessa had not expected help from him on that side of the argument.

Marl answered, “Then define the name before somebody else does. Net of sightings, not a fence. Shepherding ships away from collision as much as animals away from orbital planes.”

Kessa watched the adult’s broad lens move across the plot. Humanity had pinned its juveniles for one hundred sixty-eight years and called the wounds roads. One improvised formation could not turn that history into stewardship. It could only perform the next piece of work without repeating the injury.

“Channel designation Shepherd Net,” she said reluctantly. “Open gradients only. No closed contour. Any two nodes cut it. Put that definition in every header.”

The name propagated across ships, tugs, and routekeeper stations. The Shepherd Net appeared on the plot not as a mesh but as changing relationships: sighting lines, beam windows, escape gaps, and local veto pairs.

Preparation moved from model to hardware.

Continuity rolled to expose its repaired radiator bank. Routekeeper crews locked independent clocks onto all nine resonator trunks. The three neutral cutters spread beyond the adult’s projected leading curve. Their weapons remained sealed. Tugs from Nacre, Brine, and Orison carried released cage beams into positions no route manual recognized.

Irian began convoy thrust in staggered clusters. The movement looked almost too small to matter. Across 70.4 million tonnes, minutes of timing shifted the mass center away from Brine’s orbital plane and opened working room near the adult wake.

Threadbare crossed from node to node with the cold-shear cutter secured beneath its forward rail. Kessa checked every physical cutoff. Sena verified local lead times. Pell found one clock sharing power with a heater and moved it to an isolated cell. Anu rejected two gradient settings after fresh filament samples showed contraction.

Halvek transmitted his final timing table. “Traveling Set One opens on the adult’s first beat. Set Two follows after local trough confirmation. Set Three remains dark until the wounded juvenile clears.”

“No automatic progression,” Kessa said.

“Each set requires local routekeeper release.”

“Good.”

His image remained on the corner of her slate. “Rho, Continuity can stabilize the outer node if something fails.”

“At what cost?”

“The cruiser enters the gradient deeper than planned.”

“That is not today’s solution.”

“No. It belongs in the failure table.”

She added it without pretending the possibility carried no human crew.

By the time Threadbare reached its central sighting position, the Lumen Reach had rearranged itself around moving life. Fixed corridor marks remained dark. Released juveniles traced narrow wakes toward the adult. The convoy waited in staggered clusters. Twelve thousand people watched from provisioned shelters beyond the crossing line.

Kessa clipped Tomis’s torque key beside the local load pickup. Its obsolete cavity registered physical stress without accepting Charter classifications. She placed her left palm over the grapple console. The scar there had faded to a pale interruption, less reliable than the tools and impossible to ignore.

“Every node report,” she said.

One by one, the formation answered.

Continuity: nine projectors manual, routekeeper certified.

Neutral cutters: three sighting arcs clear.

Nacre beams: outer stations open, middle stations ready.

Brine routekeeper tugs: hard cutoffs live.

Irian: convoy mass on schedule, distributed nodes active.

Anu: filament contraction below limit, biological stop armed.

Sena: Threadbare aligned inside the central ranging gap, all exits ugly but available.

No answer promised success. Each reported a condition somebody could verify.

Ahead, bent starlight climbed over the adult’s unseen back. The remaining juveniles answered from their cages with uneven loads. Kessa felt the first beat enter the tug through Tomis’s key, the second through the grapple drums, and the third through every cable laid against the hull.

Across the Shepherd Net, ships took their positions.

The final migration pulse began.

Chapter 25 — The Crossing of No Roads

Kessa watched the first ship leave the last line on every chart.

It went sideways by instruments and forward to the eye, a Brine water tender carrying three hundred people and enough seed culture to restart six vats. Released hollows had drawn a shear thread through the dark ahead of it. The thread showed only as a kink in three stars and a disagreement between clocks. No corridor mouth opened. No resonator held a throat stable. The tender entered on a burn calculated by forty-one ships and immediately vanished from Threadbare's trustworthy geometry.

“Node Four still sees her,” Sena said.

Kessa kept one glove on the tug's manual brass rail. Tiny load changes traveled through the hull as the Shepherd Net shifted around the adult veilback. “Ask Four for raw angles. No composite.”

Pell relayed the request. A moment later, a child's drawing slate tied into the sighting bus displayed three chalk-colored lines. One came from a routekeeper tug. One came from a seed barge with an old survey cupola. The third belonged to a habitat observatory that had never expected to steer anything larger than its own mirror.

Their intersection placed the tender nine kilometres from the fleet solution.

Sena corrected the receiving mark. “Nine becomes ninety if everyone trusts the average.”

“Then nobody trusts it.” Kessa opened the fleet work channel. “All ships, reject composite position for Tender Brine Two. Carry Node Four's raw triangle. Do not chase her apparent drift.”

The order repeated outward through human voices because the network could no longer guarantee sequence. Irian had issued the manual slates on Day Eleven, when Tern broke the convoy into pieces and automatic route solutions began lying in different directions. Now those battered boards carried ship names, clock offsets, sighting pairs, and correction limits in grease pencil. Some had been made for schoolrooms.

Tender Brine Two reappeared twenty-two seconds later, exactly where Node Four had placed her. Her fusion plume bent into a pale hook. Then the ship crossed the shear thread and emerged beyond the final anchor plane.

A cheer started on a civilian channel.

Irian cut across it. “Save breath. We have seventy million tonnes behind her.”

The second vessel entered too high. A family carrier named Copper Mercy had retained one automatic correction layer after its captain misunderstood the order. When Node Two's mark shifted, the layer fired both port tugs against a position already six seconds old. The carrier began to yaw with four hundred occupied berths along its spin band.

“Kill correction,” three voices said at once.

Its captain killed the wrong bus. Cabin lights vanished across one third of the hull, but the tow program continued pulling. On Kessa's slate, local marks opened like snapped jaws.

Sena selected a direct channel. “Copper Mercy, your port tug owns the stale solution. Cut its data, not its power.”

“Port says star pair stable.”

“Stable on yesterday's road,” Kessa said. “Ask your aft blister what it sees.”

A teenager answered from the blister observatory. His voice cracked while he read angles from a manual reticle. Node Six confirmed his pair. The captain isolated the port feed and handed thrust to the blister crew. Their first correction came late, the second came rough, and the third brought the carrier through with enough clearance to spare its antenna forest.

Emergency lights returned after the ship reached ordinary geometry. The teenager kept reading marks until another navigator relieved him. He had occupied a node and carried four hundred berths where no central system could.

Accord of Dust lay six hundred kilometres aft, eleven kilometres of truss and turning habitats amid barges, tugs, cylinders, tanks, and shelters. The convoy moved like machinery dismantled in motion, held together by fuel margins and people reading separate stars.

Behind all of them, the adult bent half the visible field around its vast moving topology. Eight juveniles pulled at the last cages in answer to its pulse. Three already moved free, while Sable's wounded animal lagged outside their curve. The Shepherd Net held no creature. Its cutter beams and tug masses shaped gradients along open sides, trying to keep the molt corridor clear of Brine's inhabited plane.

Three beats entered Threadbare through the deck.

Kessa checked the pulse clock. “Next trough in seven minutes. Bring the hospital cylinder through before it closes.”

Kindly Argument had lost two navigation arrays at Tern. Its hull still carried a white scar where an exclusion cone had taken Charter fire at Orison Gap. Four sighting nodes bracketed it now. Each sent unmerged angles to the hospital's bridge, while routekeepers spoke the corrections aloud.

The cylinder crossed without incident. A grain barge followed. Then came two Brine habitats, a machine-seed carrier, six fuel bladders on a common tow, and the forward drum of Accord with its rotation slowed to protect the bearings. Every passage altered the thread. There could be no fixed timetable because every mass changed the next ship's road.

At the fleet pit, Irian refused three requests to move guidance back onto Accord.

Kessa heard the last refusal over the shared channel. “My carrier cannot see around its own freight,” Irian said. “Keep authority at the nodes that hold the angles.”

A Charter cutter captain answered, “Central helm could resolve conflicting commands.”

“Central helm could issue one mistake to everyone. Maintain distributed sighting.”

The cutter dropped the argument. Its pilot began reading Node Seven's marks instead.

Sena touched two fingers to her display. “Habitat Wren has a tow mismatch.”

Wren showed as an empty habitat module near the convoy's middle. Twelve thousand people had chosen shelters in the Reach rather than the crossing, and Irian had provisioned them before the fleet turned. Their evacuated modules still carried machinery that could not be replaced: air roots, water recovery organs, soil drums, foundries, nursery walls, and power buses old enough to have become architecture. Wren had served families for seventy years. Its spin shell now carried no one, but two cargo crews rode the tug pair moving it.

Kessa pulled the raw marks. Wren's two tugs agreed about thrust and disagreed about position. A shear fold had slipped between them. The forward tug saw the module eight metres high. The aft tug placed it twenty-one metres low.

“Both can be right,” Sena said.

“They won't stay attached if they are.”

Kessa switched channels. “Wren tow, drop common autopilot. Each tug hold local load only. Report cable phase.”

Forward tow answered first. “Red cable cycling at four seconds.”

Aft tow said, “Blue at six. We have rotation coming into the spine.”

Wren's long cylinder rolled by a fraction. Starlight along its hull split into two images, then four. One tug pulled against a position the other had not reached. The common tow frame took the difference.

“Evacuate both crews,” Irian said.

The forward pilot protested. “We can dump the machine deck and save the shell.”

Kessa examined the load path. Wren's mass could cross if the two tugs entered the same local geometry within less than half a second. Their clocks differed by three seconds under the fold. No signal could tell either pilot which second belonged to the other.

“Not with people aboard the tugs,” she said. “They'll have to leave controls live and come off by service pods.”

Wren carried no residents, yet twelve technicians worked its tow. The fleet had treated empty as permission to risk it. Kessa felt that old habit in the speed of their planning. Machinery without bodies became expendable only after someone forgot who would need it later.

Irian did not forget. “What can be removed in twelve minutes?”

“Seed cores, memory rods, two water cultures,” the module keeper said. Her voice remained level until she named the nursery archive. “And seven decades of birth records if the rack releases.”

“You have nine,” Kessa told her. “The next fold cuts your pod lane.”

Work channels multiplied. Cargo crews entered Wren through opposite hubs while the tugs maintained local load. Kessa assigned Threadbare to the forward node, close enough to throw grapples if a pod drifted but not close enough to add uncontrolled mass.

The module keeper went back for the archive herself. Her name, Irian's roster showed, was Dava Pellin. She had lived in Wren for fifty-eight of its seventy years and knew which deck plates lifted without tools. A helmet camera followed her through an unpowered nursery where painted animals walked around the curved walls. The pictures had survived six changes of settlement ownership. Pencil marks beside one door recorded children growing upward until spin made the direction seem obvious.

“Archive rack jammed at the lower rail,” Dava said. Her breath sounded too loud in the channel.

Kessa watched the tow loads. “Leave the casing. Pull the memory rods by hand.”

“The rods lose indexing outside the rack.”

Anu answered from a biological survey node. “Wrap each bundle with the label strip. I can rebuild the index from order and checksums.”

Dava removed her outer glove to reach the recessed latch. Kessa nearly ordered her to stop, then saw the keeper had isolated the compartment and tied the glove to her wrist. Old hands worked the latch by touch. The first rail released. The second remained bent under spin load.

“Wren, forward tug phase just shortened,” Pell warned.

Seven minutes remained before the pod lane folded. Dava braced one boot against the nursery door and hauled. The rack came free hard enough to drive her shoulder into a painted antler. Loose growth records fluttered past her camera, paper kept because children liked finding their own names without opening a screen.

She gathered none of the pages. She secured the rack and pushed toward the hub.

“Keeper, you left documents,” a cargo tech said.

“I left copies,” Dava replied. “I brought the births.”

The tug crossed one minor fold under Sena's hands. Stars leaned, recovered, then leaned the wrong direction. Kessa read the hull through her boots. A route had once protected ships from this kind of argument. It had also turned a living body into a fixture so no pilot needed to feel the dispute.

“Forward node just went blind,” Pell said.

Its signal vanished from the slate. Not noise or power loss. The node's carrier still answered status calls, but every sighting arrived with one star duplicated into six incompatible positions.

Kessa found the ship on external view: a routekeeper launch called Skein, running beside Wren with a survey mast built from cage inspection gear. One mast hinge had opened under differential pull. The array pointed through its own exhaust haze.

“If Skein stays blind, Wren's pods lose their near mark,” Sena said.

“Put us alongside.”

“Geometry won't hold for docking.”

“Then don't dock.”

Threadbare moved across the launch's apparent path. Sena trusted load, plume, and two distant observations rather than the glittering lie in the cupola. Kessa sealed her helmet and entered the forward lock with Vasken. The rigger carried a spool clamp, a line gun, and no suggestion that someone else should go.

Outside, the Shepherd Net filled space with separated points of light. Continuity held the strongest position aft, its nine beam ports tracing faint red lines past the adult. Neutral cutters moved as shepherd masses along the open edge. Routekeeper tugs occupied the dangerous inner nodes. Far beyond them, the leading convoy appeared as scattered sparks with no corridor walls to make them one thing.

Kessa clipped to Threadbare's rail. “Match Skein's mast root, not her hull.”

Sena rolled the tug. The launch seemed to tumble beneath them, although Kessa's suit load stayed steady. She fired the line gun. Its hook curved away, came back from a direction no straight cable should permit, and caught the broken mast below the hinge.

Vasken locked the spool clamp. “Line says nine tonnes.”

“My arm says five.”

“Split the difference?”

“Not unless you brought half an arm.”

They paid out line until the suit load and tug drum agreed. Kessa pulled herself toward the mast. The gap crossed twenty metres by laser and perhaps fourteen by cable. She kept her eyes on the braid. Space could offer as many images as it liked; tension still had to arrive somewhere.

At the hinge, a locking tooth had cracked. One half remained seated while the other floated three centimetres away, held apart by local gradient. Kessa could not force the pieces together without twisting Skein's hull.

“Cold cutter,” she said.

Vasken passed it along the line. “You're cutting off their array?”

“I'm cutting off its bad idea.”

She severed the cracked tooth, rotated the mast on its surviving pin, and drove the spool clamp through two maintenance eyes. The repair would never pass inspection. It only had to hold an angle long enough for twelve people to leave Wren.

A service pod emerged from the habitat's forward hub.

Its pilot called, “No near mark.”

Kessa slapped the mast housing. “Give them one, Skein.”

The node sent a raw star pair. The pod's course changed by three degrees, carrying six technicians and two seed cases toward a receiving barge. A second pod followed with the remaining crew. It trailed a cargo net full of dark cylinders.

“Birth rack?” Irian asked.

The module keeper answered from the pod. “Released whole.”

Wren rolled again. One tow cable went slack. The other climbed toward failure load.

“Cut both tugs free,” Kessa said.

The aft pilot hesitated. “If we release, it crosses the fleet track.”

Sena had already laid the projection. Wren would drift behind the next convoy cluster if freed now. If a tug held five seconds longer, the module could strike the machine-seed carrier sideways.

Irian spoke on the command circuit. “Tow crews, release Habitat Wren.”

No one moved for one second.

“Release,” the module keeper said.

Both cutters fired. Blue and red tow lines recoiled into darkness. Wren continued under its inherited rotation, window bands unlit, soil drums secured behind layers of metal. Seventy years of pumps, repairs, meals, arguments, and measured childhood heights crossed the sighting slate as a mass with no valid destination.

A cargo pilot asked whether a spare tug should follow.

Irian answered, “Negative. Mark it and clear the next passage.”

Kessa returned along the line while Skein's repaired mast tracked the empty habitat. Wren drifted toward the wake's unstable edge. Its hull lengthened in bent light, narrowing until the cylinder looked like a bright wire. Then the shear took the spin shell. One end moved ahead of the other by a distance no structure could absorb.

The module folded without flame.

Its pressureless decks opened in a line of pale fragments. Soil and machine housings spread along the fold, each piece following a separate curve. The nursery archive had made it off. The water cultures had made it off. The building itself did not.

No cheer followed the rescue.

Vasken pulled Kessa onto Threadbare's rail. “Mast holds.”

“Leave the clamp.”

“That was our good one.”

“Now it's their bad one.”

He laughed once, without pleasure, and entered the lock.

The convoy resumed. Wren's loss reduced the mass problem but worsened every future settlement calculation. Irian's logistics crews redistributed air-root capacity before the fragments had vanished. Marl Quade placed the rescued technicians among three ships rather than letting a single captain claim their expertise. No ceremony delayed the crossing.

By the next pulse trough, Accord of Dust reached the thread.

The carrier could not enter as one rigid body. Tug clusters pulled its attached habitats into a controlled curve while sighting nodes called local positions. Irian's voice stayed off the guidance net unless a conflict required command. Kessa heard other people carry the ship: a Brine observatory keeper, a Charter pilot from a neutral cutter, a school navigator reading grease marks, Sena correcting a clock offset from Threadbare's scarred cupola.

“Forward truss across,” someone said.

“Habitat band three sees clean stars.”

“Fuel farm low by twelve metres local.”

“Do not correct. Twelve belongs to Node Nine's clock.”

Halfway through, two nodes exchanged names. A neutral cutter had inherited the call sign of a routekeeper tug during the Orison rescue, and both still answered to Seven under pressure. One sighted Accord's seed vault. The other watched a train of water bladders two hundred kilometres away. Their angles entered the spoken net under one label.

The carrier's starboard tug cluster began a correction toward the wrong mass.

Irian could have frozen every helm from her logistics pit. Kessa saw the command request appear and remain unanswered. Instead Irian called Marl.

“Delegate check. Which Seven has local trust?”

Passenger stations polled their assigned ships. Replies came by voice, slate flash, maintenance carrier, and one line-of-sight lamp from a vessel with no working transmitter. Marl returned two new labels: Seven-Cutter and Seven-Tug. The mistaken angles separated before Accord accumulated enough sideways motion to endanger its bearings.

“Record both designators across every slate,” Irian said. “No automatic rename.”

Her bridge could have corrected the confusion faster for one minute. Leaving the correction distributed made it harder for the same error to capture everyone at once. Kessa heard the discipline in Irian's silence after the order. A commander trained to gather control had refused it while her own ship bent across the dark.

The mass carrier bent to the eye. Its central truss curved around nothing visible. Kessa knew metal could not tolerate that shape. The apparent bend belonged to light and separated local frames, but knowledge did not soften the sight.

Three beats passed through the tug again.

This time the adult answered close enough to move every unfastened tool in Threadbare's repair gallery. The hull creaked from bow to drum. Behind the convoy, darkness lifted across forty thousand kilometres. Lines of bent stars peeled away from the adult's back as if an immense skin had loosened.

Anu entered the work channel. “Topology change along the dorsal lens. Not a pursuit response. The molt has started.”

Kessa looked aft. Eleven juvenile signatures tightened along the chain toward the adult's wake. The wounded Sable animal lagged outside the common curve. Around them, the Shepherd Net adjusted node by node.

Ahead lay no charted anchor, no green cage status, and no authority capable of flattening the measurements into one approved line. The convoy's leading ships burned in ordinary dark, beyond every fixed route humanity had built.

Sena eased Threadbare across the final shear boundary.

The last anchor stars straightened behind them. Then the adult opened another luminous fracture through the dark, and its old shape began to come apart.

Chapter 26 — The Molt

The adult opened along a line no instrument agreed existed.

Bent stars tore apart behind the convoy. For half a second Kessa saw the Shepherd Net repeated in six false skies, every tug and cutter occupying several incompatible positions. Then Threadbare's displays rejected the images, leaving only raw loads, clock offsets, and the three-beat pulse moving through the hull.

Sena held the tug against a gradient that changed faster than the engines could answer. “I have two sterns.”

“Fly the one pulling on us.”

“I preferred the other.”

The cupola darkened as the adult's topology unfolded across forty thousand kilometres. Its baryonic core remained unseen, small against the lens body around it. What opened had no fleshly edge. Starlight stretched across translucent fractures, and each fracture carried the wake of a creature changing from migratory body to something larger and less compact.

Eleven juvenile signatures tightened along the migration chain. Three had traveled free since the nine-minute dark. Eight remained inside cages whose outer stations had begun going dark as the Shepherd Net formed. Each compact topology waited at a different point along the pulse cycle.

Sable's wounded juvenile remained outside the curve.

Anu sent a biological limit map to every net node. “Do not force alignment. We can shape the gradient ahead of it. We cannot pull it into phase.”

One Charter pilot asked, “What happens if it misses the molt?”

“We have one dead adult showing that answer,” Anu said. “Keep your beam off the animal.”

Kessa expanded the release board across her console. Twelve circles marked the migration chain. Eleven carried live timing. Sable blinked between signals as scarred filaments failed and regrew. The safe sequence discovered in the dead route still governed every transition: outer three, contraction wait, middle three, core triad during the pulse trough. No central command held these juveniles now, but their own folded geometry followed the same order as they left the cages and joined the adult.

“First group approaching trough,” Pell said.

Kessa touched the fleet channel. “Nacre, Brine, Morrow Span, confirm outer release.”

Three routekeepers answered with load values, not confidence. Nacre's juvenile loosened first. Brine followed. Morrow Span, carrying old cage scars, held one outer fold longer than predicted.

“Do not advance Morrow Span,” Kessa said.

A cutter beam brightened at its flank, shaping an open gradient toward the adult. Morrow Span's fold released. All three signatures moved inward.

The next pulse reached them.

Three beats. The first loaded every grapple drum on Threadbare. The second made Kessa's old palm scar prickle inside her glove. The third crossed the net and returned through eleven hulls at different intervals.

“Middle release now.”

Routekeeper tugs rolled, putting their masses across unwanted slopes. Neutral cutters reduced beam output in sequence. The juveniles unfolded without the violent snap Kessa remembered from Tern. Their wakes joined the adult's fractures, thin curves merging into a broader moving surface.

For one minute the plan worked exactly as drawn.

The Orison group entered next. Their three juveniles had lived inside linked cages whose control software treated them as one corridor machine. Release crews had severed that logic, but the animals still carried mismatched hold scars. One opened its middle folds early. Another remained compact through a full trough. The third drifted toward the first as their wakes crossed.

“Separate them with mass, not beam,” Anu said.

Two former Charter cutters moved between the signatures with drives off. Their armored hulls offered a shallow gradient each juvenile could pass around. Kessa called the release from local clocks while routekeepers at Orison read filament contraction through abandoned cage sensors. Nothing synchronized cleanly. One cutter rolled six degrees under the load and lost an antenna panel. The juveniles curved apart, completed their middle release, and took separate places along the adult's open flank.

The antenna panel did not drift clear. Orison's middle juvenile caught it in a shallow lens pocket, where the sheet of armor began circling a filament branch. Every pass drew it closer. No animal chose the metal. The local gradient treated it as available mass.

“Panel impact in forty seconds,” Pell said.

A routekeeper tug named Second Wrench held nearest, but its projector shaped the open path for the third juvenile. If it moved, two damaged wakes might cross again.

Kessa searched the Net for another mass. The lost panel measured twelve tonnes. A direct beam could push it, but the beam would close across living tissue for nearly a second.

“Cargo shell Eighteen,” Irian said over the shared board. “Empty, no crew, two working thrusters.”

The shell waited outside Orison's local fold as a distributed sighting node. Moving it would blind one angle on Accord's trailing water train.

“Can your convoy carry the gap?” Kessa asked.

“Nodes Eleven and Fourteen can cross-sight for ninety seconds. After that the water train reaches the shear.”

“Ninety is enough.”

Local crews took the shell. Its command receiver saw the panel in the wrong position, so a pilot on Second Wrench spoke each thruster pulse while Irian's sighting nodes called raw angle. The empty shell entered the lens pocket broadside. For several seconds it seemed to move away. Tension on its service loop showed approach.

“Impact,” Pell said.

The two masses met at walking speed. Cargo Shell Eighteen took the panel across its empty hold and began to spin. Its thrusters fired once more, carrying both pieces out through an open edge. Node Fourteen inherited the sighting load before the water train reached the gap.

The middle juvenile completed its release without metal crossing its filaments. The shell and panel drifted toward an unoccupied recovery orbit nobody would visit until the molt ended.

Kessa marked the node dark and heard Irian acknowledge the lost equipment. Neither treated a disposable hull as costless. The shell had bought a living clearance and left the convoy with one fewer pair of eyes.

Two distant teams followed. Their pictures arrived too late to guide Kessa, so command belonged to people beside each former cage. She heard confirmations after the actions had happened: an outer trio dark at Pale Reach, contraction holding beyond Orison, core release complete beside a station whose name she had seen only on license forms. The Net carried consequences rather than orders across those delays. The three juveniles already free began their own compact release along the adult's wake, including Tern with its uneven scars.

By the time eleven signatures aligned, hundreds of workers had made decisions no central helm could revise. Kessa's board showed a coherent migration curve built from local acts, each one small enough to fail without permission.

Then Brine's orbital alarms filled the channel.

A new lens edge had appeared above the inhabited plane. It ran toward the agricultural cylinders and the convoy's trailing stores. No creature changed direction. The adult's unfolding topology simply occupied more space than their measurements had allowed.

Irian spoke from beyond the shear boundary. “Time to plane crossing?”

“Sixteen minutes at current growth,” Sena said.

Anu corrected her. “Nine if the ventral folds open next.”

The Shepherd Net shifted. Four routekeeper tugs burned toward the inner edge. Two neutral cutters moved their beams outward. Continuity held the aft node, its nine resonator ports projecting the strongest movable gradient any human ship carried.

Kessa dragged the net geometry by hand. “Node Three must move forty kilometres spinward.”

“Node Three is a fuel barge,” Pell said.

“Then it needs help.”

Irian assigned six tugs without taking their controls. The barge began its slow relocation, but the lens edge outran it. Local clocks separated. Guidance calls arrived out of order. A tug acknowledged an instruction Kessa had not yet sent.

The adult opened another fold.

Every work light near Brine bent toward the cylinders. Transfer spines flexed under tidal load. A vat habitat reported bearing alarms. The convoy's distributed sighting nodes tracked the danger from outside, but observation could not provide mass where the net had left a hole.

Halvek entered Kessa's direct channel. His face did not appear. Only his voice and Continuity's raw beam table came through. “The cruiser can occupy Three.”

“You cannot reach it before the ventral opening.”

“I can if we stop pretending the ship needs to leave.”

Kessa studied his proposed burn. Continuity would cross two shear folds, discharge most maneuvering reserve, and settle broadside inside the failing node. Its hull mass could stabilize the local gradient. The nine resonator beams would spread the load until the juveniles completed release.

Afterward, the cruiser would have no path out.

“Your bridge is under restriction,” Kessa said.

“My officers can verify the burn.”

“That does not answer me.”

“It answers the geometry.”

A second voice joined, Captain Orra Senn from Mica Hand. She coordinated the cutter locked to Continuity after its crew accepted neutral rescue status. “Enro remains under restricted watch. I have two officers aboard his bridge and a cutter locked to his ventral port.”

“Crew count?” Kessa asked.

“Four hundred twelve remain on Continuity.”

The cruiser had already evacuated specialists and nonessential watches before entering the Net. Four hundred twelve still exceeded any acceptable sacrifice.

Halvek said, “We can clear all but the placement crews in seven minutes.”

“You have less than nine.”

“I know what less means.”

Kessa heard his old arithmetic and hated that it fit. “Begin evacuation. No one stays because you call the ship necessary.”

“Agreed.”

Senn did not soften. “Marshal, you leave on my cutter. Your arrest does not end with your command.”

A pause held long enough for Continuity's evacuation alarm to enter the channel. “Understood,” Halvek said.

The cruiser broke position.

Its departure opened the aft edge of the Net, but three routekeeper tugs moved into the temporary gap. Continuity burned across their sterns with beam ports dark and cutters clustered around its hull. Escape pods and service launches streamed from both docking bays. Every departing craft altered the burn. Halvek's bridge crew compensated by changing beam mass rather than demanding a clear lane.

Kessa watched names move from cruiser roster to receiving ships. Navigation. Reactor maintenance. Medical. Galley. Beam control. Damage crews. No category carried the word expendable.

At three hundred eighty-six remaining, the roster stopped changing.

“Dorsal bay reports full,” Pell said.

The receiving cutters had berth volume for more bodies but not for more launch mass. Two service boats waited inside Continuity with loaded crews and insufficient propellant to cross the moving fold. The cruiser could keep them aboard for the placement burn or strip fuel from its maneuver reserve.

Halvek opened the tank table. “Transfer port attitude reserve to both boats.”

Rusk objected from the bridge watch. “You need that reserve to hold broadside in Node Three.”

“Then we transfer starboard.”

“Starboard feeds your final roll.”

Every tank supported a later action. Kessa recognized the trap from salvage jobs where damaged machinery made all remaining functions look essential. Human bodies could not remain the adjustable margin.

“Empty two beam coolant tanks,” she said.

A beam officer answered, “Without coolant, Projectors Six and Eight lose seven seconds of service.”

“How many seconds does the Net require?”

“Unknown until the ventral fold opens.”

Anu placed the biological limit over their table. Seven seconds might separate an open gradient from injury. Keeping the boats aboard might place eighty-three crew inside a hull already committed to loss.

Irian entered with a third option. “My outer fuel train can send a bladder, but it reaches you after first fold contact.”

“Too late,” Halvek said.

Kessa examined the service boats. Their tanks lacked enough propellant for a free crossing because evacuation planners had budgeted them as dock-to-dock craft. They did carry pressure and docking thrust. A neutral cutter could tow both if someone built a common line before Continuity reached the fold.

“Ventral cutter takes them in train,” she said. “Boats burn only to clear the bay. Cutter supplies translation.”

Senn calculated the tow load. “My stern eyes carry one boat by code.”

“Code assumed weapons and full armor.”

“We still have armor.”

“Lose the empty missile rack.”

Senn's crew blew eight rack panels and their mounting frame into a recovery trajectory. The cutter rolled beneath Continuity's dorsal bay and fired two tow lines through the service boats' bow eyes. Cruiser deck crews joined the boats with a third line so their thrust could not separate them.

The first boat cleared under docking jets. Its tow snapped taut and pulled Senn's cutter three degrees off the verified lane. The second emerged before she corrected. Both boats swung behind her across a lens image of the cruiser's own bow.

“Train clear,” Senn said.

Eighty-three names moved from cruiser roster to boats. The cutter carried no spare acceleration margin now. It would follow Continuity into the first fold and peel away before Node Three.

Halvek restored the beam coolant tanks to the placement plan. Nobody called the solution free. Senn had discarded hardware and accepted a worse escape burn. Irian's fuel train retained its reserve. Four hundred twelve people still had a path that did not require the cruiser to survive.

Chief engineer Mera Vos refused the first transfer order. Her face appeared briefly on the shared roster channel, lined by decades in engine heat. “The ballast sequence needs my hand,” she told Halvek.

“Your deputy has the sequence.”

“He has six months in this hull.”

“Then he has six months more than the tug crews receiving you.” Halvek released her console permissions while he spoke. “Transfer the knowledge from the cutter. Go.”

Vos looked toward someone beyond her camera. “You taught us that abandoning route hardware abandoned everyone depending on it.”

“I taught you to confuse hardware with continuity.”

The admission did not ask forgiveness. It arrived as an operational correction, late and costly. Vos stripped a diagnostic ring from her wrist and locked it around her deputy's arm. Then she joined the line at the dorsal bay.

A mess worker carried two sealed casualty books under one arm. Another crew member tried to bring the cruiser crest, a metal plate too large for the cutter hatch. Senn's officer made him leave it against the bulkhead. They saved reactor logs, personnel rolls, hold-command archives, and the local copies of Halvek's suppressed model. Nobody saved the emblem.

The final galley team crossed with food packs clipped to their suits. Even under evacuation, someone had counted what the receiving cutters could feed. Kessa understood why Irian kept logistics officers near every moral decision. Survival continued to arrive in containers, and abandoning a ship did not excuse abandoning the next meal.

At three hundred remaining, the first shear fold caught Continuity's bow.

The cruiser stretched on visual display. Its hull instruments disagreed by sixty metres. A military autopilot would have corrected against the apparent bend and broken the ship. Halvek ordered manual thrust from local accelerometers. The bow came through.

At one hundred twelve, a launch jammed in the dorsal bay.

“Bay collar distorted,” a watch officer reported. “Forty-three aboard.”

Sena marked Threadbare toward them.

Kessa stopped her. “We leave Node Five, the wounded juvenile loses its open gradient.”

“I know.”

A neutral cutter reached the bay first. It could not dock, so its crew fired two grapples through the launch's emergency eyes and tore the collar outward. The launch came free trailing insulation and half a door. It rolled into the cutter's exclusion cone, close enough for both ships' alarms to merge.

“Launch clear,” Senn said. “Seventy-nine remain.”

The lens edge reached Brine's outer service web.

Metal trusses bent in apparent arcs. One transfer spindle parted, its fragments following separate trajectories without striking a habitat. Kessa had minutes before the agricultural cylinders entered the same slope.

“Continuity, light your port beams at five percent,” she said.

“Port five,” Halvek answered.

A faint red fan crossed the dark. It did not touch the adult or Sable's juvenile. It pressed against the open side of the moving topology, offering a shallower path away from the orbital plane. The lens edge slowed.

“Ten percent. Roll seven degrees.”

The cruiser rolled while evacuation craft detached. Its broadside entered Node Three. Loads climbed across its frame.

Pell read the numbers. “Keel differential past design.”

Halvek said, “Design assumed fixed space.”

“Steel does not care what design assumed,” Kessa replied. “Hold nine percent and vent your starboard ballast.”

Water, shielding pellets, and reserve propellant streamed from Continuity. The released mass spread through the node, each fragment creating a small correction. Brine's lens edge stopped three kilometres above the service web.

Eleven juvenile signatures reached middle release.

Kessa called them one by one. Nacre. Brine. Tern. Orison's three. The dead route's survivor. Four anchors whose names had once meant only destinations. Every routekeeper team confirmed contraction before allowing the next topology to open. No command propagated automatically. People read local loads and waited when the animal timing required patience.

Sable continued to lag.

Its signature fluttered outside the adult's ventral fold. Scar tissue from the doubled hold had closed in the wrong order. Each pulse drew it forward; each damaged outer layer pulled it back. The adult's topology widened around the absence, extending the dangerous edge toward Brine again.

Anu's voice tightened. “It cannot reach core release while that outer scar carries load.”

“Can we cut the scar?”

“Not at this distance. A beam would worsen it.”

Kessa looked at the net. Node Five, where Threadbare held position, sat between Sable and the adult. The tug's mass could not pull the juvenile. Its cold-shear cutter could act only within metres. No ship could cross the changing lens, cut living filament, and leave before the trough.

Tomis's torque key knocked against the console.

Three soft contacts, driven by the pulse through Threadbare's frame.

Kessa took it in her scarred hand. The tool had carried Lark-3's hidden log because its obsolete cavity escaped Charter cleanup. It had opened compressor housings, cage panels, and one truth she had accepted too late. Now its brass length gave her a better instrument than the timing display. The display averaged eleven arrivals. The key transmitted the local load under her palm.

“Sable does not need a cut,” she said.

Anu answered at once. “Explain.”

“The scar closes out of order because it never gets a full contraction wait. Every net correction pulls the outer layer during the pause.”

“We remove Node Five and lose its path.”

“We don't remove it. We go dark between beats.”

Sena saw the maneuver. “Ballistic for two point six seconds.”

Pell said, “Adult edge crosses us in two point two.”

“Local measurement?” Kessa asked.

“Node Five local.”

“Then two point two means ours.”

Kessa sent the plan to Anu and waited through a silence measured by loads. Anu checked Sable's regeneration trace. “It could contract. No certainty it will.”

“Nothing in this net came with certainty.”

“Do it on the next pulse.”

Behind them, Continuity began to fail.

A beam port tore from the cruiser's dorsal spine. Red light swung across empty space before the system shut down. The hull lost fourteen percent of its stabilizing output. Brine's lens edge started moving.

“Marshal, evacuation count,” Senn demanded.

“Six on the bridge. Eight in engineering. My cutter has room.”

“Then leave.”

“The final beam sequence needs a local release.”

Kessa checked his controls. Damage had severed remote command. Someone had to discharge the beam capacitors in order, or their fields would collapse together and kick the adult's open fold toward Brine.

“How long?” she asked.

“Forty seconds after node lock.”

The cruiser might last sixty.

Senn said, “I am docking now. Four of my people will hold your bridge.”

“No,” Halvek replied. “My order built these capacitors into a hold system. I know the release lags.”

“You are preserving your trial, not choosing your monument.”

For once, Halvek did not argue capacity. “Dock and keep engines live.”

Senn's cutter attached to the ventral port while engineering crews crossed by pressure sleeve. The six bridge officers followed. Halvek remained at local beam control under the eyes of two arrest officers positioned inside the cutter hatch.

Kessa returned to Sable's pulse trace.

“Node Five prepare dark,” she said. “All other nodes hold current gradient. Do not compensate for us.”

Sena killed automatic thrust. Pell isolated the sighting transmitter. Vasken released both grapple drum brakes so no stored line load could imitate a correction.

The first beat came through the key.

Kessa counted the rise, not the sound.

At the second beat, Sable's outer scar tightened.

“Dark now.”

Sena cut every engine. Threadbare became eighty-three metres of uncommanded mass inside a changing lens. False stars swept the cupola. The adult's edge reached for them in six luminous images. Kessa kept her hand on the key and felt the juvenile's scar unload.

One second.

Two.

A smaller tremor arrived between pulse beats. Sable had contracted.

“Middle layer opening,” Anu said.

Kessa waited. Every reflex demanded thrust. The tug rolled without permission. Somewhere aft, loose tools struck a wall.

The third beat entered her palm.

“Core triad now. Light Node Five.”

Sena fired. Threadbare's mass pressed into the gradient just as Sable's compact topology opened. The wounded juvenile moved without snapping. Its scarred outer folds trailed unevenly, then settled into the wake made by the other eleven.

The adult matched them.

All twelve signatures crossed the same pulse trough.

Its topology unfolded beyond the scale of Kessa's board. The dangerous edge lifted from Brine's plane. Juvenile wakes braided into the adult's moving body. No voice thanked them. No choice resembling human mercy shaped the departure. An injured animal had found the gradient its life required.

“Node lock,” Halvek said.

On Continuity, he began the final discharge. Eight remaining beams dimmed in sequence. Each loss transferred more load into the hull. The cruiser buckled around its central truss. Armor plates peeled away and followed the wake.

Senn shouted through the open hatch, “Enro, move.”

The seventh beam went dark.

Halvek left the control frame. His suit camera showed him crossing a bridge tilted by structural failure while two arrest officers covered the hatch. He jumped the final gap as the pressure sleeve tore. Senn fired her cutter free before the hatch sealed.

The ninth capacitor discharged inside the cruiser.

Continuity held Node Three for another four seconds as dead mass. Those seconds carried the adult's ventral fold clear of Brine. Then the cruiser entered the shear.

Its bow and stern took different roads. The central truss opened. Reactor vessels separated under automatic scram, dark and intact, while the rest of the ship flattened into a widening curve of metal. No explosion provided a clean ending. The strongest movable hold in the Reach became fragments too dispersed to pin anything again.

Senn's cutter emerged beyond the fold trailing atmosphere. “Enro aboard. Arrest remains in force. Six injured, no fatalities on final evacuation.”

Kessa closed her eyes for one breath. Saving the crew did not restore the dead at Tern, Orison, or Sable. Destroying the instrument did not erase the orders issued from its bridge. Halvek had survived to meet names, evidence, and judgment.

Across the chain, fixed routes collapsed.

Anchor mouths narrowed at Nacre, Brine, Tern, Orison, Sable, and seven distant stations. Resonator crews powered down arrays that no longer contained hollows. One by one, the approved green corridors vanished from every navigation slate. Seventy-three routes across the Reach did not all fail that hour, but the connected economy built on captive juvenile bodies had ended. No safe sequence could make the old arrangement harmless.

The adult moved outward with twelve smaller distortions gathered along its wake. Shed fibers glimmered behind the molt, thin enough to bend single stars instead of whole fields. Anu recorded them without naming a use.

Kessa watched until the final juvenile signature passed beyond the Net.

No instant corridor opened in its place.

The beams shut down. Tug drives fell quiet. Straight stars returned where the adult had occupied the sky, unfamiliar because no cage doubled them into useful arcs.

Threadbare drifted in true dark among the scattered ships.

Beneath Kessa's hand, Tomis's torque key lay still.

Chapter 27 — What Travel Costs

The relief ship arrived with frost over its name.

Seven months of fusion burn had sanded paint from the bow and driven coolant ice into every seam. Common Measure entered Brine on a plume the color of old copper, too slow to make spectacle and too heavy with cargo to waste thrust on grace. Irian watched from the logistics gallery of Accord of Dust while four local tugs eased toward it.

No route mouth delivered the ship. No doubled stars promised safe geometry. Its crew had crossed ordinary dark from Nacre with an engine, stored reaction mass, and patience measured in consumed parts. The first light from its departure had reached Brine long before the vessel.

A controller beside Irian wrote on a paper board: MONTH 7, DAY 203.

The date mattered because every report from the old route economy still wanted to compress the journey into an arrival code. On those forms, delay occupied one box. Here, delay had eaten seed reserve, separated families, and turned medical inventories into lists of names.

“Range one thousand,” the tug lead called. “Target rotation two tenths high.”

Common Measure answered after the brief local transmission lag. “We can stop rotation or preserve pump power. Choose.”

“Preserve pumps,” Irian said. “Tugs will take the mismatch.”

Her order traveled through Brine control, not a convoy command circuit. She held no centralized helm now. Accord still carried 398,000 evacuees and much of the surviving infrastructure; twelve thousand had remained in provisioned Brine shelters under the Articles. Local councils, ship captains, passenger delegates, and routekeeper cooperatives shared decisions because no single hull could supply everyone.

That arrangement consumed time.

It also caught mistakes before they became fleet policy.

The tug lead acknowledged. Two agricultural cylinders objected to the docking vector because exhaust contamination threatened open algae radiators. A machine shop offered a colder approach. Brine's council approved the change after checking habitat loads. Nine minutes passed before anyone fired an engine.

Seven months earlier, Irian would have called those minutes intolerable.

Now she counted the radiators that remained clean.

Marl Quade entered the gallery carrying three ration slates and a cup of something grown to resemble coffee by people who remembered the word better than the taste. “Inquiry wants you in twenty minutes.”

“Ship docks in forty.”

“They know.”

“Then they know which one I attend.”

Marl set the cup near her hand. “Remote testimony. You can do both badly.”

Irian looked at the slates. “Which habitat?”

“Dross Lantern missed protein yield again. Pell Orchard has spare fungal stock but wants machine hours in exchange. Sable Ring Refuge Three stopped answering yesterday.”

That last line carried no explanation. Communication between systems traveled by courier ship, tightbeam when geometry allowed, and wake-fiber relay experiments that failed more often than they worked. A habitat could stop answering because its transmitter broke. It could also stop answering because no one remained awake to use it.

“Last confirmed inventory?” Irian asked.

“Twenty-six days of air catalyst. Eleven of full ration. Surface elevators at Sable promised a launch if ring debris cleared.”

“Promised when?”

“Six weeks ago.”

Irian wrote Refuge Three beside the cargo allocation for Common Measure. Medical catalyst, pressure seals, compact food substrate, reactor control mesh. Everything she assigned there came away from people she could see.

Survival reports occupied three screens because any single total hid which reserves had failed.

Nacre had reopened three fabrication belts and lost two mining settlements when spare heat exchangers failed. Brine had avoided famine by dividing food substrate among ships before the routes collapsed, yet every cylinder ran below preferred nutrition. Sable's surface cities housed most of the 31,000 displaced from orbital structures. Smaller habitats beyond the old chain endured alone. Some built local fusion craft from station drives. Some dismantled their anchors for metal. Two had gone dark.

Brine's protected child rations and Sable Refuge Three's empty catalyst stores occupied separate lines on the same allocation board.

During Month 2, Brine had cut every adult ration by nine percent and protected children's protein allotments. Month 3 brought the first local harvest from machine seed distributed across the convoy before the crossing. Two batches failed because their host ships could not hold humidity. Five produced enough culture to cover the loss. By Month 4, workshops had standardized six pump parts that once came from separate systems. None matched the old parts' service life, but mechanics could make them without waiting for a route cargo.

Slow fusion convoys began as promises on planning boards. A courier from Sable departed with ring ceramics and three passengers. Brine sent food culture toward Nacre. Nacre assembled Common Measure from a survey tanker and two freight spines because no purpose-built carrier could be spared. Every departure removed engines, technicians, and fuel from the place sending help. Every arrival depended on the destination preserving stores for a ship it could not hurry.

Some settlements refused the exchange compact. One mining habitat kept its reactor mesh and declined Brine culture because its council expected the old routes to reopen. Another launched too early, lost a drive bell, and spent eleven weeks limping home. The public boards carried those failures beside successful departures. Hope could waste fuel as effectively as panic if nobody printed the margins.

Irian had learned the names of the dark habitats. Lattice Home, population six thousand, last message a request for valve ceramics. Sable Refuge Nine, population eleven hundred, transmitter active but life-support load unreadable. Narrow Glass, a factory spindle with enough power and no seed culture. She assigned courier mass by survival probability and hated the phrase each time. Probability did not breathe inside a failing room. It only told distant people where limited cargo might arrive in time.

“Give Dross Lantern the fungal stock,” Irian said. “Pell Orchard gets machine hours from Accord's aft works. Add a maintenance crew to Refuge Three's package and put the allocation before delegates.”

Marl studied her. “You could authorize emergency release.”

“I could. Then every settlement farther than my eyes becomes easier to spend.”

“You have learned to make us late.”

“I had excellent teachers.”

Marl took the slates to the delegate channel. Irian drank the false coffee and watched frost stream from the relief ship's bow.

A hearing pane opened beside the range plot.

The public inquiry met aboard three locations at once: a chamber on Nacre, a converted food hall at Brine, and a sealed evidence room on a neutral cutter. Its members included routekeepers, passenger delegates, Charter jurists without command authority, habitat engineers, and families of the dead. The inquiry could name facts and recommend charges. Halvek's trial would come later if enough courts survived to hold one.

Irian's image joined the Brine hall. Condensation marked the wall behind its commission table. Half the seats had once belonged to vat technicians during shift meals.

Chair Vael Rusk spoke without ceremony. “Captain Mott, we are examining the decision to maintain and intensify holds after the amplification model became available on Day Twenty. Do you affirm your submitted operations log?”

“I affirm the log and its corrections.”

“Did Marshal Halvek Enro provide the model to your convoy command?”

“No.”

“When did you learn it existed?”

“After the Sable lensquake.”

The chair placed two documents on the public record. One showed Halvek's suppressed analysis. The other carried Irian's requests for stable routes during the same period.

“Your requests argued that closure would threaten 410,000 evacuees and 70.4 million tonnes of essential freight,” Rusk said. “Do you withdraw that claim?”

“No. It remained true.”

A murmur crossed the Brine hall.

Irian kept her attention on Common Measure's docking marks. “Our dependence did not make the holds safe. It made disclosure more urgent. We needed the amplification model to choose among costs. Halvek chose for us by hiding one side.”

A routekeeper commissioner leaned toward his microphone. His union badge carried the old nine-station wheel with a black line cut through it. “Route crews also knew cages injured the structures before Day Twenty.”

“Kessa Rho's evidence established injury by Day Six,” Irian said.

“Did you stop using corridors then?”

“No.”

“Why not?”

“Because a hospital cylinder and several hundred thousand people occupied a divided route. Because I believed use could continue while release methods developed. Because I counted the immediate stranded ships more clearly than the animal carrying the road.”

The commissioner did not rescue her from the answer. “Would you make the same decision now?”

“No. Knowing what I know now changes the available choice. Claiming otherwise would turn hindsight into a performance.”

The inquiry entered her answer without comment.

Recovered Charter archives gave the inquiry three findings it could enter without repeating every witness. Responsive filament had appeared in reports for a century. Nacre supervisor Orra Dane had signed twenty-six records that recoded scars as self-sealing seams after an early biological finding cost her crew its contract. The commission placed her signatures beside photographs from the dead adult and kept her admission that she had trained apprentices to use the same codes.

Irian submitted her Day Eleven triage board with three civilian modules marked for possible jettison. Her refusal and her staff's preparation remained on the same page. Passenger delegates added the Brine food demand and the phrase nonessential mass as evidence that disposal language had traveled through shipping law before Halvek used it.

The command record carried narrower authority. Halvek had received the amplification model, suppressed it, ordered the remote hold at Brine and the seizure at Orison, then left Sable to apply a reinforcement protocol beyond certified limits. His famine history explained his choice. His signatures fixed responsibility for it.

Rusk changed the evidence feed.

Halvek appeared under guard in a small cabin aboard Mica Hand. He wore no uniform jacket. One wrist carried a movement cuff linked to the cabin hatch. The frame showed Captain Orra Senn seated outside his reach, representing neutral custody.

“Marshal Enro,” Rusk said, “do you affirm that the amplification model reached your bridge on Day Twenty?”

“I do.”

“Did you suppress external distribution?”

“Yes.”

“State your reason.”

Halvek looked older without Continuity around him. “The model predicted systemwide collapse under continued full holds. Immediate release also stranded famine relief, medical shipments, and inhabited modules. I believed staged holds could buy time for evacuation.”

“Did the analysts certify that belief?”

“No.”

“Did you inform affected systems of uncertainty?”

“No.”

“Did you order coercive action against ships refusing holds?”

“Yes.”

The chair let each answer settle into the public record.

A Sable family representative received the floor. She gave her name as Oren Tev and identified two dead route workers: her wife, Lio, and their eldest son, Per. Both had remained on the orbital ring under Charter orders during the doubled hold.

“You came to Sable after the ring broke,” Tev said. “Did you know their names then?”

Halvek did not look away. “I read them on the casualty list.”

“You used Continuity to save Brine.”

“Yes.”

“You evacuated its crew.”

“Yes.”

“My family did not receive that option.”

“No.”

The single word carried no request for absolution.

Tev placed a maintenance photograph into evidence. Two people stood beside a resonator port in work suits, one holding a graphite inspection stick. “Your final act saved lives. I am told I must say that if I want an accurate history.”

Rusk said, “You are not required to praise him.”

“I am not praising him. I am refusing to make accuracy another thing his office owns.” Tev faced Halvek's image. “You saved people at Brine. You helped kill mine at Sable. The same record can carry both.”

Halvek lowered his head once. “It must.”

Irian felt no relief. A clean villain would have offered it. Halvek remained the man who had fired through a habitat exclusion cone and the man who stayed aboard a failing cruiser until his crew escaped. Preserving both facts denied him a hero's erasure and denied everyone else the comfort of pretending only monsters had maintained the routes.

Before recess, the commission entered provisional findings of material suppression and unlawful beam use. It referred Halvek's command acts to civil trial, froze the Charter's surviving indemnity reserve for Sable and Orison claims, and ordered all recovered anchor designs copied to public fabrication archives. Three legal entries changed who controlled money, evidence, and repair work before any final verdict.

The reserve freeze interrupted two pending cutter-refit contracts before Rusk finished reading the order. Their metal allotments moved to Sable pressure trusses, with every transfer published against a claim number. Irian watched the first six entries arrive and recognized consequences no testimony could postpone.

The inquiry recessed when Brine control called final approach.

Irian closed the pane. Halvek would remain under arrest until trial. The commission would continue collecting claims from settlements whose couriers had not yet arrived. No verdict could travel faster than the people and evidence carrying it.

Common Measure filled the gallery window.

Its scale arrived gradually: first a bright drive point, then a scarred bow, then cargo spines extending behind the central tankage. Patches covered the forward radiator. One section bore bare metal where micrometeoroid shielding had been moved to protect the crew drum.

“Manifest confirmation,” Brine control said.

The relief ship transmitted its cargo in blocks. Air catalysts. Water membranes. Fungal cultures from Nacre. Sable-manufactured bearing ceramics loaded before departure. Medicines compounded aboard during cruise. Letters, legal records, school archives, repair designs, and three passengers who had spent seven months traveling to rejoin families.

No single cargo solved the Reach.

Every item closed one gap while another remained open.

The first relief convoy carried scarcity with it. One coolant pump had failed during the burn, forcing the crew to thaw reserve shielding ice. A crop vault overheated before they isolated the compartment. Twelve tonnes of protein culture arrived dead, and three medical compounds had lost certified temperature history. Irian ordered those containers marked unusable until Brine laboratories tested them. Need did not make contamination nutritious.

Delegates watched the manifest deductions in real time. Dross Lantern's requested allotment fell below its next growth cycle. Pell Orchard offered half its own stock without asking for extra machine hours. A Nacre engineer aboard Common Measure found a substitute medium among the ship's waste cultures. It yielded less protein but required no seed reserve.

“Publish the process before transfer,” Irian said.

The engineer blinked at her through the cargo feed. “It is not validated outside our reactor loop.”

“Publish that too.”

Under the Charter, a useful process would have entered a licensed route package and traveled with payment restrictions. Brine's council placed the substitute medium on the open fabrication board within the hour. Three habitats rejected it after reading the heat requirements. Two began test batches. Nothing became universal because someone in authority wanted a single answer.

The relief convoy also carried people Brine had nearly stopped expecting. A pressure technician from Nacre found her husband assigned to an algae cylinder. Two sisters from Sable arrived with separate copies of the same family archive in case one ship failed. Their reunions occupied no cargo column, yet passenger delegates had reserved berth mass for them through every ration debate.

The tugs caught Common Measure on four lines. Its rotation transferred into their frames. Two lines climbed, one slackened, and local pilots corrected by load rather than visual alignment. Irian watched people perform the same work that had carried the crossing, now without a veilback's wake distorting space. Ordinary docking had become slow enough to reveal its own difficulty.

“Outer cargo spine secure,” a controller said.

Brine's delegate council approved the first distribution. Refuge Three received catalyst and seals. Dross Lantern received fungal stock. Two distant cylinders received less than requested because Common Measure had consumed part of its reserve during an engine repair. The shortage entered the public ledger beside the reason.

Irian left the gallery for the receiving lock.

Marl met her there with a group of children from Brine's communal school. They had been chosen to carry welcome packets because adults understood how easily a ceremony could become a speech. Each packet contained local maps, ration rules, and names of the people assigned to help the arriving crew.

The inner hatch opened.

A woman in a grease-dark flight suit crossed first. Frost melted from her boots and ran across the deck in silver beads. She had shaved one side of her head around a healed burn. Behind her came two engine workers supporting a man whose muscles had weakened during the long burn.

“Captain Mott?” the woman asked.

“Locally.”

The pilot almost smiled. “Captain Sia Neral, Common Measure. Nacre sends forty-one tonnes less than promised, six weeks later than promised, and with more apologies than requested.”

“Brine accepts the cargo. We will inventory the apologies before distribution.”

Marl handed Sia a welcome packet. The first child offered another to the injured engineer and asked whether the frost came from outside the ship.

“From our own coolant,” he said. “Outside does not give you much for free.”

The child considered this. Her name strip read ALI QUADRE, AGE 9. She looked past the crew toward the scarred hull in the docking collar.

“My grandmother says Nacre used to take three hours,” Ali said.

Irian remembered corridors opening like certainty. Three hours had hidden resonator crews, scarred juveniles, hold commands, and the assumption that distance should submit because people had cargo to move.

“It did,” she said.

“How long does it take now?”

“For Common Measure, seven months.”

The child frowned at the number. “How long will the next journey take?”

Irian looked through the lock window at workers carrying catalyst cases one by one. Beyond them, the relief ship's frost loosened in clear fragments under Brine's lamps.

“If the engines hold, six months and twelve days,” she said. “If they do not, longer. We will know where it is because they will tell us, and we will keep supplies until it arrives.”

Ali nodded, not pleased and not deceived.

On the dock, someone chalked the next departure date beneath the arrival board.

Chapter 28 — The First Slow Ship

Kessa found the bad load forty minutes before departure.

It hid in the port shear vane where a new ceramic collar met an old tug bearing. The instrument called the mismatch acceptable. Her glove called it a cracked mount waiting for thrust. She braced one boot against the service rail of the small ship and put Tomis's torque key on the inner bolt.

The brass handle had lost more plating in three years. Its obsolete diagnostic cavity now held a wake-fiber calibrator no longer than Kessa's thumbnail. When she applied pressure, the key translated the vane's tiny gradient into three tactile pulses.

Two arrived clean. The third dragged.

“Port collar,” she said.

Sena's voice came from inside the hull. “We replaced it yesterday.”

“We replaced the wrong part yesterday.”

“That improves morale.”

Kessa loosened the mount. Beneath it, a graphite shim had split along the manufactured grain. Ordinary damage, not strange biology or hidden Charter code. She felt almost affectionate toward it.

The ship rested in Brine's outer construction dock, thirty-one metres of fusion drive, pressure cabin, instrument truss, and six folding shear vanes. No cage surrounded it. No resonator beam touched the dark. Far beyond the dock, one faint star bent around a wake fiber shed during the veilback molt three years earlier.

That curve marked their path.

Year 3 after the molt had begun with unmanned tests. Routekeepers pushed instrument cans into fibers and recovered half of them. The first vanished when a decaying edge rolled across its drive clock. The second came back with every exterior fastener loosened in the same direction. A third crossed four million kilometres, transmitted useful load maps, and burned during an ordinary engine fault after leaving the wake. The public report separated the wake result from the engine loss.

Kessa had flown the fourth can from Threadbare by remote grapple. It entered Coda-Seven, gained a fraction of effective velocity, and came out with less fuel than a conventional burn required. No signal passed along the fiber ahead of it. No filament closed around the probe. Anu found no stress variation resembling the migration response. Six more tests repeated those results before anyone proposed carrying people.

The first crewed proposal failed its own review. Its designers had added a low-power resonator to widen the thread around the hull. They called the device a stabilizer because nobody wished to print the word hold. Kessa placed the field diagram beside Nacre-9's old cage geometry. The project council rejected it, published the reason, and dismantled the coil under open cameras.

Construction resumed with vanes that read and rode gradients rather than imposing them. That choice enlarged the fusion drive, reduced useful cargo, and doubled the estimated crossing time. It also gave a weakening fiber permission to refuse them. If the wake faded, the ship would fall back into ordinary space and continue slowly under its own power.

The first fixed routes had carried ships between systems in hours. The vessel under Kessa's boots might reach Nacre in six weeks and four days if the fiber held its measured decay. If the thread weakened early, ordinary fusion burn would add eleven weeks. If the drive failed after midpoint, a rescue ship could take months to reach them.

Every passenger had signed those numbers without a promise attached.

Anu floated out through the service hatch with a specimen case strapped to her chest. At thirty, she had acquired more institutional titles than patience. Her suit still carried the plain green stripe of gravitational ecology.

“Please tell me you are not teaching the calibration key nostalgia,” she said.

“The key found a cracked shim.”

“The strain mesh found it yesterday.”

“The strain mesh approved it.”

“It marked one point eight percent outside nominal.”

“On a mount with one percent room.”

Anu pushed closer and examined the split. “Then the installation model approved it. The mesh behaved correctly.”

Kessa handed her the broken shim. “You can explain that distinction to the vacuum.”

Sena emerged behind Anu carrying a replacement collar. Her hair had gone silver along one temple since the crossing. She had rejected three offers to captain larger ships and accepted ownership in this one only after the cooperative removed a clause giving Brine exclusive control of departure data.

“Found this in the first crate,” Sena said. “It has an ugly edge.”

“Ugly fits.”

Together they replaced the shim, seated the collar, and checked the load again. Three crisp pulses traveled through the torque key. Kessa saved the reading to the public departure log.

Within seconds, six other docks copied it.

Every measurement from the slow ship went out openly: vane geometry, wake decay, failed materials, crew dose, fuel use, correction burns, and the equations Sena distrusted enough to test by eye. Nacre had built a sister craft from the same plans. Sable had changed the vane hinge and published the improvement. Two private carriers offered to fund faster prototypes if the cooperative delayed release of new data. The offers remained in the public archive beside the refusals.

No monopoly owned the fiber.

That rule had made construction slower. It had also produced twelve independent checks on the port collar.

The argument over ownership had nearly stopped the ship. Brine supplied dock mass and wanted priority passage for food cultures. Nacre supplied drive ceramics and wanted control over copied engine plans. Sable's rebuilding council wanted guaranteed berths for ring technicians. Former Charter offices offered navigation archives in exchange for a licensing role.
