Marl had convened the dispute in the same food hall used by the inquiry. Kessa attended long enough to explain that wake data changed while lawyers spoke. Sena displayed three independent vane designs. Anu read the biological prohibition into the record, then refused every amendment that weakened automatic publication.

The final cooperative gave builders passage priority only on the ships they physically supported. No system could restrict plans produced with shared measurements. Safety objections entered the public archive with names and evidence. A private builder could improve a design and charge for the hull, but the wake itself generated no property claim.

Routekeepers carried much of that rule into practice. Thousands had lost cage contracts when the fixed routes ended. Some became fusion mechanics. Others dismantled resonator stations for usable metal or staffed the long-baseline observatories that tracked fading fibers. A few refused the new work and petitioned to reopen empty cages, arguing that no juvenile remained to harm. Anu's surveys showed residual living filaments at four sites. Even where none appeared, the cooperative rejected rebuilding infrastructure designed to capture the next migration cycle.

Kessa had voted with that refusal. She also knew what it cost. Tomis learned his trade because route work paid enough to support a family. Removing harmful machinery did not create harmless wages. The slow-ship yards survived only because Brine, Nacre, and Sable treated navigation as shared infrastructure rather than a salvage market waiting for ownership.

Kessa entered the ship through the service hatch. Threadbare had given up its cold-shear cutter, one grapple drum, and much of its manual console to build the vessel. The old tug remained at Brine as a repair ship with a patched rail where the first filament had grown through it. Kessa had refused to turn the scar into a display. Vasken stored spare line there.

The new cabin carried eight people. Four were routekeepers. Two maintained the fusion drive. Anu owned biological limits but no authority over navigation. Sena flew. Kessa handled wake loads and exterior repair. Nobody held a title broad enough to decide alone.

Pell had stayed with Threadbare. He claimed slow travel required younger joints and then sent thirty-seven pages of instrument warnings.

Kessa strapped into the work seat behind Sena. The console used brass levers beside modern field displays because hands remained useful when local clocks disagreed. Tomis's key fit into a calibration slot at Kessa's right knee. Its handle protruded far enough to catch a careless sleeve.

Sena looked at it. “We could build a proper connector.”

“We did. It drifted.”

“We could build a proper connector that does not drift.”

“Put it on the list for Nacre.”

The departure board showed twenty-three unresolved objections. Most concerned acceptable wake decay. One came from a Sable insurer that wanted the voyage classified as experimental freight movement rather than passenger service. Marl Quade had answered by publishing the insurer's own loss assumptions with their arithmetic corrected.

The remaining objections belonged to people who had signed passage and retained the right to change their minds until the outer lock closed.

Kessa opened the crew circuit. “Final withdrawal check. Speak to Sena on private if you want off. No cause required.”

No one spoke publicly.

Sena waited the full five minutes. One engine worker requested private channel. Kessa watched his status change from green to amber while they talked. He had a daughter on Brine and a partner traveling on the second slow ship. Departure would separate them for at least a year if schedules failed.

His status returned green.

Sena said only, “Eight aboard.”

Consent did not become permanent because someone had counted it once. They would ask again before entering the fiber, and at every point where ordinary thrust still offered a safe turn.

Anu opened the wake display. “Fiber Coda-Seven has lost three percent coherence since yesterday.”

“Model allowed five,” Kessa said.

“Model also expected symmetrical decay. Port edge has thinned faster.”

Sena rotated the projected path. “Can we ride starboard?”

“We can enter starboard. The fiber may roll us inward after the second gradient peak.”

Kessa enlarged the raw observations. Most local wake fibers had faded within months. Denser strands propagated along the old molt corridor as slowly moving gradients, and Coda-Seven's navigable segment had reached Brine eleven weeks earlier. It contained no juvenile. Anu's probes found shed baryonic filaments and decaying topology, closer to a trail of skin and altered gradient than a living body. Navigation disturbed it slightly, but no response propagated back toward the departed animals.

The first specimen cut from Nacre-9 had remained in Anu's archive. Within weeks of the nine-minute dark, its folded strain collapsed into inert carbon and silicate dust. The archive kept that dust beside its complete load record and permitted no new culture attempt.

Coda-Seven's measured decay and lack of organized regeneration justified one monitored passage, not ownership.

Anu had made Kessa repeat the evidence standard at every review. Shed fiber lacked organized regeneration. Its gradient decayed without a pulse cycle. Samples did not contract around damage or alter course toward the adult's departed wake. Their instruments could still miss forms of response they did not know how to detect, so absence of evidence granted no permanent clearance.

Each slow ship carried withdrawal probes ahead of its vanes. A probe sent load changes backward through physical cable rather than interpretive software. If a filament closed, a recurring three-part rhythm appeared, or decay reversed near the hull, the cable cutter fired and the ship burned away. All three events remained unknown. Those stop conditions entered the flight charter before any departure approval.

“Coda-Seven probe still clean,” Anu said. “No organized response across sixty-two hours.”

Kessa checked the raw trace. Random tremors followed thermal changes in the cable. One broad load matched a distant cargo tug crossing behind the fiber. Nothing resembled the patient pressure she had felt through Nacre's grapple line.

“Publish the tug correlation,” she said.

“Already attached.”

Their caution could not prove innocence. It could only remain visible enough for someone else to stop the work when evidence changed.

Anu had written limits into the cooperative charter. No craft could strengthen a fiber with a hold beam. No navigator could conceal biological response. Any recurring pulse required immediate withdrawal and public review. Wake-shear travel would remain slow or cease.

“Use the outer third,” Kessa said. “Lower gain on Vane Six. If the roll exceeds two degrees local, we leave the thread.”

“Which local?” the engine worker asked.

“Yours at the drive, mine at the vane, Sena's at the hull center. Any one can call withdrawal.”

He nodded and copied the rule onto his manual slate.

A dock alarm announced visitors at the inner lock.

Irian entered with Marl and two Brine technicians carrying a case of water membranes. Three years had drawn more gray through Irian's hair and removed the convoy insignia from her jacket. She now chaired a rotating transport council when local delegates could not find someone else to blame. Accord of Dust had become a settlement spine, its great engines used only for orbital adjustments.

“You are eight hundred kilograms over filed departure mass,” Irian said.

Sena pointed at the membrane case. “You brought two hundred of it.”

“The other six?”

“Open instruments from Sable,” Anu said. “They arrived late.”

Irian looked at Kessa. “Your margin?”

“Enough if we cut ten days of ceremonial food.”

Marl opened a storage panel and removed a sealed Brine cake. “This object has threatened three committees. I volunteer it.”

Sena took the cake from him. “Denied. Ballast has navigation value.”

The exchange relieved no real risk, which made it useful. Crew shoulders lowered. Irian checked the manifest herself and found two duplicate tool kits. One returned to the dock. The membranes stayed.

Beyond the lock, departure workers began clearing the service gantry. Brine's cylinders turned beneath the red dwarf. New workshops occupied trusses once reserved for route cargo. Slow ships in several stages of construction dotted the outer docks. None shared exactly the same vane layout.

Kessa guided Irian to the instrument truss.

A sealed copy of the route inquiry occupied one archive block. Halvek's public trial record had arrived from Nacre by courier the previous year. He remained alive in custody, stripped of Charter office, with his testimony, convictions, and final evacuation orders preserved together. No annotation called the loss of Continuity repayment. Sable families had objected to that language, and the archive kept their objection beside the judgment.

Irian touched the archive status light. “You carry the whole record?”

“Every slow ship does.”

“Redundancy?”

“Distrust.”

“Better word.”

They moved forward to the cupola. The dock framed Brine in metal ribs. Without an anchor, its stars held single positions.

Irian inspected the scratched rim taken from Threadbare. “You kept the worst piece.”

“It already fit.”

“You could wait for the Nacre craft to run first.”

“Nacre could wait for us.”

“I know.” Irian rested one hand on the cupola frame. “I still had to ask.”

Kessa understood the count behind her face. Eight aboard this ship. Thousands depending on its measurements. Six weeks if the wake held. More if it did not. Irian had spent three years converting distance into stores and schedules. She had not learned to like any number merely because people consented to it.

“Brine has enough culture through the next arrival,” Kessa said. “The second ship can reach us inside three weeks after a failure call. We carry independent burn for the full crossing.”

“I have read your filing.”

“You corrected the food line.”

“It needed correction.”

They stood without finding a larger statement worth making. Kessa remembered meeting Irian outside Nacre during an emergency transfer, when six hours seemed like generosity and a closed route looked like the whole danger. Respect had grown between them through refusals, bad margins, and work neither could finish alone. It required no different name.

Irian held out her hand.

Kessa looked at it. “Inspection?”

“Goodbye.”

They clasped wrists because gloves made palms awkward.

“Send your first load table before you send impressions,” Irian said.

“Tell your council not to average it.”

“They will. I will make them print the raw values underneath.”

“Close enough.”

Irian released her and left with Marl. At the hatch, Marl reclaimed the Brine cake from Sena's ballast rack and carried it onto the dock under protest.

The lock sealed.

Departure clearance arrived from Brine control, Nacre observation, Sable tracking, and the passenger cooperative. Four approvals, none sufficient alone. Sena fired cold maneuvering jets and lifted the vessel from its cradle.

The dock fell away at walking speed.

Kessa felt each clamp release through the hull. Forward pair clean. Aft port delayed half a second. Instrument truss free. No hidden load followed them.

The ship had acquired a name during construction: Patient Distance. Kessa had voted against it because distance did not care how anyone behaved. She lost fourteen to nine. The name now crossed Brine control in Sena's voice.

“Patient Distance clear of dock. Beginning fusion departure.”

Brine answered with local traffic, radiator hazards, and no blessing.

The fusion drive lit. Acceleration pressed Kessa into the work seat. Habitat docks narrowed behind them. Accord of Dust curved along the settlement plane, too large to move casually and no longer pretending that immobility meant failure.

For nine hours, Patient Distance crossed ordinary space toward Coda-Seven. The crew checked vane loads, ate one meal, rejected an automatic clock correction, and asked consent again at the last safe turn.

Eight remained aboard.

The wake fiber appeared ahead as a single displaced star. Then a second star bent around the same invisible line. Unlike an anchor corridor, it offered no mouth and held no stable width. Its decay map changed while they approached.

Sena took manual control. “Entry in sixty.”

Kessa set Tomis's torque key into the calibration slot and wrapped her scarred hand around the handle.

“Port edge still thinning,” Anu said.

“Vane Six low gain,” the engine worker confirmed.

“Withdrawal authority remains individual,” Sena said. “Call it if your local load crosses limit.”

The numbers counted down.

At ten seconds, a faint gradient touched the key. No three-beat pulse followed. The fiber carried only the stretched tremor of the old molt, fading as it traveled.

Patient Distance entered at fusion speed.

Stars slid rather than doubled. The port vane loaded, eased, then loaded again. Sena corrected two degrees starboard. Brine's transmission stretched behind them, voices lowering as local time separated along the wake.

The first gradient peak arrived early. Vane Two climbed toward its limit while Vane Six reported almost no load. A fixed corridor would have hidden the imbalance behind cage correction. Here, it pushed the ship into a slow roll.

“My local crosses amber in eight seconds,” the drive worker said.

“Withdrawal remains open,” Sena replied.

“Hold entry. I can unload with drive trim.”

Kessa felt the torque key. The drag came from port, smooth and declining. “Two percent trim, no more.”

Fusion thrust shifted through the hull. Vane Two eased below amber. Six picked up the outer edge. The ship's roll stopped at one point seven degrees and reversed without any field pressing against the fiber.

Anu marked the event for review. Nobody renamed it routine. Their first useful correction entered the archive with the withdrawal call that almost accompanied it.

Kessa read the first shear measurement aloud. Anu published it before the next arrived. The log marked an acknowledgment window six weeks ahead, when Nacre could receive the values and answer.

No captive body held their course.

The ship moved into the fading thread, carrying eight people, open instruments, six weeks of distance, and enough fuel to leave if the wake asked more than they would take.
